- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Nuanced overall, with a mathematically impossible constant-overhead claim.

#### §13.7 cost metrics and dequantization caution

- **Metric collision:** “Time complexity counts gates” and “gate complexity distinguishes two-qubit from single-qubit”
  are not standard clean definitions. Circuit size counts total gates, depth/scheduled duration models time, query
  complexity counts oracle calls, and weighted/native/$T$ metrics count selected resources.
- **T-count overclaim:** $T$ count is often important in surface-code/Clifford+$T$ architectures, but not universally
  dominant; $T$ depth, factories, Clifford routing, qubit count, measurement latency, and spacetime volume matter.
- **Magic-state absoluteness:** Not every architecture implements every $T$ by consuming one distilled magic state.
- **Dequantization:** Excellent warning to match access models; cite Tang and specify which “2018-vintage” claims rather
  than imply an era-wide correction.
- **Tensor networks:** They are older broad simulation methods, not always labeled quantum-inspired in the same
  sample/query sense as Tang-style algorithms.
- **Promise:** The pledge to state access assumptions explicitly is valuable and should be audited chapter by chapter.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** One of the most useful research-literacy passages.

#### §13.8 Chapter 14 bridge

- **Major Simon defect:** Simon is not a single application of the displayed Boolean phase-kickback template. Its
  oracle has an $n$-qubit output, queries `$O(n)$` times across repetitions to collect enough linear equations, and
  standard execution uses coset-state interference rather than one phase pattern.
- **Query-count wording:** “Once or a small number” is acceptable only if “small” includes linear in $n$ for Simon;
  better give each exact/asymptotic query count.
- **Algorithm list:** Deutsch, DJ, and BV fit Hadamard/oracle/Hadamard closely; Simon needs its own generalized hidden-
  subgroup template.
- **Historical claim:** “Simon’s algorithm ... Shor saw and generalized” is a compressed inspiration story needing a
  citation and nuance; Shor’s order finding uses related period/HSP ideas, not a direct one-line generalization.
- **“Makes Chapter 15 read itself”:** Fun but overpromising.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Excellent excitement for the catalogue, technically inaccurate about Simon.

#### §13.8 sanity checks and footer

- **Checks 1–3:** Good conceptual/algebraic checks. For check 3, minus signs occur where
  `$101\cdot y=y_1\oplus y_3=1$` under the book’s bit convention.
- **Check 4 inherits taxonomy issue:** Query, circuit size, depth/time, weighted two-qubit count, and $T$ count need
  clean definitions before asking for dominant scenarios.
- **Check 5:** Deutsch–Jozsa/Bernstein–Vazirani are clean structured phase examples; asking singular “which one” is
  underdetermined unless “cleanest” is subjective.
- **Coverage gaps:** No check tests controlled-oracle assumptions, QFT divisibility, QPE power cost, Simon’s actual
  coset circuit, or amplification success angles.
- **Navigation:** Footer links are syntactically consistent.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Reader-facing checks match the chapter’s mindset purpose.

#### Chapter 13 overall verdict

- **Overall:** A lively and genuinely useful algorithmic-literacy chapter. Its best contributions are rejection of
  naive parallelism, oracle-cost discipline, access-model fairness, and separation of theoretical from practical
  advantage. Its main weakness is forcing distinct algorithms into one Boolean phase-sandwich story.
- **Strong-defect shortlist:** Simon is repeatedly misrepresented by a one-bit phase-kickback template and implied
  single/small fixed query count; arbitrary controlled-oracle access is treated as free; repeated squaring is said to
  implement arbitrary `$U^{2^k}$` cheaply; standard Grover is said to permit exact tuning by integer stopping; Shor QPE
  outputs are called multiples of `$N/r$`; a constant overhead is said able to absorb an asymptotic square-root gain;
  and time versus gate complexity is defined inconsistently.
- **Recurring slight concerns:** Interference is universalized as the sole speedup resource; query and uniform-circuit
  costs blur; chapter 14–16 mappings remain contradictory; current complexity beliefs/tool costs need citations;
  QFT statements omit power-of-two/divisibility/approximation assumptions; and “every/most/always” rhetoric repeatedly
  exceeds the intended heuristic.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Opinionated, memorable, and excellent at motivating scrutiny—even where its own templates
  need that scrutiny.
- **Second-pass status:** Complete for this manuscript file; the opening, all eight numbered sections, every query/QFT/
  amplification/complexity claim, sanity check, and footer were read and reviewed separately.

### `book/part-06-algorithms/14-foundational-algorithms.md` — second pass

#### Chapter 14 title, status, navigation, opening contract, and reading guide

- **Historical scope:** Deutsch, DJ, BV, and Simon are foundational query algorithms, but the claim that this exact set
  “established the field” is historical interpretation needing sources and acknowledgement of Feynman/Benioff/Shor.
- **Primitive overclaim:** QFT/phase estimation and amplitude amplification/estimation are major primitives, not tools
  that “almost every” later algorithm uses. Variational, simulation, walk, QSVT/block-encoding, and communication
  algorithms differ.
- **HSP claim:** Abelian HSP unifies several exponential-query/time candidates including order finding, but not all or
  necessarily “many” speedups without specifying model.
- **Reading-guide quote:** The quoted phrase around “phase kickback ... as engine” is syntactically cumbersome and
  universalizes one mechanism. Rewrite as ordinary prose.
- **Chapter map:** This file confirms QFT, QPE, amplitude amplification, and amplitude estimation all reside in Chapter
  14, exposing numerous earlier contradictory references that should be mechanically repaired.
- **Navigation:** Relative links are syntactically consistent.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** An exciting and well-focused catalogue opening.

#### §14.1 Deutsch problem and exact query comparison

- **Correctness:** Promise, XOR oracle, circuit, and one-query exact outcome are correct.
- **Cost-model qualifier:** The two-versus-one separation is deterministic exact query complexity under the supplied
  coherent oracle. It is not an end-to-end runtime advantage and relies on quantum access to `$U_f$`.
- **Classical randomness:** State whether randomized bounded-error or prior-average one-query strategies are excluded;
  “classically need two” otherwise sounds unconditional.
- **Oracle width:** Here Boolean input/output makes the two-qubit oracle clear.
- **Render risk:** Doubled bit-set braces recur.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Ideal minimal first algorithm.

#### §14.1 phase-kickback derivation and conclusion

- **Correctness:** `$|-\rangle$` is an X eigenstate with eigenvalue −1, producing `$(-1)^{f(x)}` and the exact
  constant/balanced result.
- **Normalization:** First display is normalized correctly; the follow-up phase-kickback equation explicitly divides by
  `$\sqrt2$`.
- **Global/relative phase:** For each fixed $x$ the factor is global, but across the input superposition it is relative;
  say this because it is the source of information.
- **“Almost every speedup” overclaim:** Many later algorithms do not use Boolean phase kickback or a Fourier-like final
  transform. Present it as the chapter’s first recurring family.
- **Proof completeness:** One line showing the first-qubit state before/after final H would make the iff outcome fully
  transparent.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Compact and satisfying.

#### §14.2 Deutsch–Jozsa promise, complexity, and circuit

- **Correctness:** Balanced promise, deterministic exact classical worst-case `$2^{n-1}+1`, one quantum query, and
  zero/all-zero amplitude distinction are correct.
- **Decision wording:** Constant functions yield `$|0^n\rangle$` up to a global sign; balanced functions yield a state
  orthogonal to it, so any nonzero measured string certifies balanced in the ideal circuit.
- **Randomized baseline:** A few uniform classical queries distinguish with one-sided error decreasing exponentially in
  sample count; quantify error and sampling without replacement rather than simply “few.”
- **Separation label:** “Exponential” refers exact deterministic query complexity versus exact quantum; under
  bounded-error randomized comparison it becomes constant-query versus one-query.
- **Asset review limitation:** `figures/deutsch-jozsa.svg` has good alt text and awaits its own file pass.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Strong scale-up from Deutsch.

#### §14.2 mechanism claim and executable Qiskit example

- **Template overclaim:** BV fits the phase sandwich; Simon does not use this one-bit phase-oracle circuit, Shor uses
  modular exponentiation and QFT coset/phase structure, and the QFT itself is a transform rather than another
  Hadamard-sandwich algorithm.
- **Parity oracle:** CNOT from every input computes `$x_0\oplus x_1\oplus x_2$`, a balanced function, and BV-like phase
  interference yields deterministic `111`; correct under Qiskit’s displayed classical-bit convention.
- **Single-shot ideality:** A single ideal statevector shot decides the promise problem, but real noisy hardware does
  not preserve the exact guarantee; the example uses an ideal `StatevectorSampler`.
- **API currency:** `qiskit.primitives.StatevectorSampler`, result indexing, and `.data.c` are version-sensitive and
  should be validated by the repository’s example tests/pinned dependency.
- **Code pedagogy:** `oracle(qc,n)` mutates the circuit; type hints/docstring and explicit Qiskit bit-order note would
  help.
- **Execution scope:** The code block duplicates an external example; automated synchronization should prevent drift.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Runnable code is valuable and the deterministic parity output is satisfying.

#### §14.3 Bernstein–Vazirani

- **Correctness:** Problem, deterministic exact classical $n$-query strategy, one-query quantum circuit, Fourier
  character state, and exact recovery are correct.
- **Classical-model nuance:** Classical randomized/quantum query comparisons and output requirement should be stated;
  recovering all $n$ bits exactly indeed needs $n$ one-bit classical oracle answers in the worst case.
- **Transform wording:** The phase state equals `$H^{\otimes n}|s\rangle$`, and self-inversion gives $s$; excellent.
- **Parallelism phrasing:** Saying the run “uses `$2^n$` amplitudes” describes its mathematical state, not separately
  available physical storage/evaluations. The subsequent structure warning helps.
- **Endianness:** Returned SDK string may reverse the book’s `$s_1\ldots s_n$` convention; the figure/example should
  explicitly map it.
- **Asset review limitation:** `figures/bernstein-vazirani.svg` has informative alt text and awaits its own file pass.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Possibly the cleanest section so far.

#### §14.4 Simon problem, complexity, and sampling

- **Correctness:** Promise with nonzero $s$, randomized classical birthday lower bound, `$O(n)$` quantum queries,
  orthogonality samples, and Gaussian elimination are standard.
- **Success probability:** Collecting `$n-1$` independent equations takes `$O(n+\log(1/\delta))$` samples for high
  probability; merely `$O(n)$` runs should state bounded/high success rather than certainty.
- **Classical cost:** Naive Gaussian elimination is `$O(n^3)$` bit operations, while faster linear algebra exists;
  labeling it as the standard bound is fine.
- **Output-register measurement:** The usual explanation measures/discards the output to obtain a coset state. Measuring
  only the input after its Hadamard yields the same marginal, but showing why the unmeasured output does not spoil the
  distribution would repair Chapter 13’s template confusion.
- **Uniformity:** Conditional/marginal y distribution is uniform over the `(n-1)`-dimensional orthogonal subspace;
  correct.
- **Render risk:** Doubled bit-set/subgroup braces recur.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Concise and finally gives Simon its correct multibit circuit.

#### §14.4 Simon-to-Shor/HSP claim

- **Historical claim:** Simon influenced Shor, but “direct ancestor” needs citation and nuance.
- **Group inconsistency:** This paragraph calls Shor HSP over `$\mathbb Z_N$` with a cyclic subgroup, while §14.9 more
  accurately models order finding over `$\mathbb Z$`/a large finite cyclic register with period subgroup
  `$r\mathbb Z$`. `$\mathbb Z_N$` also collides with the integer-to-factor, and the period need not define the claimed
  subgroup in that wording.
- **Same-template caveat:** Both are abelian Fourier sampling, but finite-vector-space Simon and approximate cyclic
  period finding have different output analysis/postprocessing.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Strong conceptual lineage when stated precisely.

#### §14.5 QFT definition and circuit

- **Correctness:** Negative-sign QFT over `$N=2^n$`, Hadamard at N=2, and ideal `$O(n^2)$` controlled-phase circuit are
  correct under consistent bit reversal/sign conventions.
- **Rotation definition:** Define controlled `$R_k$` indexing carefully; phase angles and whether `$R_1=Z$`/which gates
  start at k=2 vary by convention.
- **Bit reversal:** The circuit requires final swaps or a reversed-output convention. The prose omits this while the
  figure mentions one SWAP for three qubits.
- **Invalid complexity comparison:** Calling `$O(n^2)$` “exponentially better” than classical FFT is not an apples-to-
  apples runtime comparison: QFT consumes an amplitude-encoded quantum state and returns one, while FFT reads/writes
  all $N$ classical coefficients. The next paragraph acknowledges unreadability but does not undo the speed claim.
- **Classical FFT variable:** `$O(N\log N)=O(n2^n)$` is algebraically correct for N=2^n.
- **Asset review limitation:** `figures/qft-3qubit.svg` explicitly warns its sign convention and awaits a separate file
  review.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Elegant circuit story; remove the misleading “exponentially better” comparison.

#### §14.5 readout limitation and approximate QFT

- **Readout:** Correctly emphasizes amplitudes are not an output list and structure/concentration is needed.
- **Inverse:** Reversing/conjugating implements the inverse; correct.
- **Synthesis inconsistency:** Generic Solovay–Kitaev gives polylog exponent `$c$`, not automatically one logarithm.
  Clifford+$T$ specialized rotation synthesis can yield near-logarithmic per-angle cost. Name the compiler model.
- **Error allocation:** `$O(n^2\log(n/\epsilon))$` assumes per-gate approximation/error budgeting and a specialized
  synthesis rate; derive the norm budget rather than assert it.
- **AQFT threshold:** Dropping angles below roughly `$\epsilon/n$` and gate count `$O(n\log(n/\epsilon))$` depend on
  operator/distribution error and desired success. “Negligible fidelity cost” is not a quantitative guarantee.
- **“In practice/serious” claim:** Actual Shor resource estimates may use semiclassical QFT, windowing, phase-gradient
  states, lattice surgery, or algorithm-specific approximations. This 2026 implementation claim needs sources.
- **Coppersmith citation:** Add the primary reference.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Excellent practical caveat, too confident in one synthesis formula.

#### §14.6 phase-estimation circuit and sign convention

- **Correctness:** With the book’s negative-sign QFT, the positive-phase register is an inverse-QFT image, so applying
  the forward negative-sign QFT is consistent. The `$4/\pi^2$` nearest-integer success lower bound is standard.
- **Notation defect:** `$F_t$` should be `$F_{2^t}$`; earlier `$F_N$` indexes dimension, not qubit count.
- **Precision:** “t bits” and rounded estimate require explaining exact phases versus nearest integer, phase modulo one,
  and extra bits/repetition to raise confidence.
- **Power ordering:** State which control receives `$U^{2^k}$`; bit reversal/QFT output conventions matter.
- **Eigenstate promise:** Having `$|u\rangle$` is a major input assumption.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The sign-convention explanation is unusually careful.

#### §14.6 controlled-power cost and scaling

- **Major no-fast-forwarding defect:** For a general efficiently implemented $U$, repeated squaring does not produce
  `$U^{2^k}$` in `poly(k)` gates; it normally needs `$2^k$` uses. This is correctly said for black boxes but then
  incorrectly waived for broad examples.
- **Hamiltonian error:** Implementing `$e^{-iH2^kt}$` by Trotter/simulation generally costs at least roughly proportional
  to simulated time `$2^kt$` (subject to model), not `poly(k)` merely because $H is structured. No-fast-forwarding
  results make this crucial.
- **Modular arithmetic distinction:** Shor classically precomputes `$a^{2^k}\bmod N$` and builds a modular-multiplication
  circuit for that constant; this is not repeated squaring of the unitary circuit itself.
- **Parameterized rotations:** A native arbitrary-angle rotation can encode the multiplied angle in one logical
  parameter, but finite precision/fault-tolerant synthesis cost grows with angle specification/error.
- **Query scaling:** Summing powers through `$2^{t-1}$` gives `$Θ(2^t)=Θ(1/\epsilon)$` base-U evolution/query cost;
  correct standard Heisenberg scaling.
- **Sampling comparison:** Classical `$1/\epsilon^2$` applies i.i.d. Monte Carlo probability estimation, not all
  classical phase/frequency estimation under matched coherent-time resources.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The right caveat is present but contradicted by its examples.

#### §14.7 Grover amplitude amplification

- **Correctness:** Query complexities, two-dimensional rotation, angle definition, amplitude formula, and optimal
  iteration scaling are correct for known M and uniform search.
- **Zero/unknown M:** M=0 makes theta/solution undefined; unknown M requires a search strategy rather than the stated
  stopping time.
- **Oracle implementation:** `$I-2P_{\rm good}$` is a phase oracle; converting a Boolean XOR oracle costs ancilla/
  kickback assumptions.
- **Classical bound:** `$Θ(N/M)$` is randomized expected/worst bounded-error query scaling under uniform unstructured
  search.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Excellent geometric explanation.

#### §14.7 generalized amplification

- **Inverse notation:** For unitary preparation use `$A^\dagger$`, not generic `$A^{-1}$`, to match circuits.
- **Sign conventions:** The displayed `-$AS_0A^{-1}S_f$` is consistent with the preceding Grover operator under the
  chosen reflection signs; explain global minus/convention once.
- **Success overclaim:** Standard fixed-phase amplitude amplification boosts success near one with
  `$Θ(1/\sin\theta)$` calls when $\theta$ is known/bounded; it does not generically boost **to exactly 1**. Exact
  amplification uses tailored phases, and unknown amplitude changes the method.
- **Cost:** Count both A and `$A^\dagger$` calls plus good-state oracle/reflections.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Highly reusable general form.

#### §14.8 amplitude estimation

- **Correctness:** Grover eigenphases, phase-estimation approach, and quadratic additive-error query scaling are
  standard at headline level.
- **Phase ambiguity:** QPE estimates ±2θ/2π branches; postprocessing maps either to `$a=\sin^2\theta$`.
- **Error dependence:** Canonical bounds include terms depending on `$a(1-a)$`, constants, and confidence; state
  additive error and success probability.
- **Resource comparison:** Quantum AE assumes coherent access to A, `$A^\dagger$`, and reflections/Grover powers.
  Classical Monte Carlo uses samples. These stronger access/coherence requirements must accompany the speedup.
- **Applications:** Option pricing/partition functions require state loading, payoff encoding, precision, and fault-
  tolerant depth; caveat/citations are necessary.
- **Variant claims:** Iterative/maximum-likelihood methods have differing rigorous guarantees; “trading a log factor”
  and “standard route” are method/hardware/version dependent. Present-day feasibility claims need sources.
- **Near-term wording:** Deep Grover powers can remain infeasible even without QFT, so shallow relative to QPE does not
  mean practically shallow.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Strong conceptual bridge from search to numerical estimation.

#### §14.9 HSP definition and examples

- **Definition:** Standard HSP promise and goal are correct.
- **Deutsch–Jozsa error:** Arbitrary balanced Boolean functions are not generally constant on/distinct between cosets of
  a subgroup, so HSP does **not** simply generalize the full Deutsch–Jozsa promise.
- **BV:** `$f_s(x)=s\cdot x$` hides kernel `$H=s^\perp$`; finding H determines nonzero s up to the binary scalar (hence
  uniquely). Correct.
- **Simon:** Correct subgroup over `$\mathbb Z_2^n$`.
- **Shor:** Infinite cyclic period subgroup is conceptually right, but implemented finite-register Fourier sampling is
  approximate and not literally an HSP over a finite `$\mathbb Z_N$` as §14.4 claimed.
- **Dihedral/lattice precision:** Regev reductions connect dihedral coset/HSP variants to specified approximation
  factors of unique-SVP/gap-SVP. The current broad cryptography claim needs the exact direction, parameters, and
  primary citations.
- **Render risk:** Doubled subgroup braces recur.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Powerful unification after removing DJ.

#### §14.9 Fourier-sampling template and research status

- **Normalization:** The state `$\sum_x|x\rangle|f(x)\rangle$` needs `$1/\sqrt{|G|}$`.
- **Finite group:** The sum/query count assumes finite G; Shor’s infinite-group intuition uses a finite window.
- **Abelian efficiency qualification:** `$O(\log|G|)$` samples plus polynomial postprocessing assumes an efficiently
  represented finite abelian group, efficient QFT, and suitable group decomposition/access. State model assumptions.
- **Nonabelian HSP:** “Symmetric group HSP would imply graph isomorphism” is oversimplified; GI reduces to an HSP over a
  symmetric/wreath-product-related group with particular hidden subgroups, and generic efficient HSP solution would
  solve it.
- **Status claims:** Kuperberg subexponential algorithms and 2026 “central question/progress slow” statements need
  current primary/survey citations.
- **“Most known exponential” overclaim:** HHL/linear algebra under access assumptions, quantum walks, forrelation,
  topological/number-theoretic algorithms, and others complicate the claim. The paragraph names two oracle exceptions
  but still lacks a defensible census.
- **Simulation exclusion:** Explicitly excluding Hamiltonian simulation helps, but “factor through HSP” remains vague.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Excellent research horizon, citation-heavy by necessity.

#### §14.10 Chapter 15/16 bridge

- **Scope overload:** Chapter 15 is promised Shor, Grover, HHL, quantum walks, VQE, and QAOA—six major families that
  elsewhere received separate chapter/section references. Verify the actual file can support this depth and repair all
  earlier chapter mappings.
- **Primitive mapping:** Grover is already substantially developed in §14.7, so “complete form” should identify new
  material rather than repeat it.
- **HHL caveat:** HHL uses QPE/controlled rotations/amplitude amplification under sparse-oracle, condition-number, state-
  preparation, and output assumptions; the bridge should preview these.
- **Chapter 16:** Hamiltonian simulation, block encoding, qubitization, and QSVT form a coherent modern toolkit, though
  QSVT’s “unifies much” claim needs scope/citation.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Strong momentum into landmark/modern algorithms.

#### §14.10 sanity checks and footer

- **Check 1:** For `$f(x)=x_0$`, the ideal DJ result is the BV character corresponding to that bit—nonzero with
  probability one. Bit-order convention should state which measured string.
- **Check 2:** Correct character identity.
- **Check 3:** Under negative-sign QFT, amplitudes for QFT4`$|1\rangle$` are
  `$(1,-i,-1,i)/2$`; correct.
- **Check 4:** `$R_Z(\theta)|1\rangle=e^{i\theta/2}|1\rangle$`, so
  `$\varphi=\theta/(4\pi)$` modulo one; correct, but restrict/wrap $\theta$ and address phase modulo one.
- **Check 5:** For N=16/M=1, `$\theta=\arcsin(1/4)$`, $k=3$ standard iterations give success
  `$\sin^2(7\theta)\approx0.961$`, illustrating that ordinary Grover is not exactly successful—usefully contradicting
  the book’s prior exact-tuning claim.
- **Coverage gaps:** No check exercises Simon sampling/elimination, approximate QFT error, AE query assumptions, or HSP
  cosets.
- **Navigation:** Footer links are syntactically consistent.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Strong, calculationally meaningful checks.

#### Chapter 14 overall verdict

- **Overall:** A brisk and engaging algorithm catalogue with excellent equations for Deutsch/BV, QPE sign conventions,
  Grover geometry, and measurement limits. Its largest issue is repeatedly collapsing distinct Fourier/coset,
  phase-oracle, and amplitude-amplification algorithms into one template.
- **Strong-defect shortlist:** Simon is still partially forced into the Boolean phase-sandwich/HSP narrative;
  Deutsch–Jozsa is incorrectly listed as a general HSP instance; QFT is called exponentially faster than FFT despite
  incomparable input/output; arbitrary Hamiltonian evolution/powers are implied fast-forwardable via repeated
  squaring/Trotterization; standard amplitude amplification is said to boost exactly to one; HSP Fourier state lacks
  normalization; Shor’s group is described inconsistently; and exact/approximate QFT synthesis scalings are asserted
  without a coherent gate/error model.
- **Recurring slight concerns:** Sign/bit-reversal conventions need circuit-level verification; success/confidence and
  oracle-access assumptions are omitted; API/current research claims need citations; finite versus infinite group
  models blur; chapter 15 is heavily overloaded; and doubled LaTeX escapes persist.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The chapter is highly readable and supplies several of the book’s most satisfying algorithmic
  derivations.
- **Second-pass status:** Complete for this manuscript file; the opening, all ten numbered sections, each algorithm,
  query/complexity claim, code example, sanity check, and footer were read and reviewed separately. Three referenced
  SVGs remain queued for their own file passes.

### `book/part-06-algorithms/15-landmark-quantum-algorithms.md` — second pass

#### Chapter 15 title, status, navigation, opening contract, and reading guide

- **Scope:** The chapter spans fault-tolerant number theory, query algorithms, linear systems, walks, NISQ variational
  methods, and QML in only 165 lines. This is a survey/teaser, not a place that can responsibly derive all “landmark
  algorithms”; say so.
- **Tone:** Calling QML “most over-promised and under-delivered” may be defensible editorially but needs evidence and
  neutral criteria; it risks sounding polemical before the survey.
- **HHL claim:** “Most exponential speedup stories outside cryptography” is a census claim and ignores simulation,
  sampling, walks, optimization/oracle separations, and QML-related proposals.
- **2026 device advice:** Essential-for-experiments and outlook claims are time-sensitive; cite current platforms/results.
- **Cryptographic stakes:** Grover, Shor factoring/DLP are relevant, but §§15.1–15.4’s mandatory label should distinguish
  symmetric versus public-key effects.
- **Navigation:** Relative links are syntactically consistent.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Strong, candid opening.

#### §15.1 Grover setup and exact rotation formula

- **Correctness:** Marked fraction, Grover geometry, rounded iteration choice, exact success formula, guarantee
  `$1-M/N$`, and query scaling are correct for known `$0<M\le N$`.
- **Mean-reflection language:** “A little amplitude drains” is intuitive for equal unmarked/marked symmetry; for
  arbitrary initial amplitudes, the two-dimensional reflection picture is safer.
- **Rounding:** Define how ties are handled and note `$M/N$` large can make zero iterations optimal.
- **Oracle cost:** Query complexity treats one phase marking as unit cost; physical implementation can dominate.
- **Asset review limitation:** `figures/grover-iteration.svg` has descriptive alt text and awaits its own file pass.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Excellent quantitative correction to Chapter 13’s false exact-tuning slogan.

#### §15.1 unknown-M and minimum-finding extensions

- **Algorithm defect:** Unknown-M search does not simply run successively doubled deterministic iteration counts; the
  Boyer–Brassard–Høyer–Tapp method randomizes the number of Grover iterations within a growing range to avoid periodic
  overshoot. State the actual strategy.
- **Verification:** Unknown-M algorithms need a way to verify a measured candidate and handle M=0.
- **Minimum finding:** Dürr–Høyer expected `$O(\sqrt N)$` queries is correct under oracle comparison/access; include
  success probability/repetition.
- **Citation:** Both extensions need primary references.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Valuable extensions, one oversimplified into an incorrect procedure.

#### §15.1 practical interpretation and optimality

- **Complexity wording:** Grover gives a quadratic improvement in search-space size N, but for a Boolean witness space
  `$N=2^n$` it changes `$2^n$` brute force to `$2^{n/2}$`—still exponential in input bits. Saying “not an exponential
  [speedup]” without specifying parameter is ambiguous.
- **NP wording:** Grover can quadratically accelerate brute-force verification over NP witnesses; it does not make
  NP-complete problems polynomial or exploit their structure automatically.
- **Fault-tolerance:** Oracle reversibilization, uncomputation, T/Toffoli depth, routing, and memory can dominate;
  correct direction.
- **“Current consensus”:** Search/cryptanalysis/branch-and-bound usefulness is workload- and architecture-dependent and
  requires current citations, not an unattributed consensus.
- **Cheap-oracle contradiction:** Cryptanalytic or branch-and-bound predicates can be very expensive reversible
  circuits; they are not obvious examples of “cheap” oracles.
- **Optimality:** BBBV gives unstructured quantum query lower bounds; cite it and distinguish success model.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Appropriately skeptical, if parameter language is tightened.

#### §15.1 Qiskit implementation

- **Correctness:** Two iterations for N=8/M=1 yield success
  `$\sin^2(5\arcsin(1/\sqrt8))\approx0.945$`; “roughly 95%” is correct.
- **Oracle decomposition:** H–MCX–H on the last qubit implements a phase flip of `|111⟩` under the given controls.
- **Diffusion:** The sequence is a standard all-zero phase reflection up to global sign; annotate it or factor into a
  helper so readers can verify.
- **Output reproducibility:** A literal stochastic count dictionary should not be presented as stable output; use a
  seeded sampler if supported or show probabilities/“example run.”
- **API currency:** StatevectorSampler result layout and MCX behavior are version-sensitive; validate with pinned
  dependencies/repository tests.
- **Bit order:** `111` is reversal-invariant, hiding Qiskit/book endianness issues; a nonpalindromic marked state would
  teach the mapping better.
- **Code style:** Semicolon-packed statements reduce readability in a teaching book.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Concrete and numerically well chosen.

#### §15.2 factoring reduction

- **Correctness:** Random-base order finding, even-order/non-minus-one condition, and gcd extraction are standard.
- **Input cases:** First compute `$\gcd(a,N)$`; if nontrivial, factoring succeeds without order finding. Prime powers,
  even N, and composite assumptions affect the success proof.
- **Probability bound:** The “≥1/2” useful-base probability needs conditions (e.g. odd composite not a prime power) and
  a citation/proof; expected constant rounds then follows.
- **Miller attribution:** Cite the exact classical reduction and distinguish its relation to the later quantum
  algorithm.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Clear reduction.

#### §15.2 quantum order finding and complexity

- **Oracle definition:** Reversible modular multiplication must specify initialization/domain and action outside valid
  residues; `$|y\rangle\mapsto|a^xy\bmod N\rangle$` is unitary when multiplication is invertible and register handling
  is defined.
- **Measurement scaling defect:** The measured integer `$c$` does not itself lie “close to `$s/r$`”; the ratio
  `$c/2^{2n}$` does. This missing denominator is a substantive statement error.
- **Output width:** A 2n-bit phase register is a standard sufficient textbook choice, with optimizations possible.
- **QFT sign:** Either sign yields recoverable ±s/r with adjusted postprocessing; connect to convention.
- **Gate-count claim:** `$O(n^2\log n\log\log n)$` using Schönhage–Strassen reversible modular exponentiation is a
  nontrivial resource theorem, not a standard one-line fact. Reversible multiplication overhead, uniform gate set, and
  error correction must be specified/cited; common textbook circuits are `$O(n^3)$`.
- **Fast multiplication currency:** Modern multiplication algorithms and practical constant factors differ; the named
  classical algorithm does not automatically transfer at identical asymptotic cost reversibly.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Good architecture, overly compressed complexity claim.

#### §15.2 cryptographic implication and standards

- **Threat qualification:** Cryptographically relevant Shor requires fault-tolerant scale and enough runtime/fidelity;
  conditional wording is present.
- **Protocol scope:** RSA factoring and finite-field/elliptic-curve DLP systems are vulnerable; symmetric cryptography
  sees Grover-style rather than total break.
- **NIST omission:** In August 2024 NIST finalized three initial PQC standards, not only ML-KEM/FIPS 203 and
  ML-DSA/FIPS 204; SLH-DSA (FIPS 205, derived from SPHINCS+) was also finalized. The sentence is incomplete.
- **Currency:** Standards status is temporally unstable and must link official NIST publications as of the book’s
  snapshot.
- **Terminology:** ML-KEM/ML-DSA are standardized descendants of Kyber/Dilithium; correct.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Immediate real-world consequence.

#### §15.3 moving-target warning and resource metric

- **Warning:** Excellent and necessary; the cited 2025 arXiv estimate and May 2026 interpretation need direct primary
  links and independent assumption comparison.
- **Metric completeness:** Physical qubits and wall-clock hours are headline metrics, but physical error model, cycle
  time, connectivity, code/factory layout, logical error target, classical decoding, power/cooling, and success
  probability determine comparability.
- **“Best published”:** Requires a systematic scope (surface code? gate model? credible peer review?) and date.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Responsible framing.

#### §15.3 two RSA-2048 estimates

- **Comparability:** Verify both estimates use genuinely matched physical error, cycle time, architecture, connectivity,
  code model, failure budget, and hardware assumptions before claiming a >20× reduction “without weakening
  assumptions.”
- **Publication status:** Distinguish 2019 preprint/2021 journal and unreviewed 2025 arXiv status.
- **Attribution chain:** Approximate residue arithmetic, yoked codes, and distillation changes need exact citations and
  an explanation of which improvement contributes what.
- **Rounding:** “Under 1 million/under one week” hides central estimates/ranges; quote precise values and uncertainty
  from the paper.
- **Magic-state cost:** Likely dominant in these surface-code models but architecture-dependent.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Dramatic trend, citation-heavy.

#### §15.3 logical-to-physical overhead and threat framing

- **Patch formula:** “Roughly `$2d^2$` per logical” is a rotated planar patch data-qubit heuristic, excluding syndrome
  ancillas, spacing, lattice-surgery workspace, routing, factories, storage, and architecture. It cannot directly
  explain total estimate millions.
- **Distance:** “High twenties” must come from the cited logical-error budget/model; not universal at physical error
  `$10^{-3}$`.
- **Current-device comparison:** 2026 qubit counts/error rates mix modalities, physical/logical qubits, gate metrics,
  and vendors. Official current sources are required.
- **“Degree, not kind”:** This is a forecast/philosophical claim; scaling control, fabrication, decoding, cryogenics,
  correlated noise, and yield may introduce qualitative engineering barriers.
- **Harvest-now examples:** Stored encrypted traffic/long-lived confidential data fit harvest-now-decrypt-later. Root
  certificates and signing keys face future forgery/migration risks, not retrospective decryption of captured
  ciphertext in the same way; separate confidentiality from authentication.
- **TLS wording:** Ephemeral forward-secret TLS sessions may resist later recovery unless endpoints/keys/protocol
  choices expose vulnerable long-term material; do not list all TLS sessions generically.
- **Prediction:** “When, not whether” assumes scalable fault tolerance is achievable; standards migration is prudent
  risk management without asserting a CRQC arrival.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Urgent and readable, too certain.

#### §15.3 non-Shor factoring proposals

- **Evidence:** Toy variational factoring lacks demonstrated asymptotic/practical advantage; fair caution.
- **Consensus claim:** The 2025 consensus on Schnorr/Yan proposals needs review articles/technical rebuttals, not
  assertion.
- **Proposal details:** Distinguish Schnorr’s classical claim from Yan et al.’s hybrid use, target bit sizes, oracle/
  preprocessing costs, and what “few hundred qubits” represented.
- **Terminology:** QAOA-style factoring can encode huge classical preprocessing/lattice work; qubit count alone is
  misleading.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Useful hype correction.

#### §15.4 discrete-log algorithm

- **Reduction precision:** The two-register Fourier-sampling relation depends on sign/QFT conventions; variables
  `(a,b)` are used both for prepared exponents and measured frequencies, inviting confusion.
- **Modular inversion:** A sample with invertible coefficient can recover x under the stated congruence; noninvertible
  samples require combining/repeating.
- **Group assumptions:** Cyclic subgroup order r, efficient group operations, representation, and QFT modulus/precision
  must be specified; not every arbitrary group DLP has identical implementation.
- **Ekerå reference:** Refined lattice postprocessing needs citation/details.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Clear headline.

#### §15.4 cryptographic systems and resource comparison

- **Scope:** DH, ElGamal, DSA, ECDSA, Schnorr, and pairing-group DLP constructions are vulnerable to sufficiently large
  Shor-type algorithms; correct broadly.
- **Pairing nuance:** Some pairing systems’ security also depends on related finite-field DLP after pairing reductions;
  parameter/security impacts vary.
- **Resource ratio overclaim:** “Roughly half the qubits and gate count for comparable security” depends on comparing
  specific RSA/ECC sizes, circuit designs, physical/logical metrics, and architectures. The cited studies should supply
  exact ratios; older estimates may not match 2026 factoring advances.
- **Arithmetic wording:** Elliptic-curve point multiplication still uses modular field arithmetic; “no modular
  exponentiation” does not make implementation simply cheaper without detailed reversible costs.
- **False migration universal:** Not **any** asymmetric primitive must be replaced—hash-based and some code/lattice-
  based schemes are already post-quantum. Replace vulnerable factoring/DLP-based primitives.
- **Standards:** “Before computers come online” needs crypto-agility/deployment lead-time and harvest-now threat
  distinctions.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** High relevance, overly broad conclusion.

#### §15.5 HHL complexity comparison

- **Headline:** Original-HHL scaling is broadly recognizable, but exact dependencies include Hamiltonian-simulation
  oracle model, norm/time scaling, success amplification, state preparation, and output error metric. State the theorem
  rather than one detached big-O.
- **Unfair classical baselines:** Dense Gaussian elimination `$O(N^3)$` and sparse `$O(Ns\kappa)$` are not a matched
  best-classical comparison. Iterative solvers have precision, spectrum, preconditioning, and often
  `$\sqrt\kappa\log(1/\epsilon)$`-type iteration dependencies; input/output access differs radically.
- **Exponential claim:** HHL is polylogarithmic in N only under sparse/efficient oracle, state-preparation, conditioning,
  precision, and observable-output promises. Saying “the speedup is in N” before these conditions can mislead.
- **Successor scaling:** Near-linear $\kappa$ QLSAs have log/precision/query factors and lower-bound qualifications.
  “Essentially linear” needs the exact Childs–Kothari–Somma/QSVT bound and oracle model.
- **Dates/citations:** HHL was published 2009 after a 2008 preprint; specify which year convention and cite primary
  papers.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Appropriately caveat-oriented, though the opening comparison is still hype-prone.

#### §15.5 spectral procedure

- **Correctness:** Eigenbasis expansion, QPE, reciprocal-conditioned rotation, postselection, and uncomputation yield a
  normalized state proportional to `$A^{-1}|b\rangle$`.
- **Eigenvalue encoding:** QPE produces approximations with sign/range/scaling; Hermitian A may have negative
  eigenvalues, requiring signed reciprocal rotation and a known spectral gap away from zero.
- **Choice of C:** Must satisfy `$|C/\lambda_j|\le1$` over supported eigenvalues; success probability is tied to
  $\kappa$ and b’s spectral weight.
- **Postselection cost:** Amplitude amplification is needed to obtain the advertised runtime; bare postselection can be
  rare.
- **Hamiltonian access:** Efficient `$e^{iAt}$` simulation is a major oracle/sparsity assumption, not a free use of
  spectral decomposition.
- **Precision:** Finite QPE and reciprocal arithmetic errors determine output-state norm/trace error.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** A clear and valuable procedural summary.

#### §15.5 three caveats and verdict

- **Readout scaling:** Reconstructing all N amplitudes to useful precision can require at least linear and often much
  larger sample/time cost; “Theta(N) measurements” is too exact without norm/error/output assumptions.
- **Observable extraction:** Estimating `$\langle x|M|x\rangle$` takes `$O(1/\epsilon^2)$` ordinary samples (or stronger
  coherent methods), and M must be efficiently implementable/measurable; it is not automatically polylog measurements.
- **State loading:** Generic amplitude loading is linear-scale, while QRAM query depth may be logarithmic only after
  building/maintaining an O(N)-scale data structure with strong coherence assumptions.
- **Conditioning:** Large κ can erase exponential advantage, but `$\kappa=\Omega(N)$` is not the only threshold and may
  still permit some polynomial comparisons; preprocessing/preconditioning and effective spectral support matter.
- **“Unavoidable”:** Lower bounds constrain κ dependence in specified black-box models; cite rather than assert.
- **Dequantization overclaim:** Tang and follow-ups dequantized important recommendation/linear-algebra algorithms under
  sample-and-query access, not “most explicitly proposed HHL applications” as a demonstrated census. Finance and all
  QML are too broad.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The caveat list is the right pedagogical priority.

#### §15.6 quantum-walk definitions and examples

- **Definition:** Discrete coined and continuous adjacency/Laplacian walks are standard; “unitary analogue” is useful
  but quantum walks have several inequivalent quantizations.
- **Glued trees:** Exponential oracle separation is correctly qualified; cite exact graph-access model/runtime/error.
- **Element distinctness:** `$Θ(n^{2/3})$` quantum query complexity and linear classical lower bound are standard; the
  text gives only upper quantum/lower classical.
- **Triangle finding:** The listed historical/latest exponents are time-sensitive, model-specific, and need current
  primary citations; clarify n vertices versus input size and query versus time complexity.
- **Attribution:** Full citations for Childs et al., Ambainis, Belovs/Le Gall et al. are required.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Strong breadth in little space.

#### §15.6 Szegedy framework and downstream claims

- **Spectral-gap speedup:** Quantized Markov-chain search/hitting can achieve quadratic dependence improvements under
  reversibility/ergodicity/setup/checking assumptions; not every chain/task inherits it.
- **Mixing caveat:** Good explicit acknowledgment that generic quantum mixing speedup is not established.
- **Monte Carlo:** Quantum walk/amplitude estimation frameworks depend on coherent stationary-state preparation and
  reflections; chapter reference should preview access costs.
- **Qubitization:** It uses a walk-like product of reflections and is related to quantum walks, but calling walks “the
  backbone” of all modern Hamiltonian simulation/QSP is interpretive.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Helpful unification.

#### §15.7 VQA recipe and design choices

- **Dominance claim:** VQAs have been a dominant NISQ research target, but “the dominant class” in 2026 is time-sensitive
  and needs publication/workload evidence.
- **Cost scope:** Not every VQA minimizes a Hamiltonian expectation; supervised losses, fidelities, likelihoods, and
  constraints vary.
- **Ansatz generalization:** Hardware-efficient circuits are not always shallow after expressibility needs, and
  problem-inspired ansätze are not uniformly deeper/harder.
- **Optimizer taxonomy:** Quantum natural gradient and imaginary-time methods are update geometries/evolution
  algorithms, not simply a third optimizer class parallel to COBYLA.
- **Parameter-shift:** Conditions/reused-parameter shot costs from Chapters 8/11 should be retained.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Clear recipe.

#### §15.7 barren plateaus and advantage status

- **Scope:** Exponentially vanishing gradient variance occurs under concentration/design, global cost, depth,
  initialization, noise, and ansatz assumptions—not all “many circuit families” generically.
- **Mitigation evidence:** Problem-inspired/local costs, shallow/layerwise initialization can avoid/delay plateaus under
  conditions; “initialisation tricks” is too vague and does not guarantee trainability.
- **Other obstacles:** Shot noise, optimizer scaling, noise-induced plateaus, expressibility, local minima, and classical
  simulation/baselines also matter.
- **NISQ-era status:** “Central open question” is fair editorially but should be sourced/current.
- **Citation:** McClean et al. formal reference needed.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Honest and relevant.

#### §15.8 VQE Hamiltonian and ansätze

- **Correctness:** Fermion-to-qubit mappings, Pauli-sum cost, and measurement decomposition are standard.
- **Mapping completeness:** Tapering symmetries, active-space selection, orbital/basis choices, and Hamiltonian
  construction dominate practical resources.
- **Static-correlation defect:** UCCSD based on a single reference is often poor for strong/static correlation; claiming
  it “captures static correlation accurately” is false as a general property.
- **Depth:** Trotterized UCCSD depth/gate count depends on mapping/order/connectivity; “deep” is directionally right.
- **ADAPT claim:** It can reduce parameter/gate counts for some systems but incurs many gradient measurements and does
  not uniformly achieve comparable accuracy with shorter circuits.
- **Hardware-efficient:** Minimizing depth alone can violate particle number/spin/symmetry and worsen trainability.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Useful chemistry map, one important methodological overclaim.

#### §15.8 measurement/noise costs

- **Shot formula defect:** For `$H=\sum c_kP_k$`, total precision cost is not generically
  `$O(K\,\mathrm{Var}/\epsilon^2)$`; it depends on coefficient-weighted variances, grouping covariances, allocation,
  confidence, and optimization iterations. Chapter 11 already exposed this.
- **Numerical estimate:** `$10^9$–$10^{12}$` shots for “50-orbital active space” needs a specific molecule, mapping,
  ansatz, grouping, precision, optimizer, and source. Orbital versus spin-orbital/qubit count must be clear.
- **Runtime contradiction:** At 1 kHz, `$10^{12}$` shots is decades; at 1 MHz it is about 12 days before overhead.
  Calling the entire range “several days” without an assumed shot rate is false.
- **Shadows:** Classical shadows do not universally reduce chemistry Hamiltonian measurement versus optimized
  commuting grouping/low-rank factorization.
- **Mitigation:** ZNE/PEC can reduce bias only under assumptions; PEC sampling overhead can grow exponentially and ZNE
  extrapolation adds bias/variance.
- **Snapshot:** May 2026 claims require current primary benchmarks.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Quantifies the bottleneck, but numbers lack enough denominator to be meaningful.

#### §15.9 QAOA definition

- **Correctness:** Standard depth-p alternating cost/mixer ansatz and sampling workflow are correct.
- **Sign/objective:** Maximizing `$\langle H_C\rangle$` assumes H_C encodes larger objective as larger eigenvalue.
  Later “converges to exact ground state” is inconsistent unless the cost sign is reversed.
- **Constraints:** Standard X mixer does not preserve constrained feasible subspaces; problem-specific mixers may be
  required.
- **Ordering:** Right-to-left layer order is correct under the displayed convention.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Compact and clear.

#### §15.9 approximation and adiabatic claims

- **Ratio condition:** The `$0.6924…$` p=1 Max-Cut guarantee is associated with triangle-free 3-regular graphs/particular
  asymptotic analysis, not all 3-regular graphs without qualification. Cite exact theorem.
- **Baseline:** Random cut ratio 1/2; correct comparison, but Goemans–Williamson’s ~0.878 guarantee is the meaningful
  classical approximation baseline.
- **Adiabatic-limit defect:** A discretized slowly varying schedule can approach adiabatic evolution as p/total time
  grows under a nonclosing gap and suitable interpolation. It converges to the target **maximum** state only with sign
  conventions matching maximization; “ground state” conflicts with preceding H_C definition.
- **Optimization:** Existence of an adiabatic schedule does not imply a classical optimizer efficiently finds those
  parameters.
- **Finite p:** Correctly denies a generic guarantee.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Good ideal-versus-learnability distinction.

#### §15.9 2020–2025 status assessment

- **Evidence scope:** “Most natural instance distributions” is an enormous empirical/theoretical census claim requiring
  a review/meta-analysis and precise metrics.
- **Classical comparison:** Goemans–Williamson applies Max-Cut; other CSPs have different best algorithms/guarantees.
- **Large-p trainability:** Optimization can be hard, but depth/noise/classical simulation and parameter transfer also
  affect evidence.
- **Positive examples:** Low-autocorrelation/dense-instance claims need exact papers and whether advantage is proven,
  query-based, or empirical.
- **“Has not materialised”:** Reasonable snapshot opinion only with dated sources/criteria for advantage.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Admirably sober.

#### §15.10 QML taxonomy

- **Taxonomy:** Classical-data QML, learning quantum data, and parameterized quantum models are useful categories but
  overlap; also include quantum generative/sampling and quantum-enhanced experimental design if relevant.
- **Terminology:** “Quantum neural network” has no single standard architecture; quotation marks appropriately signal
  caution.
- **Snapshot duplication:** Two back-to-back May-2026 warnings are redundant.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Clear framing.

#### §15.10 QML status bullets

- **Major universal overclaim:** HHL-style regression/kernel/recommendation/PCA speedups are not “essentially all
  dequantised.” Important algorithms were dequantized under sample-and-query assumptions, while other separations/
  access models remain. Cite a scoped list.
- **QRAM causality:** Some exponential claims rely on strong state-preparation/QRAM assumptions, but saying all were
  artifacts of QRAM ignores condition number, output, rank, precision, and complexity assumptions.
- **Quantum kernels:** Proven separations typically use constructed data/concepts/oracles; “generic-data empirically
  modest” needs benchmark surveys and baseline/fair-training caveats.
- **QNN production claim:** Absence of demonstrated advantage is fair snapshot language, not proof of limited utility.
- **Learning quantum data:** Classical shadows and quantum experiments can beat naive full tomography for property
  estimation, but this is not automatically a computational quantum advantage over the best classical processing of
  measurement data. Specify access/task/lower bound.
- **Hamiltonian learning:** May use classical algorithms controlling quantum experiments; label what part is quantum.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Valuable skepticism, excessively sweeping.

#### §15.10 conclusion

- **“Most claims evaporate”:** Another unsupported census. Replace with “many prominent claims weaken under matched
  input models.”
- **NISQ experiments:** Feasibility versus advantage distinction is excellent; cite representative benchmarking
  failures/successes.
- **Quantum problems:** Natural access to quantum states can create sample/communication advantages, but error
  correction, measurement, and classical shadow processing still determine end-to-end value.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Strong honest takeaway when quantified.

#### §15.11 bridge

- **Chronology:** QSP/QSVT/block encoding/qubitization foundational papers predate or straddle 2020; “algorithms
  developed in 2020–2025” is historically inaccurate as a blanket label.
- **“Supersede HHL”:** Modern QLSAs improve/reshape HHL under oracle models; not every listed Hamiltonian-simulation
  technique supersedes HHL or early simulation for practical regimes.
- **Top-level distinction:** Good warning that subroutine improvements change resource estimates.
- **Chapter scope:** Verify Chapter 16 contains all promised tools with sufficient depth.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Strong forward hook.

#### §15.11 sanity checks and footer

- **Check 1:** `$M/N=1/64$`, `$\theta=\arcsin(1/8)$`; rounded optimum `$k=6$` gives success
  `$\sin^2(13\theta)\approx0.997$`.
- **Check 2:** With N=15, n=4 and 2n=8 phase qubits, ideal r=4 peaks at c=0,64,128,192; good, though actual order-
  finding distribution and valid s/coprimality should be discussed.
- **Check 3 underspecified:** A generic 2×2 Hermitian A and b do not give one numerical rotation; ask readers to choose
  a concrete matrix, bound C, include negative eigenvalues, and compute success probability.
- **Check 4:** Analytic two-vertex QAOA is useful but depends on H_C sign/constant and mixer conventions not supplied.
- **Check 5 violates self-containment:** “Look up current” requires internet access, date/source criteria, and may
  conflict with the already claimed May-2026 snapshot. It is valuable research practice but unlike a stable sanity
  check.
- **Coverage gaps:** No DLP congruence, quantum-walk step, VQE shot allocation, barren plateau, or QML access-model
  exercise.
- **Navigation:** Footer links are syntactically consistent.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Broad, with underdetermined checks.

#### Chapter 15 overall verdict

- **Overall:** A candid, highly topical survey that excels at naming assumptions and real resource bottlenecks. Grover’s
  exact rotation and HHL’s procedure/caveats are especially strong. The short format, however, turns many research-
  status judgments into unsupported consensus declarations.
- **Strong-defect shortlist:** Shor measurement omits division by `$2^{2n}$`; NIST’s third 2024 standard FIPS 205 is
  omitted; unknown-M Grover is described as deterministic doubling rather than randomized iteration ranges; HHL uses
  mismatched/outdated classical baselines; UCCSD is falsely said to handle static correlation reliably; VQE shot
  scaling/runtime is dimensionally unsupported; QAOA’s maximization sign conflicts with its ground-state claim; its
  0.6924 ratio lacks the triangle-free condition; essentially all HHL/QML speedups are declared dequantized; and all
  asymmetric crypto is said to require replacement.
- **Recurring slight concerns:** May-2026 hardware/research claims need primary-source verification; gate/resource
  estimates mix logical and physical models; big-O formulas omit precision/confidence/access costs; named algorithms
  need citations; “consensus/most/genuine” language overreaches; and chapter references continue drifting.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Fast-moving, skeptical, and consistently engaging.
- **Second-pass status:** Complete for this manuscript file; the opening, all eleven numbered sections, each algorithm,
  code/resource estimate/status claim, sanity check, and footer were read and reviewed separately. The Grover SVG
  remains queued for its own file pass.

### `book/part-06-algorithms/16-modern-algorithmic-frontier.md` — second pass

#### Chapter title, status, navigation, and opening thesis

- **Title/scope:** “Modern Algorithmic Frontier” is inviting but extremely broad; the actual chapter is primarily the
  Hamiltonian-simulation/LCU/block-encoding/QSP/QSVT lineage plus selected applications. A subtitle could prevent
  readers from expecting communication, optimization, cryptanalysis, or other algorithmic frontiers.
- **Workflow metadata:** “prereviewed,” “Phase: 3,” and “9 / 9” are production notes rather than reader-facing prose.
  If retained in the published book, define the phase vocabulary; otherwise strip it during the release build.
- **Navigation:** The previous, contents, and next links are coherent.
- **Historical overclaim:** The assertion that post-2015 reformulations have replaced HHL’s presentation “in every
  modern reference” is almost certainly false literally. Many current courses, surveys, and expositions still teach
  the original HHL circuit. “Increasingly supplement or reframe” would be defensible.
- **Unification needs qualification:** QSVT can express or illuminate instances of Hamiltonian simulation, amplitude
  amplification, phase-processing primitives, and linear-system transformations, but saying that phase estimation and
  several Monte Carlo speedups all “fall out” as special cases hides substantial choices of signal oracle, block
  encoding, polynomial approximation, and readout. State whether “special case” means an exact circuit identity, an
  alternative implementation, or a shared mathematical framework.
- **Citation need:** Gilyén–Su–Low–Wiebe (2019) is named appropriately, but a bibliography key or direct reference is
  needed. The opening also needs sources for its historical claim rather than presenting a disputed historiography as
  settled.
- **Reader orientation:** The dependency-order map is excellent. It gives readers a reason for the sequence rather
  than merely listing section contents.
- **Premature verdict:** “speedups that genuinely survive scrutiny” promises an unusually strong adjudication of
  quantum Monte Carlo, nonlinear ODE solvers, and dequantisation. Each result survives only under specified input,
  conditioning, precision, output, and comparison models; §16.8 must earn this wording or the phrase should soften.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The bold unifying thesis creates momentum, although its absolutes invite distrust.

#### §16.1 — problem definition and complexity claim

- **Norm unspecified:** The condition `$\\|V-e^{-iHt}\\|\\leq\epsilon$` should identify the operator/spectral norm.
  Diamond norm would be natural for channels and gives a related but not identical statement.
- **Input model missing:** “Given a Hermitian operator” is not algorithmically meaningful until the representation is
  specified. An arbitrary `$2^n\times2^n$` matrix has exponentially many entries. Preview the local-term, sparse-oracle,
  and block-encoding access models in the definition itself.
- **Unitary-synthesis nuance:** The task constructs a circuit, so its complexity classification is not automatically a
  decision-problem statement. BQP-completeness normally applies to appropriately formulated prediction, matrix-element,
  sampling, or local-observable versions of Hamiltonian dynamics, under promises and a succinct Hamiltonian input.
- **Collapse claim too compressed:** A classical algorithm that prints the full unitary is not the comparator; that
  output is exponentially large. The BQP-to-BPP conclusion requires an efficient classical algorithm reproducing the
  relevant decision probabilities or samples under the same succinct access model and inverse-polynomial accuracy.
- **“Few robust speedups”:** This is a defensible perspective, not an established count. Define “robust” and cite the
  simulation hardness/completeness theorem.
- **Pedagogy:** Connecting the abstract norm approximation to Feynman’s motivation works well, but one sentence
  distinguishing “simulate the unitary” from “learn a useful property of the evolved state” would prepare the later
  output caveat.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** It rapidly connects physics, circuits, and complexity, though the caveats currently trail
  the excitement.

#### §16.1 — structural classes

- **Local definition:** The definition of `$k$`-locality is clear. It should say that `$k=O(1)$`, term descriptions are
  efficient, and the algorithmic normalization may depend on `$\sum_j\|H_j\|$`, not merely on `$\|H\|$`.
- **Overgeneralization:** “Almost all physical Hamiltonians” is too sweeping; effective gauge theories, long-range
  interactions, bosonic modes before truncation, and continuum models complicate the claim. “Many standard lattice
  models after encoding” is safer.
- **Strong technical defect:** “typically `$k=4$` for fermionic systems after Jordan–Wigner” conflates quartic
  fermionic interaction degree with qubit locality. Jordan–Wigner parity strings can make a mapped hopping or
  interaction term act on a number of qubits growing with orbital separation. The text should distinguish
  few-fermion-body structure from Pauli-string support.
- **Normalization assumption:** `$\|H_j\|=O(1)$` is a chosen scaling assumption, not part of locality. Physical
  coefficients and units may vary, and rescaling shifts cost into simulation time.
- **Sparse model:** The row-oracle definition is useful but normally needs separate location and value oracles,
  reversibility/coherence, precision bounds, and a norm bound. Mentioning these would stop readers from interpreting
  classical random access as automatically coherent quantum access.
- **Local-to-sparse bound:** Each `$k$`-qubit term can contribute at most `$2^k$` entries per row, so
  `$s\leq 2^kL$` is a safe loose upper bound. Calling the local case simply “a special case” also assumes an efficient
  procedure to enumerate and combine duplicates/cancellations.
- **Block encoding:** The forward pointer is well placed. Efficient block encodings do not follow from locality or
  sparsity alone without efficient term/oracle access, coefficient preparation, and an explicit normalization
  `$\alpha$`; preview those costs.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The taxonomy is crisp and useful, but the Jordan–Wigner error will jar knowledgeable readers.

#### §16.1 — historical progression and claimed optimal scaling

- **History/citations:** The named lineage is valuable and broadly appropriate, but every year/theorem should resolve
  to the bibliography. “Building on” may undersell parallel precursor work; avoid making the paragraph the sole
  historiography.
- **Short-time optimality:** “gate-count-optimal for very short times” needs a theorem, regime, gate set, Hamiltonian
  class, and error model. Product formulas can be practically favorable without being universally optimal.
- **Access normalization omitted:** Modern query complexities are generally expressed in terms of a block-encoding
  normalization `$\alpha t$` (or sparse/local oracle parameters), not solely `$t\|H\|$`. An inefficient encoding can
  have `$\alpha\gg\|H\|$`; the displayed formula therefore cannot be advertised as the generic algorithmic cost.
- **Precision formula oversimplified:** Optimal Hamiltonian-simulation bounds have regime-dependent expressions, often
  written with a denominator such as
  `$\log(e+\log(1/\epsilon)/(\alpha t))$`, rather than a universally additive
  `$\log(1/\epsilon)/\log\log(1/\epsilon)$`. State the assumptions and asymptotic regime of the displayed simplification.
- **Resource ambiguity:** `$T$` is not defined as queries, primitive gates, or logical gates. Qubitization query
  optimality does not automatically imply the same total gate complexity after PREPARE/SELECT and synthesis costs.
- **Lower-bound matching:** No-fast-forwarding establishes worst-case linear dependence under particular black-box
  models; it does not say every physical family requires `$t\|H\|$`, since some Hamiltonians are fast-forwardable.
- **Trotter comparison overstated:** A fixed-order product formula has polynomial precision dependence, but choosing
  order with the target accuracy produces subtler subpolynomial/exponent-shrinking behavior. “The
  `$\mathrm{poly}(1/\epsilon)$ that Trotter delivers” should explicitly mean fixed order.
- **Conceptual merit:** The paragraph correctly emphasizes that precision scaling was a central breakthrough; a small
  cost table by access model would make that lesson accurate as well as memorable.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The historical race toward optimality is compelling; exact qualifications would strengthen,
  not slow, the story.

#### §16.1 — what simulation outputs

- **Important distinction:** Explicitly separating state preparation from observable extraction is one of the
  chapter’s strongest pedagogical choices.
- **Strong formula defect:** `$\langle\psi_0|Oe^{-iHt}O^\dagger|\psi_0\rangle$` is generally a complex transition
  amplitude, not an ordinary expectation value of an observable. If the intended quantity is the expectation after
  evolution, write
  `$\langle\psi_0|e^{iHt}Oe^{-iHt}|\psi_0\rangle$`; if it is a time-correlation function, define its operator ordering
  and measurement circuit.
- **Amplitude-estimation mismatch:** Standard amplitude estimation estimates a probability/amplitude encoded by a
  specified state-preparation unitary and “good” projector. It does not directly read an arbitrary complex matrix
  element. A Hadamard test, linear combination of real/imaginary parts, or a bespoke amplitude encoding is needed.
- **Cost-accounting generalization:** Some theoretical papers isolate oracle queries to simulation, but many modern
  end-to-end resource estimates include state preparation, controls, measurement, and repetition. Replace “usually”
  with a precise convention adopted by this chapter.
- **Dominance claim:** Measurement can dominate, but phase estimation can instead make controlled simulation and fault-
  tolerant synthesis dominant. “Frequently” is acceptable only with examples or a scoped application class.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** This is exactly the practical reality check readers need, marred by the central example’s
  incorrect labeling.

#### §16.2 — first-order product formula

- **Formula/convention:** The first-order product is standard. Because matrix products act right-to-left, state whether
  the listed term order is merely conventional; reversing it changes the leading commutator error but not the order.
- **Bound conditions:** The `$O(t^2\|[A,B]\|/r)$` bound suppresses constants and assumes bounded Hermitian terms with
  unitary evolution measured in operator norm. Calling it “per the BCH expansion” is informal; rigorous bounds need a
  remainder argument.
- **Many-term bound:** The commutator sum is a useful leading bound but may acquire ordering/time-dependent refinements.
  It should be presented as a sufficient simplified estimate, not the unique total error.
- **Strong scaling omission:** From the displayed bound one needs
  `$r=O(t^2\sum_{j<k}\|[H_j,H_k]\|/\epsilon)$`. The next sentence silently treats the commutator sum as constant, even
  though it can scale strongly with `$L$`; consequently `$O(Lt^2/\epsilon)$` is not the generic gate count.
- **Gate ambiguity:** Each exponential `$e^{-iH_jt/r}$` need not be one elementary gate. Count term exponentials first,
  then separately state synthesis/routing cost for Pauli strings or general local terms.
- **Precision language:** Again, fixed first order is polynomial in `$1/\epsilon$`; the wording should not pre-judge
  all adaptive-order product-formula methods.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The derivation is brisk and readable, and a normalized concrete example would make the
  scaling memorable.

#### §16.2 — symmetric and higher-order formulas

- **Second-order formula:** The palindrome is correct, though the two central half steps of `$H_L$` merge within a
  step, and boundary terms can merge across repetitions. Raw exponent count and optimized count should be separated.
- **Missing coefficients:** `$O(t^3/r^2)$` suppresses nested-commutator sums and norm/order dependence. Likewise, the
  higher-order expression omits coefficients that can scale with term count and recursive stage count.
- **Choice of steps:** `$r=O(t^{3/2}/\sqrt\epsilon)$` follows only after treating the hidden nested-commutator factor as
  constant. Show it symbolically before giving the normalized shorthand.
- **“Kills” wording:** Symmetry cancels the even-power/local leading contribution in the logarithmic expansion; “kills
  the leading error term” is accessible but could be made more exact.
- **Recursive cost:** “Exponentially growing prefactor” is directionally correct. Naming the usual fivefold Suzuki
  recursion, negative time coefficients, and exponential count in order would give the reader something concrete.
- **Empirical assertion:** “`$S_2$` or `$S_4$` usually wins” depends on target accuracy, structure, parallelization,
  compilation, and hardware. Cite benchmark evidence or say “often serves as a practical starting point.”
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The symmetry story is elegant; a one-line circuit/order illustration could lift it further.

#### §16.2 — claimed advantages

- **Locality versus geometry:** A `$k$`-local term acts on few qubits but need not respect physical hardware
  connectivity. Long Jordan–Wigner strings and nongeometric couplings may require routing, so “matches the
  connectivity” is false in general.
- **Ancilla overclaim:** Direct Pauli rotations can be synthesized without persistent work ancillas, but exponentiating
  an arbitrary succinct local term or optimizing parity/routing networks may use ancillas. Say product formulas do not
  intrinsically require the LCU success register.
- **Analysis claim:** Bounds do not depend “only” on pairwise commutator norms; higher orders use nested commutators,
  and tight evaluation can itself be difficult. Coarse offline bounds are available, which is a more reliable virtue.
- **Strong exclusivity defect:** Trotter is not the “only practical choice” on present-day devices. Analog simulation,
  randomized product formulas/qDRIFT, variational real/imaginary-time methods, Krylov/subspace methods, and native
  pulse approaches are all practical in suitable regimes.
- **NISQ terminology:** “Present-day NISQ” is time-sensitive and underspecified in a book. Give a dated hardware model
  or use “shallow, ancilla-constrained digital experiments.”
- **Entertainment: 3/5.** The decisive list is easy to remember but derives much of its punch from claims that need
  retreat.

#### §16.2 — limitations and NISQ/fault-tolerant dichotomy

- **Symbol collision:** `$t$` has meant physical simulation time throughout, then suddenly means the number of phase-
  estimation bits. Use `$m$` for the estimation-register width.
- **Error budgeting:** An `$m$`-bit phase estimate does not imply every controlled simulation call must independently
  have error exactly `$2^{-m}$`. Approximation errors must be allocated across powers, confidence amplification, and
  the complete phase-estimation circuit.
- **Post-Trotter scaling:** “deliver `$\log(1/\epsilon)$`” suppresses the `$\log\log$` denominator and access/oracle
  costs just discussed. Use one consistent bound and distinguish query from gate complexity.
- **False dichotomy:** Fault-tolerant resource studies can favor high-order or randomized product formulas in relevant
  chemistry regimes, while near-term experiments can use methods other than Trotter. Present Trotter and
  qubitization/QSVT as points in a trade space, not eras of a technology timeline.
- **“Asymptotically correct”:** Better precision asymptotics do not settle finite-regime logical resources or state-
  preparation/readout costs. “Asymptotically preferable in suitable block-oracle models” is accurate.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The contrast provides a strong mnemonic, but it is too binary to remain trustworthy.

#### §16.2 — commutator-scaling bridge

- **Good bridge:** Returning from worst-case asymptotics to Hamiltonian structure is exactly the right transition.
- **Terminology:** The cited work develops theory of Trotter error with commutator structure; “effective error often
  scales as” needs the applicable product order and nested-commutator quantity. The first-order pairwise sum shown here
  is not the full general result.
- **Comparison baseline:** Calling a sum-of-commutators improvement over a “naive sum-of-norms” is sensible, but write
  the latter bound so readers can see what becomes smaller.
- **Evidence needed:** “Frequently much smaller” and “the reason Trotter remains competitive on real chemistry
  instances” are empirical/causal claims. Cite resource comparisons and acknowledge compilation constants,
  low-rank/factorized structure, and native gate sets as additional reasons.
- **Bibliographic precision:** Verify the author order, year, and exact cited result in the bibliography.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** It ends with a satisfying rescue of an apparently obsolete method and encourages structural
  rather than slogan-level thinking.

#### §16.3 — LCU setup and decomposition

- **Scope:** LCU addresses more than one problem “Trotter cannot”; it is a representation/implementation primitive for
  nonunitary linear combinations. Saying precisely that Trotter directly composes exponentials whereas LCU coherently
  combines terms would make the contrast less adversarial and more illuminating.
- **Coefficient assumption:** Requiring `$\alpha_j>0$` is harmless only after phases/signs have been absorbed into the
  unitaries `$U_j$`. State this conversion, including complex coefficients, or readers may think LCU excludes ordinary
  signed Pauli decompositions.
- **Zero coefficients:** Strict positivity unnecessarily excludes zero terms; they can be removed or allow
  `$\alpha_j\geq0$`.
- **Input-cost omission:** “Can be implemented as a quantum circuit” does not ensure SELECT can be implemented with
  cost comparable to one `$U_j$`. A multiplexed SELECT may scale with `$L$` unless structure/QROM/oracles are supplied.
- **Odd example:** “giant SWAP-like permutations” is vivid but undefined and distracts from the clean Pauli example.
  Either exhibit a useful permutation decomposition or replace it with a standard sparse-matrix/oracle example.
- **Hermiticity:** The displayed positive combination of arbitrary unitaries need not be Hermitian despite being called
  `$H$`. Require the overall sum to be Hermitian when it represents a Hamiltonian, or use `$A$` for the generic LCU.
- **Goal wording:** Applying a nonunitary `$H$` cannot be a deterministic normalized quantum operation on arbitrary
  states. Preview that LCU embeds it as a probabilistic block rather than saying simply “apply.”
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The setup gives the reader a concrete problem quickly; the unexplained “giant” example feels
  more whimsical than useful.

#### §16.3 — PREPARE, SELECT, and unprepare

- **Register size notation:** For non-power-of-two `$L$`, write `$a=\lceil\log_2L\rceil$` consistently and use
  `$|0^a\rangle$`; `$|0^{\log L}\rangle$` suggests a fractional number of qubits.
- **PREPARE correctness:** The amplitudes are normalized because `$\alpha=\sum_j\alpha_j$`; it would help to verify
  this explicitly and state what PREPARE costs or what oracle supplies the coefficients.
- **SELECT unitarity:** The formula is unitary only when its definition is completed on unused selector basis states
  `$j\geq L$`. Define those branches as identity (or any fixed unitary).
- **Controlled implementation cost:** SELECT is the algorithmic heart, not a free operation. The exposition should
  distinguish one SELECT query from its logical-gate/QROM implementation.
- **Circuit order:** The prepare–select–unprepare order and algebra are correct and exceptionally clear.
- **Residual notation:** `$|\bot\rangle$` is generally an unnormalized vector, despite ket notation often implying a
  normalized state. Say this explicitly; its squared norm is `$1-\|H|\psi\rangle\|^2/\alpha^2$`.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** This is a compact, memorable construction with a satisfying algebraic reveal.

#### §16.3 — post-selection and amplification

- **Normalization defect:** Measurement followed by successful post-selection leaves
  `$H|\psi\rangle/\|H|\psi\rangle\|$`, not `$H|\psi\rangle/\alpha$`. The latter is the unnormalized good component
  before conditioning. “Up to a normalisation factor” acknowledges this but leaves the main sentence contradictory.
- **Success probability:** The probability and quadratic improvement are correct when `$H|\psi\rangle\neq0$`.
  Explicitly handle the kernel case, where no amplification can create a nonexistent good component.
- **Amplification assumptions:** Ordinary amplitude amplification requires reflections tied to the complete initial
  state, including `$|\psi\rangle$`, and knowledge/handling of the success amplitude. Oblivious amplitude amplification
  is often the relevant LCU tool but needs conditions on the encoded operation. The stated overhead is not automatic
  from Chapter 14 alone.
- **Big-O interpretation:** `$O(\alpha/\|H|\psi\rangle\|)$` counts uses/iterations, not total gates; multiply by
  PREPARE/SELECT/reflection costs.
- **State dependence:** A linear-algebra routine whose cost depends on the unknown input overlap can be unstable near
  the kernel. Flag this now because condition numbers later encode the same issue.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The Grover connection is rewarding, though the conditioned-versus-unnormalized state must
  be fixed.

#### §16.3 — subnormalisation as an optimization target

- **Terminology:** `$\sum_j|c_j|$` is the coefficient one-norm for a specified decomposition, not “the one-norm” of
  the operator. The distinction matters because different decompositions have different values.
- **Grouping claim needs care:** Merely partitioning identical Pauli coefficients does not change their absolute sum.
  Reducing `$\alpha$` by orders of magnitude requires a genuinely different LCU (for example, combining terms into
