  efficiently selectable unitaries or exploiting factorization), with possible PREPARE/SELECT cost tradeoffs.
- **Optimization objective incomplete:** Minimizing `$\alpha$` alone is not always primary; the meaningful resource is
  often `$\alpha$` times block-encoding query/gate cost. A smaller normalization with much harder state preparation can
  lose overall.
- **Chemistry evidence:** “Orders of magnitude” requires a cited decomposition/resource comparison and a clear
  Hamiltonian family.
- **Notation:** This paragraph correctly switches from positive `$\alpha_j$` to signed `$c_j$`; explicitly connect the
  two by phase absorption.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** It reveals the hidden number that controls the method, an excellent design insight.

#### §16.3 — LCU as a block encoding

- **Core insight:** Identifying the top-left block is exact and provides a clean conceptual bridge to the next section.
- **Scaling language:** The compound is an `$(\alpha,a,0)$` block encoding of `$H$`, equivalently its top-left block is
  `$H/\alpha$`. Calling it “a block encoding of `$H/\alpha$`” risks later confusion over whether the normalization
  parameter is 1 or `$\alpha$`; adopt the standard tuple immediately.
- **Projector notation:** The parenthetical top-left-block description is helpful but slightly cumbersome. Define
  `$\Pi=|0^a\rangle\langle0^a|\otimes I$` once and reuse it.
- **Composition premise:** The compound is unitary because each factor is unitary; spelling this out reinforces why an
  apparently nonunitary matrix action can live inside a legal circuit.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** This is the chapter’s dependency chain paying off in one line.

#### §16.3 — truncated-Taylor simulation

- **High-level construction:** Expanding `$H^k$` into products of selected unitaries is correct, but the practical
  algorithm segments total time so the LCU coefficient sum and amplification overhead stay bounded. Omitting
  segmentation makes the “one large LCU” description misleading for large `$t$`.
- **Truncation regime:** `$K=O(\log(1/\epsilon)/\log\log(1/\epsilon))$` applies after segmenting/normalizing a bounded-
  size time interval. In general `$K$` depends on segment parameters and the Hamiltonian LCU normalization.
- **Strong complexity defect:** The gate count `$O(Lt\log(1/\epsilon)/\log\log(1/\epsilon))$` omits
  `$\alpha=\sum_j\alpha_j$`, PREPARE/SELECT costs, segmentation, and additive/regime subtleties. `$L$` is not necessarily
  the right multiplier if SELECT is an oracle or structured circuit.
- **Historical priority:** “The first algorithm” should be tied to an exact model/result and citation. Earlier sparse-
  Hamiltonian simulation work already improved precision dependence in different regimes; the intended breakthrough
  appears to be near-linear time and logarithmic-over-logarithmic precision scaling.
- **Comparison consistency:** Calling every Trotter method polynomial in inverse precision repeats the fixed-order
  overstatement.
- **Pedagogical opportunity:** A two-term, second-order Taylor LCU would let readers see how selector indices encode
  products rather than asking them to imagine a combinatorially large sum.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The exponential-precision payoff is exciting, but the missing segmentation conceals the
  cleverest engineering in the method.

#### §16.4 — block-encoding definition and matrix form

- **Definition:** The exact `$(\alpha,a,0)$` definition is clear and standard for square `$A$` acting on the system
  register.
- **Norm constraint:** `$\alpha\geq\|A\|$` follows from a subblock of a unitary being a contraction; saying this rather
  than merely declaring it would build intuition.
- **Approximate tuple omitted:** The prose mentions a nonzero third parameter but never defines the convention, usually
  a bound such as
  `$\|A-\alpha(\langle0^a|\otimes I)U_A(|0^a\rangle\otimes I)\|\leq\epsilon$`.
- **Dimension caveat:** QSVT commonly accommodates rectangular matrices using distinct left/right projectors or
  projected unitary encodings. The square top-left-block definition is fine for this chapter if its scope is stated.
- **Matrix display:** The stars are appropriately described, but their dimensions could be noted; this is especially
  useful once ancilla and system dimensions differ greatly.
- **Notation consistency:** Calling `$\alpha$` “subnormalisation” is conventional, though “normalization factor” is
  more intuitive; using both once would help newcomers.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** An abstract interface becomes surprisingly tangible through the block matrix.

#### §16.4 — applying and reading a block encoding

- **Unnormalized residual:** As in §16.3, `$|\bot\rangle$` is not normally normalized and should be identified as the
  component in the orthogonal ancilla subspace.
- **Conditioned state:** Successful post-selection produces the normalized state
  `$A|\psi\rangle/\|A|\psi\rangle\|$`, not an operationally available subnormalized state.
- **Amplification overclaim:** Recovery “to `$O(1)$`” depends on the good amplitude, reflections, and whether ordinary
  or oblivious amplitude amplification applies. If `$A|\psi\rangle$` is tiny or zero, the required cost diverges or
  success is impossible.
- **Composition wording:** Downstream block operations preserve an encoded subspace algebraically; they do not in
  general preserve the physical good component after simply applying each unitary. This distinction motivates the
  projected-unitary/QSVT machinery.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The operational interpretation is useful and naturally raises the right concern—how to avoid
  measuring after every block.

#### §16.4 — LCU source

- **Scaling convention:** Again say “an `$(\alpha,a,0)$` block encoding of `$H$`” rather than “of `$H/\alpha$`” to keep
  the tuple convention stable.
- **Chemistry universality:** “Almost every chemistry application” is unsupported and likely too broad; chemistry also
  uses sparse access, tensor/low-rank factorizations, first-quantized encodings, product formulas, and other oracles.
- **Cost tradeoff:** Coefficient one-norm is important, but PREPARE and SELECT realization, logical ancillas, QROM
  bandwidth, and Toffoli/T cost need mention.
- **Positive feature:** The backward reference makes the abstraction feel earned rather than dropped on the reader.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Familiar machinery acquires a useful new name without repetition.

#### §16.4 — sparse-access source

- **Oracle specification:** The row-position and entry-value oracles need coherent reversible definitions, value
  precision, a known sparsity bound, and a promise for invalid positions/duplicate entries.
- **Normalization:** `$s\|A\|_{\max}$` is a familiar coarse normalization, but the exact construction and whether `$A$`
  is Hermitian should be cited. Improved row/column norm encodings exist, so avoid presenting this as the only sparse
  interface.
- **Ancilla-count suspicion:** `$O(\log N+\log s)$` ancillas appears construction- and convention-dependent and may
  count a duplicated index register that some definitions treat differently. Verify it against the cited theorem.
- **Query-complexity suspicion:** `$O(\log(1/\epsilon))$` oracle queries may be conflating arithmetic/state-rotation
  synthesis precision with calls to sparse-access oracles; standard constructions can use a constant number of oracle
  calls while gate precision scales polylogarithmically. This deserves source-level verification.
- **Faulty density conclusion:** Sparsity normalization degrading with `$s$` does not explain why *dense* matrices
  rarely have useful encodings “outside LCU”; dense structured matrices may have efficient state-preparation,
  QRAM-style, Fourier, density-operator, or arithmetic encodings. Input access and normalization, not density alone,
  determine usefulness.
- **Dimension terminology:** `$N$` appears without definition; state that `$A\in\mathbb C^{N\times N}$`.
- **Entertainment: 3/5.** This is valuable concreteness, but several resource numbers feel dropped in rather than
  derived.

#### §16.4 — density-matrix source

- **Construction omitted:** A purification state alone is not an executable oracle. Given a unitary `$G$` preparing
  the purification and access to `$G^\dagger$`, a SWAP-based construction yields the block encoding. State the circuit
  and which subsystem purifies which.
- **Normalization:** `$\alpha=1$` is correct for a density operator, but the useful complexity also depends on repeated
  coherent calls to purification and inverse-preparation, not possession of physical copies alone.
- **Tomography link questionable:** Block-encoded spectral transformations can support entropy, fidelity, and related
  estimation tasks, but full tomography still has output-size/sample barriers. “Tomography questions enter” risks
  implying those barriers disappear.
- **Chapter reference:** Chapter 11 should be checked for the exact purification/block-encoding material; otherwise the
  cross-reference promises background the reader will not find.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The bridge from states to matrix algorithms is genuinely surprising and deserves the missing
  one-line circuit.

#### §16.4 — black-box portability and its price

- **Interface insight:** This is an excellent software-abstraction analogy and likely the section’s most memorable
  paragraph.
- **“Automatically portable” overclaim:** Algorithms also impose Hermiticity, singular-value interval, parity,
  conditioning, error, and state-preparation promises. A syntactically valid block encoding is not sufficient for every
  downstream theorem.
- **Incomplete price:** `$\alpha$` is central, but encoding query cost, approximation error, ancilla count, controlled
  access, inverse access, and initial/final state overlap can dominate.
- **Post-selection framing:** QSVT often avoids literal post-selection at intermediate transformations, so saying
  `$\alpha$` determines “every post-selection” understates its role in polynomial degree while overstating the number
  of actual measurements.
- **Resource-estimate claim:** “The dominant cost parameter” is application-dependent and needs examples; surface-code
  factories, SELECT synthesis, and data loading can dominate even at fixed `$\alpha$`.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The black-box interface gives the technical stack a clean conceptual center.

#### §16.4 — adjoints, controls, products, and QSVT bridge

- **Adjoint identity:** The adjoint statement is correct for the same ancilla projector and exact encoding.
- **Controlled-unitary ambiguity:** A controlled `$U_A$` has a block such as
  `$|0\rangle\langle0|\otimes I+|1\rangle\langle1|\otimes A/\alpha$`; it is not simply another encoding of `$A$` unless
  the control is fixed/projected appropriately. Define the intended controlled subspace.
- **Product construction:** Products of block encodings require arranging separate ancillas/projectors and compatible
  system action; the normalization multiplies and errors also accumulate. “Ancilla count adds” is a safe basic
  construction, not necessarily a lower bound or an unavoidable optimized cost.
- **QSVT scope:** For a general non-Hermitian matrix, QSVT transforms singular values with left/right singular-vector
  structure; it is not naively “a polynomial in `$A$`.” Polynomial eigenvalue transformation applies under additional
  Hermitian/normal structure and parity constraints.
- **Ancilla reuse:** QSVT’s constant/small signal-ancilla reuse is a crucial advantage, but polynomial degree still
  requires repeated `$U_A$` and `$U_A^\dagger$` queries plus phase rotations. State this cost so “avoids blowup” does
  not sound free.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The final warning about naive multiplication creates an effective cliffhanger for QSVT.

#### §16.5 — qubitization overview and setup

- **Conceptual introduction:** Calling qubitization a spectral lift is excellent, and locating it between block
  encodings and signal processing makes the dependency graph easy to retain.
- **Strong construction concern:** For a completely generic block encoding `$U_H$`, the simple walk
  `$W=(R\otimes I)U_H$` need not preserve the stated two-dimensional subspace or have phases
  `$\pm\arccos\lambda$`. The standard one-reflection construction assumes an appropriate Hermitian/involutory
  standard-form signal oracle (as SELECT often is), while a general projected unitary encoding uses a construction
  involving both `$U_H$`, `$U_H^\dagger$`, and projectors/reflections. State the missing signal-unitary condition or
  replace the walk with the fully general construction.
- **Renormalization wording:** An `$(\alpha,a,0)$` encoding already has top-left block `$H/\alpha$`; one does not
  physically “divide” `$H$` again. Define the normalized signal matrix `$\widetilde H=H/\alpha$`, whose norm is at most
  one, and carry `$\alpha t$` into all costs.
- **Reflection implementation:** `$R=2|0^a\rangle\langle0^a|-I$` is a multi-controlled phase, not free. Its synthesis
  cost and possible clean ancilla should enter gate accounting even if it costs no block-oracle query.
- **Terminology/history:** Verify whether the cited 2017 paper is the intended qubitization paper and distinguish
  qubitization from the subsequent QSP-based optimal simulation result.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The bridge metaphor is strong and makes a difficult construction feel motivated.

#### §16.5 — invariant-subspace and spectral mapping claim

- **Dependence on missing premise:** The elegant two-dimensional rotation statement is valid only after fixing the
  signal-oracle construction noted above; it cannot rescue an arbitrary `$U_H$` from unspecified off-diagonal blocks.
- **Residual normalization:** The vector
  `$U_H|0^a\rangle|\lambda\rangle-\lambda|0^a\rangle|\lambda\rangle$` has norm
  `$\sqrt{1-\lambda^2}$` under the relevant assumptions and should be normalized before serving as a basis vector.
- **Endpoint exception:** At `$\lambda=\pm1$` that residual vanishes, so the “2D subspace” collapses to one dimension.
  Treat endpoints separately.
- **Phase/sign conventions:** Whether the walk eigenvalues are `$e^{\pm i\arccos\lambda}$`, have a minus sign, or use a
  doubled angle depends on the exact walk/reflection convention. Derive the 2×2 matrix once to pin the convention.
- **“Spectrum consists” overclaim:** The claim accounts for signal subspaces corresponding to `$H$` but not necessarily
  every eigenvalue on unused ancilla/system subspaces. Say the relevant invariant subspaces have those eigenphases.
- **One-to-one wording:** `$\lambda\mapsto\arccos\lambda$` is one-to-one on `[-1,1]`, but each eigenvalue yields a phase
  pair and degeneracies persist. “Correspondence” is safer than a global one-to-one spectrum claim.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The geometry is beautiful; a normalized two-vector derivation would make it one of the book’s
  standout explanations.

#### §16.5 — QSP consequence and optimal simulation bound

- **Polynomial language:** A generic polynomial in a unitary `$W$` is constrained on the unit circle; QSP implements
  structured Laurent/polynomial transformations through signal projectors. “Any polynomial function” is too broad.
- **Control model:** Standard QSP alternates signal unitaries and phase rotations/reflections; it does not always
  require a separately controlled `$W$` or “single auxiliary qubit” in the way stated. Keep one consistent convention
  through §§16.5–16.7.
- **Strong asymptotic inconsistency:** The displayed optimal precision term matches §16.1’s simplified
  `$\log/\log\log$` form, while §16.6 later claims a Jacobi–Anger degree
  `$\Theta(t+\log(1/\epsilon))$`. Both cannot describe the same optimal query degree. Use the regime-sensitive optimal
  expression consistently.
- **Normalization missing again:** Query complexity should scale with `$\alpha t$` for an `$\alpha$`-block encoding,
  not merely `$t$`, unless `$H$` has explicitly been renamed to the normalized signal and the requested evolution time
  adjusted.
- **Theta caution:** Upper and lower bounds require oracle-model and parameter-regime qualifications; additive
  simplified terms may not be a uniform `$\Theta$` statement.
- **Jacobi–Anger detail:** It approximates trigonometric/exponential functions through Bessel/Chebyshev coefficients;
  calling it “the Jacobi–Anger polynomial of `$W$`” is shorthand that should be unpacked.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** This is an exciting payoff, but the contradictory precision formulas undermine confidence
  in the headline result.

#### §16.5 — priority and 2026 resource-efficiency claims

- **Priority claim:** “First algorithm” and “without ... polynomial-in-log overhead” need exact citations and a
  comparison against truncated-Taylor and corrected-walk approaches in the same query model.
- **Potential characterization error:** Truncated Taylor already achieved near-optimal
  `$\log(1/\epsilon)/\log\log(1/\epsilon)$` truncation; describe precisely what overhead qubitization removed (query,
  robust amplification, ancilla, or gates) rather than saying vaguely polynomial in log.
- **Time-sensitive superlative:** “Most resource-efficient ... known in 2026” must be verified against current primary
  resource-estimation literature and scoped by Hamiltonian representation, precision, hardware cost model, and task.
- **“Many” needs evidence:** Tensor hypercontraction and double factorization can reduce normalization/resource costs,
  but no universal winner follows. Product formulas, interaction-picture algorithms, first-quantized methods, and
  alternative factorizations can win in other regimes.
- **“Modest precision” tension:** Large fixed costs can make qubitization less competitive exactly at modest precision;
  this sentence conflicts with §16.7’s later admission that Trotter/LCU can be cheaper there.
- **Terminology:** Use “double factorization” consistently (British versus American spelling is secondary, but the book
  should pick a house style).
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Concrete chemistry methods add relevance, though the unsupported leaderboard tone dates the
  prose immediately.

#### §16.5 — spectral-lift recap

- **Recap value:** Rephrasing the mechanism as a spectral lift is useful rather than redundant; this is the right idea
  for readers to retain.
- **Unstructured-unitary issue:** A block encoding’s off-block structure is deliberately unconstrained, reinforcing why
  the simple `$RU_H$` walk cannot have the advertised spectrum without additional structure.
- **Overbroad downstream claim:** Phase estimation can act on eigenphases directly, but amplitude estimation needs a
  good-subspace probability oracle and reflections; it does not automatically apply merely because eigenphases exist.
- **Layer description:** “Unifying lower layer” is persuasive but should acknowledge that QSVT can be formulated
  directly with alternating projected phases and a block encoding rather than requiring readers to materialize a
  separate walk.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The final paragraph delivers a clean mental model and a satisfying transition.

#### §16.6 — QSP signal question

- **Signal convention:** The defined `$W(x)=e^{i\arccos(x)\sigma_X}$` has diagonal entry `$x$` and imaginary
  off-diagonal entries. Later formulas for `$Q$` must respect those factors of `$i$`; write the full 2×2 matrix.
- **Hidden parameter framing:** “Hidden” is evocative, although in applications `$x$` is an eigenvalue/singular value
  across invariant subspaces rather than a classical unknown supplied to a one-qubit device.
- **Rotation convention:** Quantum-information rotations often use `$e^{-i\theta X/2}$`; explicitly declaring this
  signal convention avoids factor-of-two/sign confusion.
- **Historical attribution:** The two cited QSP stages are plausible but need full bibliography links and a note on
  related composite-pulse origins if the chapter makes priority claims.
- **Question design:** Presenting QSP first as a self-contained synthesis problem is pedagogically excellent.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** It turns a formidable acronym into an intriguing, sharply posed puzzle.

#### §16.6 — polynomial characterization theorem

- **Counting error:** The prose says “three conditions” but enumerates degree bounds, parity of `$P$`, parity of `$Q$`,
  and a norm identity—at least four clauses depending on grouping.
- **Convention verification required:** With the displayed `$X$`-signal and `$Z$` phases, the off-diagonal entry may
  carry an `$i\sqrt{1-x^2}$` factor and conjugation/sign relations. Verify the exact matrix form against the theorem.
- **Sufficiency likely incomplete:** Standard QSP characterizations include parity, boundedness/unitarity, reciprocal or
  reality/conjugation symmetries depending on convention, and sometimes conditions outside `[-1,1]`. The stated
  complex-polynomial pair theorem may need more than the single interval norm identity to be exactly converse.
- **Degree language:** “With `$d+1$` phases” there are `$d$` signal calls; the degree bounds are clear and should be
  retained.
- **Efficient phase finding:** “Efficient algorithms” should state complexity, numerical precision model, and whether
  they synthesize exact compatible pairs or approximate target polynomials. This matters given the later instability
  discussion.
- **Citation hygiene:** Confirm the long 2020 author list, paper title, and what exact phase-finding guarantee it proves.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The theorem feels powerful, but four dense conditions without a degree-2 example tax the
  reader.

#### §16.6 — reachability consequence and qubitized application

- **“Only obstructions”:** This is true only if the preceding theorem is complete in the chosen convention. Until then,
  the sweeping sentence amplifies any omitted condition.
- **Extra-qubit ambiguity:** Earlier QSP was itself a single-qubit signal model; qubitization often uses the signal
  subspace/ancilla projector directly. Explain exactly where the “extra auxiliary qubit” enters and which operation is
  controlled.
- **Measurement overstatement:** A QSP/QSVT sequence yields a block encoding whose good component is `$P(H)|\psi\rangle`;
  measuring an auxiliary qubit alone may not select all signal ancillas, and successful conditioning normalizes the
  state only when that component is nonzero.
- **Complex polynomial:** For general complex `$P$`, physical implementation and extraction may occupy different
  matrix entries/parity sectors. “Different choices implement algorithms” is directionally right but hides these
  compatibility requirements.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The abstract synthesis theorem suddenly becoming a matrix algorithm is the section’s central
  thrill.

#### §16.6 — Hamiltonian-simulation example

- **Scaling variables:** If `$x$` is an eigenvalue of normalized `$H/\alpha$`, approximate
  `$e^{-i\alpha tx}$`; writing `$\|H\|t$` assumes an optimal norm-normalized encoding that may not exist.
- **P/Q mismatch:** The matrix-entry theorem has off-diagonal `$\sqrt{1-x^2}Q(x)$`; simply choosing `$Q$` to approximate
  sine does not directly make that entry `$\sin(\tau x)$`. The even/odd real and imaginary polynomial components and
  their placement must be described accurately.
- **Strong precision contradiction:** Degree
  `$\Theta(\|H\|t+\log(1/\epsilon))$` is not the “optimal query count” claimed one section earlier with a
  `$\log/\log\log$` term. A looser big-O could be correct, but it cannot be called the matching optimum.
- **Approximation degree:** Jacobi–Anger truncation has parameter-regime-dependent tails; cite the exact degree bound
  rather than compressing it into an inconsistent theta formula.
- **Sign:** The target alternates between `$e^{+i\|H\|tx}$` and `$e^{-iHt}$`; phases can correct the sign, but the text
  should not make the reader infer that.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The example is the promised payoff, but inconsistent equations make it harder to trust than
  the intuitive lead-in.

#### §16.6 — matrix-inversion example

- **Boundedness omission:** QSP entries must remain bounded by one, so the implementable target is a scaled reciprocal,
  roughly `$1/(\kappa x)$`, not raw `$1/x$` on a domain where it reaches `$\kappa$`.
- **Output normalization:** Conditioning can yield the normalized inverse state, but success probability introduces
  condition-number and input-overlap costs. Polynomial degree alone is not the complete query complexity.
- **Degree claim:** Verify whether the stated `$\Theta(\kappa\log(\kappa/\epsilon))$` is the approximation degree,
  total queries after amplification, or a simplified upper bound. Relative versus absolute solution-state error also
  changes logarithms.
- **Hermiticity:** Qubitization of `$A$` directly assumes Hermitian structure here. A general linear system needs
  Hermitian embedding or QSVT’s singular-vector transformation with compatible state/block interfaces.
- **HHL comparison:** Original-HHL complexities depend on sparsity, simulation, condition number, desired output error,
  and later improvements. The bare `$O(\kappa^2/\epsilon)$` baseline and “exponentially better” statement need a
  citation and a like-for-like success/error convention.
- **Readout reminder:** Producing `$\lvert A^{-1}b\rangle$` does not output the classical solution vector; retain the
  Chapter 15 caveat.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Recasting HHL as reciprocal approximation is a powerful explanatory win once scaling is
  repaired.

#### §16.6 — unification conclusion

- **Excellent thesis:** “Once you know the polynomial” is memorable and captures the algorithm-design shift.
- **Necessary qualifier:** One must also know that the polynomial satisfies parity/boundedness constraints, possess an
  efficient block encoding, compute stable phases, prepare the input, and extract the answer. Adding “much of the
  query-level algorithm” would prevent the slogan from erasing implementation.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** A strong, concise close that earns its rhetorical confidence conceptually.

#### §16.7 — QSVT definition

- **Arbitrary-matrix dimensions:** A top-left block of a unitary is naturally square; genuinely rectangular matrices
  require projected unitary encodings with different input/output projectors or padding. Specify which convention is
  intended.
- **Notation collision:** `$W$` denoted the qubitized walk in §§16.5–16.6 and now denotes the left-singular-vector
  matrix in the SVD. Use conventional `$U\Sigma V^\dagger$` or another letter to avoid a severe local collision.
- **Transformation parity:** Singular-value transformation does not always produce simply
  `$W P(\Sigma)V^\dagger$`. Odd polynomials map between right and left singular spaces in that form; even polynomials
  act within a side with different projector structure. State the parity-dependent output.
- **Complex-polynomial conditions:** Referencing “the QSP parity-and-norm conditions” is too compressed given the prior
  theorem’s possible convention omissions; give the exact QSVT theorem or a carefully scoped simplified version.
- **Scaling:** A general `$(\alpha,a,\epsilon_{\rm BE})$` encoding transforms singular values of `$A/\alpha$`, with
  accumulated implementation error. Starting at exact normalization one is pedagogically fine, but restore costs
  before applications.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The SVD extension is naturally exciting, though overloaded notation makes the key formula
  unnecessarily slippery.

#### §16.7 — Hermitian reduction and hermitisation claim

- **Strong conceptual defect:** For Hermitian `$A$`, singular values are `$|\lambda_j|$`, but QSVT does not simply
  “reduce to QSP on qubitization” for every polynomial; sign information and odd/even parity determine how eigenvalues
  transform. This needs a careful eigenvalue-transformation statement.
- **Hermitian embedding comparison:** QSVT’s direct rectangular/projected treatment can avoid an explicit doubled
  Hermitian matrix, but claiming hermitisation “would double ... the subnormalisation” is dubious. The standard block
  matrix `$\begin{pmatrix}0&A\\A^\dagger&0\end{pmatrix}$` doubles dimension but preserves operator norm, so why would
  normalization necessarily double?
- **“Genuinely new”:** Historically/rhetorically plausible, but explain the mathematical novelty—alternating projector
  phases transforming singular subspaces—rather than defining it by an overstated avoided cost.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The generalization lands well, but the false normalization penalty should be removed.

#### §16.7 — claimed algorithm unification list

- **Scope overclaim:** “Essentially every quantum algorithm built on a block-encoded matrix” is close to tautological
  yet still overbroad; algorithms may use rational functions, variable-time methods, measurements, adaptivity, or
  multi-matrix structure not captured by one polynomial alone.
- **Amplitude amplification:** Standard Grover rotations relate to Chebyshev polynomials, but stating simply
  `$P(x)=T_k(x)$` applied to a projector block encoding misses the odd-degree amplitude polynomial, phase conventions,
  and fixed-point versus ordinary amplification distinctions.
- **Phase estimation:** A filter/comb polynomial can approximate phase discrimination, but QPE produces a coherent
  multi-bit estimate and its distribution, not merely a binary polynomial transform. “Equivalent up to constant
  factors” needs a theorem, accuracy/gap/confidence model, and output construction; likely it is too strong as written.
- **Matrix inversion:** Restore reciprocal scaling, conditioning, post-selection/amplification, and non-Hermitian
  parity details from §16.6.
- **Hamiltonian simulation:** Restore normalization `$\alpha t$`, sign, and the corrected optimal approximation degree.
- **Ground-state preparation:** Polynomial filtering also requires an initial overlap with the target subspace, a
  promised spectral gap/threshold, energy-location knowledge, and amplification; these can dominate.
- **Thresholding/fitting:** “Fitting” is undefined and quotation marks do not clarify it. State the actual operation and
  distinguish hard threshold approximation (which needs a transition gap) from regression/model fitting.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The rapid parade of applications is exhilarating; precise input/output promises would turn
  it from a slogan list into a durable reference.

#### §16.7 — approximation-theory thesis

- **Query-degree relation:** Degree generally determines the number of signal-oracle uses up to convention-dependent
  constants, but total gates also include phase rotations, controls, block-encoding cost, error synthesis, and
  repetitions.
- **Not polynomial approximation alone:** State preparation, normalization, conditioning, success probability,
  observable extraction, and sometimes rational/variable-time constructions remain quantum-algorithm design problems.
- **Classical theorem references:** Bernstein and Jackson denote families of results, not self-identifying citations;
  specify the particular approximation bounds used. Chebyshev is a polynomial family/method, not a theorem parallel
  to the other two.
- **Positive insight:** Reducing query design to approximation theory is indeed QSVT’s unifying contribution and is
  very well expressed.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** This is intellectually satisfying and opens a genuine bridge between fields.

#### §16.7 — grand-unification slogan

- **Quotation/citation:** The short quotation is within a reasonable excerpt length, but supply a page/section and
  verify the wording against Martyn–Rossi–Tan–Chuang (2021).
- **Analogy status:** Clearly label it as an analogy, not an equivalence; classical signal processing includes much
  beyond Fourier analysis, and quantum “signal” here has a specialized meaning.
- **Literal-QSP explanation:** The follow-up sentence usefully grounds the metaphor in alternating signal-dependent and
  signal-independent rotations.
- **Control wording:** “Controlled rotation” should match the actual signal convention established earlier; the
  displayed `$W(x)$` was an ordinary one-qubit rotation parameterized by `$x$`, not explicitly controlled.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The line is quotable and the explanation prevents it from floating away as mere branding.

#### §16.7 — classical phase computation

- **Engineering relevance:** Surfacing classical compilation is excellent; many expositions pretend phase sequences
  appear for free.
- **Threshold claim:** “Naive root-finding is unstable for `$d\gtrsim10^3$`” is method-, polynomial-, conditioning-,
  precision-, and implementation-dependent. Cite a benchmark rather than setting a universal threshold.
- **Large-degree claim:** “Handle `$d$` up to `$10^5$` or more in double precision” is highly specific and must be
  verified for each named algorithm and target-polynomial class. Some methods trade accuracy, symmetry, runtime, or
  require a feasible polynomial representation.
- **Terminology/reference check:** Verify the exact names and guarantees of Haah’s “halving” method,
  Chao–Ding–Gilyén–Huang–Szegedy, and Dong–Meng–Whaley–Lin’s symmetric-QSP work.
- **Precision propagation:** Explain how phase error accumulates into implemented-polynomial/operator error and how
  accurately each physical `$Z$` rotation must then be synthesized fault-tolerantly.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The numerical-instability surprise is engaging and refreshingly practical.

#### §16.7 — constants and deployment conclusion

- **Balanced caveat:** Acknowledging constants and finite-regime competitors is necessary and improves credibility.
- **“Sometimes large”:** Give at least one representative block-encoding/query/T-count comparison; otherwise the phrase
  conveys no actionable scale.
- **Repeated false dichotomy:** QSVT is not automatically the fault-tolerant default. Fault-tolerant product formulas,
  interaction-picture simulation, truncated Taylor, and specialized algorithms may win depending on Hamiltonian,
  precision, and oracle implementation.
- **Near-term inconsistency:** Earlier §16.5 called qubitization most resource-efficient even at modest precision,
  whereas this paragraph admits hand-tuned LCU/Trotter can be cheaper. Reconcile the regimes.
- **NISQ framing:** QSVT depth and coherent oracle requirements often make it unsuitable near term, but “fault-
  tolerant default” is still a recommendation requiring a dated cost model, not a theorem.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The sober ending is welcome, though it retreats into the same oversimple technology split
  criticized in §16.2.

#### §16.8 — section framing

- **Practitioner focus:** Naming the four threads gives the closing survey a clear job and stops “modern view” from
  becoming an unstructured miscellany.
- **Date sensitivity:** “2025–2026 literature” will age immediately. Either make this an explicitly dated snapshot with
  a source cutoff or phrase it as a durable guide to evaluating new literature.
- **Selection rationale:** Explain why these four threads, rather than error mitigation, interaction-picture methods,
  quantum optimization, or lower-bound methodology, represent the relevant practitioner view.
- **Promise calibration:** “What the toolkit delivers” suggests all four threads are direct QSVT consequences;
  dequantisation and resource accounting are critical perspectives, not outputs of the toolkit.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The change from mechanism to consequences refreshes the chapter at the right moment.

#### §16.8 — Monte Carlo scaling

- **Classical rate scope:** Sample-mean root-mean-square error is `$\sigma/\sqrt N$` for independent samples with finite
  variance; the Central Limit Theorem describes asymptotic distribution, while the variance identity itself gives the
  RMSE rate. Confidence/high-probability error needs additional assumptions or robust estimators.
- **Quantum rate oversimplified:** Canonical amplitude estimation estimates a bounded encoded amplitude with additive
  error roughly `$O(1/N)$`, not automatically `$O(\sigma/N)$`. Variance-dependent quantum mean estimators exist under
  specific boundedness, oracle, and success-probability assumptions; cite the exact theorem intended.
- **Query definition:** A “Grover-style query” includes coherent sampler/function oracle calls and their inverses.
  Compare these to classical sample costs only after accounting for reversible arithmetic and state preparation.
- **Encoding requirements:** The paragraph rightly emphasizes coherent access, but `$f$` must also be bounded/scaled
  into a rotation or probability, and desired precision in arithmetic affects gates and qubits.
- **Opaque-computation overstatement:** A classical computation can often be compiled reversibly, so opacity does not
  mathematically imply “no speedup”; it may make coherent query cost erase the advantage. The fair statement is that
  black-box classical samples without coherent oracle access do not supply the amplitude-estimation model.
- **Quadratic label:** The query-complexity improvement is quadratic in inverse additive error, not necessarily an end-
  to-end runtime advantage.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The catch arrives promptly after the headline and keeps the account grounded.

#### §16.8 — claimed application domains

- **Sweeping feasibility claim:** Saying coherent requirements “are met” for path integrals, Markovian finance, and
  partition functions covers enormous problem classes. Give one explicit algorithm/access construction per domain and
  specify when efficient state preparation is known.
- **Path-integral ambiguity:** “Path-integral simulations of physical processes” could mean quantum amplitudes, lattice
  field theory, stochastic Feynman–Kac expectations, or classical trajectories; these have very different sign,
  preparation, and conditioning problems.
- **Finance caveat:** Markovian path generation and payoff arithmetic can be reversible, but calibration, loading
  distributions, discretization bias, tail truncation, and Greeks/risk outputs matter.
- **Partition-function caveat:** General partition-function estimation is computationally hard; random-walk encodings
  require mixing/gap/temperature assumptions. The sentence must not imply generic efficient quantum estimation.
- **“Genuine”:** A theorem-level query separation under coherent-oracle assumptions can be genuine while practical
  wall-clock advantage remains absent. Distinguish theoretical from end-to-end advantage.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Concrete applications create interest, but the breadth feels like a highlight reel.

#### §16.8 — option-pricing headline numbers

- **Apples-to-oranges comparison:** `$10^7$` Toffolis and `$10^{10}$` classical samples are different resource units.
  Report classical operations/runtime, quantum logical qubits/depth/failure budget, and physical runtime under a common
  target error and hardware assumption.
- **Source verification:** Confirm that both cited papers actually report these values, for which contracts, confidence,
  error, arithmetic precision, and amplitude-estimation variant. A range compressed across studies can invent a
  benchmark neither paper states.
- **“Real but moderate”:** No real-world advantage follows from fewer abstract quantum gates than classical samples,
  particularly when each logical Toffoli is expensive. Call it a prospective query/resource tradeoff unless a full
  crossover analysis exists.
- **Magic-state detail:** Toffoli/CCZ factories are often optimized directly; describing only T-state manufacture may
  be too narrow. Chapter 19’s precise factory model should match this wording.
- **Missing costs:** State preparation, payoff evaluation, controlled inverse calls, logical error correction, and
  repetitions/confidence must be included.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Specific orders of magnitude are gripping, which makes exact comparability especially
  important.

#### §16.8 — linear differential equations

- **Scope:** Quantum linear-system methods generally prepare a quantum state encoding a discretized trajectory or
  solution; they do not output all components/time steps classically. State the requested observable/output model.
- **Strong complexity compression:** `$O(\kappa\log(1/\epsilon))$` omits dimension/access, sparsity, evolution time,
  discretization order, stability, state preparation, normalization, success amplitude, and oracle/gate costs. It is
  not a generic end-to-end ODE complexity.
- **Affine term:** Handling `$b$` typically requires augmentation and an oracle/state preparation for it; this is not
  free.
- **Discretization conditioning:** `$\kappa$` belongs to a particular embedded linear system and may grow with time
  resolution or dynamics. Readers need the relationship to the original ODE.
- **Spelling consistency:** The chapter uses British “qubitisation” here after mostly American “qubitization”; choose
  one house style.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The linear-system connection is elegant but races through the conditions that decide whether
  it helps.

#### §16.8 — nonlinear ODEs and Carleman linearisation

- **Core explanation:** Describing the infinite hierarchy of polynomial observables and finite-degree truncation is a
  good high-level account.
- **Technical precision:** Carleman linearization normally lifts monomials of the state into an infinite linear system;
  “Hilbert space of polynomial functions” may suggest a quantum Hilbert space prematurely. Define the lifted vector.
- **Parameter definition missing:** `$R$` is called a dissipativity parameter but never defined. Its units,
  normalization, dependence on initial condition/nonlinearity, and theorem-specific threshold are essential.
- **Universal-threshold concern:** “Efficient only for `$R<1$`” is tied to a particular algorithm/theorem, not all
  Carleman or nonlinear quantum methods. Cite the exact definition and distinguish sufficient from necessary
  conditions.
- **Complexity omissions:** Truncation degree, time horizon, solution norm, sparsity/oracles, condition number, success
  probability, and observable extraction are absent.
- **Hamiltonian/PDE leap:** A nonlinear ODE result does not automatically classify nonlinear PDE algorithms; spatial
  discretization and conservation/stability add new parameters. “Degrades sharply” and “no general-purpose algorithm”
  need scoped citations.
- **Fluid-headline judgment:** “Most often quoted,” “marginal regime,” and “do not suggest near-term advantage” are
  literature-review conclusions demanding a documented corpus and current resource estimates.
- **Author citation:** Verify the seven-author ordering, year, and exact result; a bibliography key is preferable to a
  long inline list.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The lift-and-truncate idea is fascinating, and the dissipativity catch supplies real
  intellectual tension.

#### §16.8 — Tang and the dequantisation wave

- **Historical importance:** Tang’s result is correctly highlighted as a turning point in evaluating data-access
  assumptions.
- **Strong equivalence overclaim:** The 2018 recommendation algorithm achieved polylogarithmic dependence on dimension
  under sample-and-query access, but saying it had “the same `$\mathrm{poly}\log N$` scaling” hides very large
  polynomial differences in rank, condition/precision, and recommendation parameters. Compare complete bounds.
- **“Same assumptions” nuance:** The classical model was inspired by and classically analogous to quantum state-
  preparation/data-structure assumptions, but whether it is literally the same access depends on how the original
  QRAM/state oracle was costed.
- **Follow-up list:** PCA, clustering, regression, and kernel-method dequantisations vary in guarantees and oracle
  models. Cite representative papers separately instead of presenting a uniform wave.
- **Intent attribution:** “Did not exploit” and “implicitly assumed” are rhetorically sharp; describe the formal model
  mismatch without speculating about authors’ awareness.
- **Stable-rank claim:** Not every classical follow-up has polynomial stable-rank dependence, nor does every quantum
  counterpart share the same hidden factor. Replace the universal statement with an example table including rank,
  condition number, precision, and access costs.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** This is a genuinely dramatic scientific reversal and one of the book’s strongest narrative
  threads.

#### §16.8 — claimed surviving exponential speedups

- **List cannot be exhaustive:** “The surviving exponential speedups are” is far too definitive and mixes proven
  algorithms, oracle separations, conjectural sampling advantages, and application-specific claims. Say “prominent
  candidate families include.”
- **Hamiltonian simulation qualification:** Exponential advantage depends on succinct quantum input, evolution time,
  requested local/sample output, and classical hardness assumptions; not every Hamiltonian or observable is hard.
- **HSP wording:** Shor factoring/order finding and discrete logarithm arise from abelian-HSP techniques, but “over any
  classical algorithm” is not a proved unconditional lower bound. They outperform the best known classical algorithms;
  polynomial classical algorithms have not been ruled out.
- **Sampling status:** Boson/random-circuit sampling advantages rely on specific complexity and noise/approximation
  conjectures; experimental “supremacy” claims also depend on verification and evolving classical simulators.
- **Strong HHL dichotomy defect:** “HHL on a classical input read in via QRAM is dequantised” is not a general theorem.
  Dequantisation applies to particular low-rank/well-conditioned tasks, access models, outputs, and error regimes, often
  with worse polynomial factors. Fully quantum pipelines can also lose advantage through conditioning or readout.
- **Excellent principle:** Input access is indeed part of the problem statement. The absolute final clause is
  rhetorically effective; add output/error/comparator assumptions as equally essential.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The stakes are high and the taxonomy memorable, but the certainty exceeds current complexity
  knowledge.

#### §16.8 — classical-shadows callback

- **Category error:** Classical shadows are not simply “a classical estimator informed by quantum information theory.”
  Standard protocols require randomized quantum measurements on copies of a quantum state followed by classical
  reconstruction; the full method is hybrid.
- **“Quantum-inspired” misuse:** Tang-style quantum-inspired algorithms are classical algorithms borrowing quantum
  ideas/access structures. Classical shadows are ordinarily a quantum measurement protocol, so putting them in the
  same category obscures rather than clarifies.
- **Universal pipeline claim:** It is false that “any modern QML or VQE pipeline uses” classical shadows. Many use
  commuting-group measurement, direct sampling, derandomized shadows, adaptive allocation, or other estimators.
- **Not necessarily post-processing only:** Measurement ensemble/circuit design is an essential quantum layer, not just
  classical post-processing.
- **Useful contrast salvage:** The remove-versus-combine distinction could work if rephrased: dequantisation replaces a
  quantum algorithm with a classical one under comparable access, whereas shadow protocols reduce measurements within
  a quantum experiment.
- **Cross-reference:** Verify §11.5 uses compatible terminology and does not repeat the same category error.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The attempted conceptual callback is welcome, but specialists will resist the classification.

#### §16.8 — resource-aware framing

- **Resource list incomplete:** T/Toffoli/CCZ consumption, logical depth, and workspace are important, but physical
  qubits, code distance, Clifford/measurement cycles, routing, feed-forward latency, factory throughput/footprint,
  connectivity, and total failure budget can dominate.
- **Logical versus physical:** Ancilla count sets logical workspace, not directly the physical-qubit footprint; each
  logical qubit’s physical cost depends on code and distance.
- **Tradeability:** The statement that resources are not freely interchangeable is excellent. A Pareto-frontier diagram
  would be more useful than a three-item declaration.
- **Architecture scope:** Surface-code emphasis should be labeled as one leading architecture, not the universal fault-
  tolerant setting.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The shift to engineering gives the chapter weight and practical texture.

#### §16.8 — T-count bullet

- **Metric mismatch:** A Toffoli is not a T gate. Reporting Toffoli count as though it were T-count requires an assumed
  decomposition or direct CCZ/Toffoli-state factory and correction protocol.
- **Surface-code generalization:** Magic-state factories often dominate non-Clifford cost, but data-block physical
  qubits, routing, reaction time, and Clifford cycles may dominate particular architectures/circuits.
- **RSA number verification:** The quoted “`$3\times10^9$` Toffolis for RSA-2048” is a highly time-sensitive estimate;
  identify the paper, year, circuit assumptions, and whether it counts Toffolis, Toffoli depth, or magic states.
- **Factory conclusion:** Lower non-Clifford count can reduce factory volume/time, but factory number is chosen jointly
  with depth and throughput; “cheaper factories” is imprecise.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The RSA-scale number commands attention, provided it is sourced and compared responsibly.

#### §16.8 — logical-depth bullet

- **Correct core:** Once non-Clifford supply and classical reaction latency are adequate, dependency depth constrains
  wall-clock runtime.
- **Terminology:** Specify whether logical depth counts all logical operations, measurement layers, or only Toffoli/T
  depth.
- **Conditional comparison:** With the same total T-count, a shallower circuit finishes faster only if hardware,
  routing, workspace, and factory throughput support the parallelism; the sentence notes magic states but not the other
  constraints.
- **Error-budget effect:** More parallel operations can change required code distance only through total spacetime/error
  accounting; include spacetime volume as a useful combined metric.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The factory-throughput picture is intuitive and practical.

#### §16.8 — ancilla-count bullet

- **Gross undercount:** LCU’s selector uses `$O(\log L)$` index qubits, but PREPARE/SELECT implementations can require
  coefficient, QROM, alias-sampling, arithmetic, routing, dirty-workspace, and phase-gradient registers far larger than
  that selector.
- **Sparse encoding undercount:** Sparse oracles require row/column/value/arithmetic work registers, not merely an extra
  `$O(\log s)$`.
- **Qubitization claim:** It may reuse signal/block-encoding registers, but the statement that it adds only one QSP
  qubit is convention- and implementation-dependent; phase rotations can sometimes use projectors directly, while
  controlled operations and synthesis need other workspace.
- **Trade generalization:** Reducing ancillas can raise count/depth, but sometimes algebraic optimization reduces all
  three. Say “often trades against” rather than “usually increases.”
- **Architecture dependence:** Clean versus dirty ancillas, mid-circuit reset, factory workspace, and routing make raw
  ancilla count an incomplete footprint metric.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The trade-space intuition is useful, though the asymptotic register counts badly understate
  real layouts.

#### §16.8 — 2026 alpha rule of thumb

- **Regime limitation:** A tenfold decrease in `$\alpha$` yields roughly a tenfold query saving only when the
  `$\alpha t$` term dominates polynomial degree. Precision/filter-degree terms can dominate.
- **Not automatically T-count:** Changing the representation to lower `$\alpha$` can make PREPARE/SELECT more
  expensive; T-count is roughly degree times per-query cost, not degree alone.
- **Error-form inconsistency:** The formula again uses `$\alpha t+\log(1/\epsilon)$`, conflicting with the earlier
  claimed optimal `$\log/\log\log$` dependence. Resolve this globally.
- **Phase-estimation cost:** A lower simulation normalization helps controlled evolution, but total phase-estimation
  cost also depends on target energy precision, overlap/state preparation, repetition, and block-oracle cost.
- **Chemistry chronology:** “Last five years” relative to 2026 and the spelling/focus on tensor hypercontraction/double
  factorization require dated citations. These techniques can reduce lambda/normalization but also alter circuit cost.
- **Rule presentation:** Replace the universal rule with
  `$C\approx d(\alpha t,\epsilon)\,C_{\rm query}$` and discuss when holding `$C_{\rm query}$` fixed is reasonable.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** A quantitative rule is exactly what readers want; qualifying its regime would preserve its
  usefulness.

#### §16.8 — closing representation thesis

- **Strong synthesis:** Representation, approximation, and resource accounting form an excellent three-part summary of
  the modern design mindset.
- **“Less about new algorithms”:** This is an editorial thesis rather than a fact and arguably understates new
  primitives, interaction-picture methods, variable-time algorithms, error-aware compilation, and application
  algorithms.
- **Approximation example:** QSVT-friendly polynomials are not always Chebyshev expansions, and parity/boundedness/gap
  constraints deserve a reminder.
- **Resource example:** Modern accounting needs physical spacetime/error budgets as well as T-count and logical depth.
- **QSVT centrality:** It is a major common language for block-encoded transformations, not the sole language in which
  modern optimizations are compared.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** This is a crisp, thoughtful ending that successfully changes how a reader may categorize
  algorithmic progress.

#### §16.9 — recap of the toolkit

- **LCU relationship error:** Block encodings package operators; LCU is one way to construct them. LCU does not
  generally “turn those packages into quantum walks”—qubitization does, under the signal conditions already noted.
- **Over-unification:** Hamiltonian simulation and matrix inversion are natural QSVT transformations, but amplitude
  amplification and phase estimation are also independent foundational algorithms and need not be presented as
  nothing but QSVT examples. Quantum Monte Carlo uses amplitude estimation and coherent expectation encoding; calling
  it QSVT applied to a block encoding elides most of the algorithm.
- **“Optimal” qualifier:** QSP/QSVT query optimality is target- and oracle-model-specific, not a blanket property of
  every polynomial transformation.
- **Chapter dependency:** Referring back to Chapters 13–15 is helpful, but verify that those chapters actually define
  every assumed primitive and use consistent notation.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The recap is satisfying but presses the grand-unification thesis beyond its clean domain.

#### §16.9 — complexity-theory preview

- **Complexity phrasing:** Chapter 17 can discuss known containments and complete problems, but many natural problems
  are neither “provably hard” nor “provably easy.” The binary preview should acknowledge open status and conditional
  hardness.
- **Class relationships:** Listing the classes creates anticipation; include oracle/promise-problem nuances if the next
  chapter uses them.
- **QSVT in membership proofs:** QSVT can construct circuits used in BQP algorithms, but it is not generally “the
  constructive side” of BQP membership proofs. Many BQP circuits have no useful block-matrix/QSVT formulation.
- **Strong final-claim defect:** A polylogarithmic-time quantum algorithm with an explicitly classical input cannot even
  read a general length-`$N$` input without a strong random-access/data-structure oracle. The issue is not that its
  advantage is formally “weaker”; its problem definition and preprocessing cost are different. State this rather than
  promising a dubious formal theorem.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The upward zoom from algorithms to classes is a natural and motivating handoff.

#### §16.9 — sanity check 1

- **Calculation:** The natural three-Pauli decomposition has coefficients all one, so `$\alpha=3$`; the unconditioned
  success probability on `$|\psi\rangle$` is `$\|H|\psi\rangle\|^2/9$`.
- **Input dependence:** The check should explicitly supply or ask the learner to choose `$|\psi\rangle$`; otherwise no
  numerical success probability exists.
- **Notation ambiguity:** `$X_1X_2+Y_1Y_2+Z_1Z_2$` is recognizable, but define subscripts as qubit locations and note
  the operator’s norm/degenerate action.
- **Useful extension:** Ask for eigenstate cases, including whether any state lies in the kernel, to expose why
  normalization and state-dependent success matter.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Short and concrete, with a good connection to the central cost parameter.

#### §16.9 — sanity check 2

- **Strong invalid premise:** For a generic exact block encoding `$U_A$`, `$W=(R\otimes I)U_A$` need not have the claimed
  invariant subspaces/eigenvalues. The exercise repeats the main construction defect and could teach a false theorem.
- **Normalization inconsistency:** The chapter’s setup uses a `$(1,a,0)$` encoding of a norm-at-most-one signal, while
  this check starts with an `$(\alpha,a,0)$` encoding and inserts `$\lambda/\alpha$`; the walk theorem must be stated
  consistently.
- **Underdetermined matrix:** Knowing `$A$`, `$\alpha$`, and `$a=1$` does not fix the unconstrained blocks of `$U_A$`,
  so the learner cannot write `$W$` explicitly. Supply a concrete standard-form/involutory encoding.
- **Endpoint cases:** Include `$\lambda/\alpha=\pm1$`, where the two-dimensional residual basis degenerates.
- **Entertainment: 2/5.** As written it is frustratingly impossible; with a concrete 2×2 signal oracle it could become
  an excellent derivation.

#### §16.9 — sanity check 3

- **Identity:** `$|x|^2+(1-x^2)|1|^2=1$` verifies the displayed norm condition on `[-1,1]`.
- **Phase underdetermination:** The corresponding phase sequence depends on global-phase, signal, rotation, and product-
  ordering conventions. The chapter must give the exact 2×2 QSP matrix before expecting a unique answer.
- **Other conditions:** The exercise tests normalization but should also ask learners to verify degree and parity.
- **Simple answer opportunity:** Under an explicitly chosen convention this is essentially the bare degree-one signal,
  making the example ideal for anchoring all later factors of `$i$` and signs.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** A satisfying one-line identity can provide relief after the abstract theorem.

#### §16.9 — sanity check 4

- **Underdetermined comparison:** No winner can be identified from `$L,t,\epsilon$` alone. Trotter depends on
  commutator/nested-commutator structure and term-exponential cost; qubitization depends on `$\alpha$`, block-encoding
  query cost, reflection/control cost, and synthesis model.
- **Gate-versus-query mismatch:** The prose has mostly supplied query complexity for qubitization but asks for
  asymptotic gate count. This comparison is invalid until SELECT/PREPARE implementation costs are specified.
- **Second-order expression:** Preserve the nested-commutator coefficient rather than using the normalized
  `$t^{3/2}/\sqrt\epsilon$` shorthand.
- **Better exercise:** Supply a commuting Pauli family and a noncommuting nearest-neighbor family plus a concrete LCU
  circuit, then ask for symbolic crossover conditions.
- **Entertainment: 3/5.** The comparison is valuable in principle but currently invites hand-waving rather than
  calculation.

#### §16.9 — sanity check 5

- **External-dependency issue:** “Read the abstract” requires internet/library access, provides no stable link or
  bibliography shortlist, and makes the check non-self-contained.
- **Abstract insufficiency:** An abstract may not specify the full sample-and-query oracle or whether the original
  quantum paper made it explicit. Those questions often require theorem/input-model sections and the earlier paper.
- **Good research skill:** Comparing paired quantum/dequantized papers is an excellent advanced exercise if sources are
  bundled and learners are told to quote theorem assumptions rather than infer intent.
- **Date boundary:** The 2018–2020 restriction is sensible historically but should name two or three eligible papers.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** It invites genuine detective work and critical reading, unusually strong for a chapter-end
  exercise.

#### §16.9 — separator and footer navigation

- **Formatting:** The horizontal rule cleanly separates body and navigation.
- **Navigation:** Previous, contents, and next targets are consistent with the opening navigation.
- **Missing references:** The chapter contains many named theorems, papers, contemporary benchmarks, and quotations but
  no visible references subsection. Ensure the project-wide bibliography resolves every attribution.
- **Entertainment: 3/5.** Functional and unobtrusive.

#### Chapter 16 overall verdict

- **Overall:** The dependency chain from LCU through block encodings to qubitization, QSP, and QSVT is ambitious,
  exciting, and often conceptually excellent. Its recurring “prepare–select–unprepare,” spectral-lift, and polynomial-
  design explanations could make a difficult research toolkit unusually approachable. The chapter is not yet reliable
  as a technical reference because it repeatedly turns model-specific query results into universal gate/runtime
  claims and pushes the QSVT-unifies-everything thesis beyond its precise domain.
- **Strong-defect shortlist:** Jordan–Wigner is incorrectly said to make fermionic systems 4-local on qubits; the
  example observable/correlation formula is malformed; generic block encodings do not support the stated simple
  `$RU_H$` qubitization walk; the conditioned LCU output is misnormalized; Taylor simulation omits normalization and
  segmentation; optimal precision formulas contradict one another; raw reciprocal `$1/x$` violates QSP boundedness;
  Hermitian QSVT loses sign subtleties and hermitisation is wrongly said to double normalization; QPE and Monte Carlo
  are overclaimed as mere QSVT examples; classical shadows are misclassified; blanket HHL dequantisation is false; and
  sanity check 2 is underdetermined/invalid for a generic block encoding.
- **Recurring slight concerns:** Block-oracle, state-preparation, output, confidence, normalization, and gate-synthesis
  assumptions disappear from headline complexities; query and gate/T counts are mixed; fixed-order Trotter is treated
  as all product formulas; empirical 2026 superlatives lack primary sources; conditioned and unnormalized states are
  conflated; QSP conventions omit signs/phases; non-Hermitian parity/projector details are compressed; and NISQ versus
  fault-tolerant recommendations are presented as a false binary.
- **Improvement priority:** First repair the generic qubitization construction and adopt one exact QSP/QSVT convention.
  Then create a compact table listing, for every headline algorithm, input oracle, normalization, target output,
  error/confidence, query bound, per-query gate cost, and post-selection/amplification cost. Finally replace contemporary
  rankings and universal dequantisation claims with dated, sourced case studies.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The chapter has exceptional intellectual momentum and repeatedly delivers the pleasure of
  seeing separate algorithms collapse into a common language. Precision repairs would make that excitement durable
  rather than fragile.
- **Second-pass status:** Complete for this manuscript file; the opening, all nine numbered sections, each displayed
  construction and complexity claim, application/resource subsection, five sanity checks, and footer were read and
  reviewed separately.

### `book/part-07-complexity/17-complexity-theory.md` — second pass

#### Chapter title, status, navigation, and opening purpose

- **Title:** “Complexity Theory Around Quantum Computing” accurately signals a practitioner-facing perimeter rather
  than a complete complexity course.
- **Workflow metadata:** As in prior chapters, “prereviewed,” “Phase: 3,” and “14 / 14” need a defined publication
  purpose or build-time removal.
- **Navigation:** Previous, contents, and next links are structurally coherent.
- **Part reference:** “Part 6 catalogued algorithms” is plausible from the directory structure, but Chapter 16 says
  Chapter 15 closed a sequence; verify published part numbering and wording.
- **Impossibility overpromise:** Complexity theory rarely tells a practitioner that an algorithmic shape is
  unconditionally impossible. It supplies reductions, lower bounds in restricted models, completeness evidence, and
  conditional/oracle barriers. Soften “tells you ... whether ... even possible.”
- **Excellent resource warning:** The immediate insistence on precise resources and access models correctly continues
  Chapter 16’s most important lesson.
- **Audience/scope:** The practitioner vocabulary goal is clear and sensible, but “quantum-software practitioner” spans
  application programmers, compiler engineers, and theorists. Identify expected mathematical maturity.
- **Reference accessibility:** Arora–Barak and Watrous are strong recommendations; give full bibliography entries,
  distinguish a paid textbook from freely available notes, and specify relevant chapters.
- **“Survives” rhetoric:** Saying the chapter is what remains after rigorous study sounds authoritative and personal.
  “A compact working subset” is less dismissive of details the chapter necessarily omits.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The zoom outward creates fresh stakes and promises useful skepticism.

#### How-to-read guide

- **Navigation value:** Classifying sections as recap, spine, lookup table, claim-evaluation guide, and engineering
  bridge is exceptionally useful.
- **Prerequisite mismatch:** §17.1 allegedly recaps PSPACE, but its title omits PSPACE/coNP/EXP; either retitle it or
  make the guide list match.
- **Lookup-table promise:** §§17.4–17.5 contain proofs/relationships as well as definitions; “read once” is reasonable,
  while “lookup table” suggests a summary table that should actually be provided.
- **Supremacy terminology:** The guide promises help parsing “quantum supremacy” and “advantage”; ensure §17.8 defines
  historical/industry usage and explains why many authors now prefer “advantage.”
- **Engineering bridge:** Gate count, T-count, and simulability are relevant, though dequantisation is conceptual and
  access-model-driven as much as engineering.
- **Spelling consistency:** The file uses British “dequantisation,” matching some prior prose but not “qubitization”;
  establish whether technical coinages follow source spelling or house style.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** It makes a dense chapter feel navigable before any notation arrives.

#### §17.1 — class framing and P/NP/coNP

- **Technical terminology:** Complexity classes are sets of languages/decision problems, often identified with Boolean
  characteristic functions; calling them directly “functions” blurs language and function-class conventions that
  §17.2 later tries to distinguish.
- **Encoding/input length:** Mention that complexity is measured against a finite encoding and its bit length. Numeric
  magnitudes versus binary/unary encoding will matter for optimization examples.
- **P definition:** Correct at this level, assuming a standard deterministic multitape/single-tape model whose
  polynomial differences do not matter.
- **NP definition:** Correct. Add the polynomial-time verifier formula
  `$x\in L\iff\exists w,\ |w|\leq\mathrm{poly}(|x|):V(x,w)=1$` to anchor witness language.
- **Nondeterminism:** “Solvable” means acceptance on at least one computation branch for yes-instances and no accepting
  branch for no-instances; newcomers often mistake it for random guessing.
- **coNP wording:** Correct, but a short TAUT or primality-style example would stop readers from assuming coNP means
  “problems not in NP.”
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Familiar material is compressed well without becoming lifeless.

#### §17.1 — BPP, PSPACE, and EXP

- **BPP:** The every-input bounded-error definition and conventional `$1/3$` threshold are correct.
- **Amplification condition:** Majority voting assumes independent runs and Boolean decisions; Chernoff bounds yield
  `$e^{-\Omega(k)}$`. State that any constant gap above one half is equivalent.
- **PSPACE wording:** “Regardless of time” can suggest non-halting computations count. A polynomial-space decider must
  halt; it can be assumed to run at most exponential time after removing repeated configurations.
- **Savitch nuance:** Savitch gives
  `$\mathrm{NSPACE}(s)\subseteq\mathrm{DSPACE}(s^2)$` for suitable `$s$`, from which NPSPACE=PSPACE follows. Naming the
  theorem is good; a phrase about quadratic-space simulation would explain it.
- **EXP convention:** Specify deterministic exponential time and whether EXP means
  `$\bigcup_k\mathrm{TIME}(2^{n^k})$`; `$2^{\mathrm{poly}(n)}$` communicates this adequately but can include notation
  ambiguity.
- **Count:** The text correctly supplies six classes, although the heading names only four.
- **Entertainment: 3/5.** Efficient and serviceable; PSPACE’s surprising resistance to nondeterminism is the liveliest
  point.

#### §17.1 — containments, separations, and beliefs

- **Displayed chains:** The containments shown are correct but omit coNP after introducing it. Add
  `$\mathrm{P}\subseteq\mathrm{NP}\cap\mathrm{coNP}\subseteq\mathrm{PSPACE}$` or a diagram.
- **Strictness wording:** P⊊EXP is the only strict separation *among the adjacent/major classes displayed here* known
  from the deterministic time hierarchy. Complexity theory has many other hierarchy separations, so “the only strict
  separation proved” is too global literally.
- **BPP placement:** Sipser–Gács–Lautemann indeed places BPP in
  `$\Sigma_2^p\cap\Pi_2^p$`; calling it the “strongest known placement” may overlook alternative incomparable
  containments/low-for-self consequences. Say it is the standard PH upper bound.
- **“Just above NP”:** `$\Sigma_2^p$` contains NP, while `$\Pi_2^p$` contains coNP; neither should be colloquially
  collapsed into a single layer “just above NP” without explaining alternating quantifiers.
- **Open equalities:** The examples are correct. It may be more illuminating to note that NP=PSPACE would collapse a
  vast region of the hierarchy.
- **Belief calibration:** P≠NP is overwhelmingly standard; P=BPP is a common derandomization belief conditional on
  suitable circuit lower bounds/hardness assumptions, but “standard belief” should be sourced rather than attributed
  to failed attacks.
- **Evidence wording:** Oracle separations can both support and limit intuition; they are not direct evidence for
  unrelativized equality. “Three decades” is also dated and now understates the P-versus-NP history.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The mixture of known map and blank territory gives the class zoo genuine tension.

#### §17.1 — reductions and completeness

- **Reduction type:** The definition is a polynomial-time many-one/Karp reduction, not every notion written
  `$\leq_p$`. Name it explicitly because Turing, randomized, approximation-preserving, and quantum reductions differ.
- **Hardness scope:** Usually every language in `$\mathcal C$` reduces under the chosen reduction type; correct once
  that type is fixed.
- **Completeness intuition:** “Localises” is elegant and accurate as a metaphor.
- **QBF name:** Use “true quantified Boolean formula” or TQBF/QSAT carefully; validity of a fully quantified Boolean
  formula is PSPACE-complete. Capitalize Boolean consistently.
- **Natural NP examples:** 3-colouring, Hamiltonian cycle, and vertex cover require decision formulations. Integer
  programming feasibility is NP-complete under standard finite binary-encoded rational/integer constraints; state the
  formulation to avoid numerical-model ambiguity.
- **Reduction provenance:** Not every listed natural problem is most naturally shown by a direct reduction “from SAT”;
  completeness via chains is still ultimately transitive. Reword to “via polynomial reductions.”
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Familiar examples make abstract hardness operational.

#### §17.1 — reductionist reflex

- **Excellent instinct:** Seeking reductions in both directions is exactly the productive habit to teach.
- **Direction clarity:** A known-hard problem reducing *to the new problem* gives a lower-bound/hardness result; the new
  problem reducing *to a known solvable/complete problem* gives an upper bound. Spell this out because beginners often
  reverse the arrows.
- **Limits:** Reductions can be too lossy for fine-grained, parameterized, approximation, or practical-runtime
  conclusions. Add one warning that NP-hardness alone says little about typical instances.
- **Decision versus optimization:** Practitioners often meet optimization/search tasks; explain that complexity claims
  usually pass through associated decision versions and reductions.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** This is concise, actionable, and likely to change how readers approach unfamiliar problems.

#### §17.2 — BQP definition and uniformity

- **Definition:** The bounded-error uniform circuit-family definition is standard, with an implicit final computational-
  basis measurement/accept bit.
- **Uniformity precision:** The circuit description should be generated from `$1^n$` in polynomial time (often logspace
  uniformity is also used), but the circuit must still receive `$x$` as input. “Computed from `$n$`” is broadly right.
- **Gate entries:** Uniformity also prevents arbitrary uncomputable real amplitudes in gates; fixing an efficiently
  specifiable finite gate set handles this.
- **Advice wording:** A nonuniform polynomial-size family corresponds to BQP/poly-style classical advice rather than
  literally “unbounded advice”; the advice length remains polynomial but may encode undecidable/nonuniform
  information across lengths. Replace “unbounded” with “nonuniform polynomial advice.”
- **Promise problems:** Much quantum complexity, including approximation/physics complete problems, is naturally
  promise-problem based. Restricting entirely to decision languages should be acknowledged now.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The anti-smuggling explanation makes uniformity memorable.

#### §17.2 — gate-set invariance

- **Strong gate-set nuance:** `$\{H,\mathrm{CCNOT}\}$` generates real-amplitude computation, not arbitrary complex
  single-qubit unitaries on the same space. It captures BQP only through the “one extra rebbit” simulation of complex
  amplitudes; explain that qualification rather than listing it as straightforwardly universal.
- **Circular wording:** “Any other fixed universal set efficiently approximable by reasonable gates” is vague. Require
  a finite, efficiently specifiable, approximately universal gate set with inverses or an appropriate compilation
  theorem.
- **Solovay–Kitaev scope:** The theorem applies to suitable finite inverse-closed dense generating sets and has
  polylogarithmic length; modern number-theoretic synthesis gives sharper bounds for Clifford+T. It is not simply a
  theorem about “any universal set.”
- **Precision budgeting:** To preserve bounded error for a polynomial-size circuit, compile each gate to inverse-
  polynomially small error; polylog overhead then remains polynomial. Showing this one line would prove the class
  invariance claim.
- **Cross-reference:** Verify §8.11 states the same assumptions and exponent `$c$`.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The practical message is reassuring, though the real-gate exception is subtle and
  interesting enough to explain.

#### §17.2 — amplification and function problems

- **Amplification:** Independent repetition and majority vote correctly reduce BQP decision error exponentially with
  linear repetition overhead; intermediate measurements can be deferred if the circuit model requires it.
- **Coherent alternatives:** Majority voting is sufficient for class definitions; amplitude amplification is not
  needed and would require additional reversibility/access assumptions.
- **FBQP terminology:** FBQP is used for polynomially bounded classical outputs with bounded error, but conventions vary
  and relational/search classes may be more natural. “Sometimes called” appropriately signals this.
- **Shor nuance:** Shor’s factoring routine solves a relation/function problem; a BQP decision formulation can ask for
  bits/properties or use reductions. This is a good example.
- **Output size:** No polynomial-time function class can print exponentially many bits; say outputs are polynomially
  bounded.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The decision/function distinction is compact and genuinely useful.

#### §17.2 — unconditional containment chains

- **Chains:** BPP⊆BQP⊆AWPP⊆PP⊆PSPACE is the clearest combined presentation; showing PP then separately AWPP makes the
  “tightening” slightly harder to visualize.
- **BPP simulation:** Hadamards generate fair quantum coins and reversible classical gates simulate the computation;
  uncomputing garbage or measuring random bits can make the construction precise.
- **Path-sum explanation:** Polynomial-space enumeration of exponentially many paths is a good intuition, but exact
  comparison of amplitudes/probabilities requires an exactly representable/algebraic gate set or controlled
  approximation. State the fixed gate-set assumption.
- **PP definition:** The gapless threshold characterization is essentially correct. Formally PP uses a probabilistic
  polynomial-time Turing machine and strict majority acceptance; arbitrary biased real coins are not implied.
- **Boundary convention:** Yes `>1/2`, no `≤1/2` is one valid convention; complement closure makes boundary choices
  manageable.
- **AWPP claim:** It is a tighter standard counting-class upper bound than PP, but “the tightest known cap” is a broad
  current-status superlative and may ignore incomparable upper bounds. Say “a notable tighter upper bound used here.”
- **AWPP role:** If the class appears nowhere materially later, naming it without defining its gap-function character
  may add zoo burden rather than practitioner value.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The sum-over-paths argument makes the surprising classical upper bound intuitive.

#### §17.2 — relationship with NP

- **Unknown containments:** Correct and important.
- **Oracle result:** State precisely that Bennett–Bernstein–Brassard–Vazirani established an oracle (and, if intended,
  probability-one random-oracle statement) relative to which `$\mathrm{NP}^A\nsubseteq\mathrm{BQP}^A$`. Such a result
  rules out relativizing proofs, not unconditionally NP⊄BQP.
- **Grover inference:** The black-box lower bound blocks a polynomial quantum speedup from treating SAT as unstructured
  search over assignments. It does not prove that structured NP-hard problems have no polynomial quantum algorithms.
  The text mostly says this, but “do not admit” should remain explicitly oracle-model qualified.
- **Strong factoring defect:** Factoring is a function/search problem, so saying “factoring is in
  `$\mathrm{NP}\cap\mathrm{coNP}$`” is imprecise. Associated decision problems such as compositeness have certificates;
  “the factorisation itself is a witness either way” is false for a no-instance/primality unless a primality certificate
  or primality algorithm is invoked.
- **NP-completeness consequence:** If an appropriate factoring decision problem in NP∩coNP were NP-complete under
