  access model; possible polynomial advantages and different preprocessing costs did not necessarily disappear.
- **Biographical aside:** Tang being an undergraduate is engaging and relevant history, but source it and ensure it does
  not substitute for explaining the theorem.
- **Intent:** Say the original comparison did not grant the classical method equivalent structured access, rather than
  implying it intentionally chose an unfair baseline.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The undergrad detail and hidden-access discovery make this a superb scientific story.

#### §17.12 — programme and example list

- **Overbroad collective claim:** Not one Tang “framework” dequantised every listed task; multiple quantum-inspired
  algorithms with different length-square sampling, low-rank, condition, and output assumptions form the programme.
- **Linear systems:** “Matching HHL on low-rank instances” needs a named algorithm and full parameter comparison.
  Classical algorithms may retain polynomially worse dependence while matching polylog dimension.
- **“Low-rank singular-value transformation”:** Define the classical task/output and cite the quantum-inspired
  singular-value transformation result; this phrase can misleadingly suggest generic QSVT was classically simulated.
- **PCA/recommendation:** These predate quantum computing classically; the result dequantises particular quantum
  algorithms/access-based speedup claims, not the underlying tasks.
- **QML breadth:** “Several” is acceptable only with named examples. Many amplitude-encoding algorithms have no complete
  dequantisation or use fully quantum data.
- **Rank restriction:** Low rank/stable rank and favorable norms are load-bearing assumptions, not minor adjectives.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The breadth is impressive, but a theorem table would be more trustworthy than a victory list.

#### §17.12 — recurring dequantisation pattern

- **Excellent checklist:** Inspect access, equalize the comparator, then seek a sampling/sketching analogue is a durable
  research workflow.
- **Not all cases:** Quantum-inspired methods can exploit weaker, stronger, or differently priced access and may change
  error/output guarantees; “in all cases” is too universal.
- **Preprocessing:** Both quantum and classical access structures may take linear time/memory to build. Amortization over
  many queries must be explicit.
- **Artifact criterion:** Access asymmetry can be only part of the speedup; conditioning, low-rank promises, output
  sampling, or hidden classical preprocessing also contribute.
- **Failure evidence:** Failure to find a dequantisation is not evidence of quantum hardness without lower bounds or
  reductions.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** This is concrete methodological advice rather than retrospective history.

#### §17.12 — claimed dequantisable/non-dequantisable divide

- **Category mistake:** Shor and Hamiltonian simulation are not “quantum-linear-algebra speedups” that survive an
  amplitude-encoding dequantisation programme; they are useful contrasts, but the paragraph changes scope.
- **Epistemic overclaim:** “Has not been dequantised” means no known comparable classical algorithm, not that a result is
  non-dequantisable. The next use of “non-dequantisable” turns absence of discovery into a theorem.
- **Classical-data pattern:** Encoding classical data, applying linear algebra, and returning a small classical sample/
  statistic is indeed a warning sign, but not a sufficient criterion for dequantisation.
- **Readout wording:** Recommendation algorithms often output a sample/recommendation rather than a full classical
  vector; readout cost can still matter without being exponentially large.
- **Hidden parameters:** Quantum papers do not necessarily “hide” Frobenius norm or condition number; sometimes they
  state them but headlines omit them. Audit the cited theorem before imputing concealment.
- **“Intrinsic quantum structure”:** Periodicity can be a property of a classical modular function, and many-body
  dynamics can be classically easy. Intrinsic-versus-encoded is a suggestive heuristic, not a known dividing theorem.
- **Hamiltonian caveat:** Restricted Hamiltonian families are routinely classically simulable; specify the hard
  succinct families/output tasks.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The attempted structural synthesis is provocative, but it must be labeled a heuristic.

#### §17.12 — “honest takeaway”

- **Presumption wording:** A claimed exponential QML speedup should be treated as conditional on its complete model,
  not “provisionally believed.” Skepticism should begin before, not only after, a failed dequantisation attempt.
- **Failure is not validation:** No unsuccessful search can prove absence of a classical algorithm. Positive hardness
  evidence requires reductions, lower bounds in a model, or defensible complexity assumptions.
- **Better checklist:** Demand matched input construction/access, preprocessing amortization, rank/condition/precision,
  output and verification cost, and best modern randomized linear-algebra baseline.
- **Scope:** Quantum-native data and quantum-to-quantum subroutines may evade classical-input objections while retaining
  other bottlenecks.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The skepticism is welcome, but the epistemic rule is backwards.

#### §17.13 — simulability framing

- **Definition needed:** “Classically simulable” may mean strong probability computation, weak sampling, expectation
  estimation, state representation, exact simulation, or approximation. Each family below has different guarantees.
- **Candidate-region logic:** Fewer known simulable families do not prove the complement is hard; absence of a
  simulation theorem is not evidence of advantage.
- **Strong count defect:** Far more than three classes are known: low-depth/limited-treewidth circuits, fermionic
  Gaussian/matchgate systems, bosonic Gaussian optics under suitable measurements, positive-discrete-Wigner models,
  computationally tractable states/gates, stoquastic/restricted circuits, and hybrids. Say “three important families.”
- **Boundary metaphor:** The union of known islands is not a characterized boundary of quantum advantage.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The flip-side framing is excellent despite the false exhaustiveness.

#### §17.13 — stabilizer/Gottesman–Knill definition

- **Circuit scope:** Clifford unitaries, stabilizer-state preparation, Pauli measurements, classical feed-forward, and
  appropriate mixtures can be efficiently simulated. The listed gate set is a useful core but not the full theorem.
- **Measurement adaptivity:** State whether Pauli measurements may be adaptive and how postselection changes
  simulability.
- **Tableau size:** A stabilizer tableau has `$O(n^2)$` bits; runtime depends polynomially on gates and measurement
  updates, as stated.
- **Spelling:** “Stabiliser” is British; theorem is often written Gottesman–Knill. Maintain one house style.
- **Entanglement insight:** GHZ and cluster-state examples correctly demonstrate that entanglement alone is insufficient.
  Note that cluster states become universal with adaptive non-Pauli measurements, pinpointing the missing resource.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Highly entangled yet classically tractable states remain one of the field’s best surprises.

#### §17.13 — magic and simulation cost

- **“Complement” misuse:** Non-Clifford resources extend Clifford computation toward universality, but the set-theoretic
  complement of Clifford circuits is not uniformly “magic” nor automatically hard.
- **Measure terminology:** Robustness/mana and stabilizer extent/rank are related simulation resources, but stabilizer
  rank has subtleties as a monotone. Verify the exact list and definitions.
- **T-count insufficiency:** Simulation cost depends on circuit structure, T depth, cancellations, state rank/extent,
  measurement pattern, approximation error, and chosen simulator—not solely non-Clifford count.
- **“Free”:** Clifford simulation is polynomial, not zero-cost.
- **Single-T claim:** One T per qubit gives linear T-count and generic stabilizer-rank algorithms can scale
  exponentially in that count, but “already beyond reach” depends on qubit number, structure, error, and hardware.
- **Terminology:** Magic is a resource-theory concept relative to a free set; define it instead of relying on quotation-
  mark intuition.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The boundary between Clifford ease and non-Clifford difficulty is compellingly concrete.

#### §17.13 — matchgate circuits

- **Definition absent:** “Specific form” is too vague for a reference chapter. Give the parity-preserving matchgate
  matrix form or define generated quadratic-fermion/Gaussian transformations.
- **Geometry is load-bearing:** Nearest-neighbor matchgates on a path are efficiently simulable under suitable
  inputs/measurements, while allowing SWAP/non-nearest interactions or slight extensions can yield universality.
- **Jordan–Wigner nuance:** Free-fermion quadratic dynamics maps cleanly; boundary conditions, parity, and observables
  affect simulation.
- **“Second main island”:** There is no canonical ranking of simulable families; fermionic Gaussian computation is
  important but not uniquely second.
- **Examples:** Name one concrete free-fermion model/scattering observable and its simulation guarantee.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** A narrowly constrained gate geometry hiding free fermions is fascinating, but readers are
  given no way to recognize a matchgate.

#### §17.13 — MPS/PEPS descriptions

- **Description size:** MPS with bond dimension `$\chi$` have polynomial-size descriptions when `$\chi$` is
  polynomial. PEPS can also have polynomial-size tensor descriptions, but that does not imply efficient contraction.
- **Strong 2D defect:** Exact/approximate contraction of general PEPS can be #P-hard; operations on PEPS are not
  generically polynomial in `$n$` and `$\chi$`. The statement “operations on such states” falsely transfers the 1D MPS
  property to 2D.
- **Entanglement qualifier:** Limited entanglement across relevant cuts can enable tensor-network simulation, but
  network treewidth/geometry and contraction order matter as much as entropy.
- **Dynamic growth:** Even initially low-bond states can develop rapidly growing bond dimension under time evolution.
- **Approximation:** State the truncation error/norm and how it accumulates; polynomial description does not guarantee
  accurate efficient dynamics.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Bond dimension is the right intuition, though the MPS/PEPS merge creates a serious falsehood.

#### §17.13 — area laws and DMRG

- **Strong implication defect:** An area law alone does not universally guarantee polynomial bond dimension or an
  efficiently findable tensor-network representation, especially in 2D. For gapped 1D ground states, rigorous area-law/
  MPS approximation and polynomial-time algorithms exist under specified gap/accuracy assumptions.
- **Boundary scaling:** In 2D an area-law entropy proportional to boundary length can still require bond dimension
  exponential in boundary width for certain cuts.
- **DMRG:** DMRG is extraordinarily successful empirically for many 1D/low-entanglement systems but can encounter local
  minima and lacks a blanket efficient guarantee in its common implementation.
- **VQA comparison:** “Routinely outperform” needs matched Hamiltonians, accuracy, compute/hardware, and citations.
  Classical MPS baselines should indeed be mandatory in 1D VQE studies.
- **2D state of art:** Tensor networks are important for many 2D systems, but contraction/optimization remains hard and
  “gapped, area-law-obeying” does not automatically ensure efficient performance.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The classical-versus-variational comparison is provocative and practically important.

#### §17.13 — claimed escape from all three families

- **Necessary/sufficient logic:** Escaping these three sufficient-simulability conditions is neither known necessary nor
  sufficient for hardness; many other classical algorithms/families remain.
- **Output task:** A circuit may prepare a complex state while the measured statistic remains easy. Advantage attaches
  to the full sampling/estimation task.
- **Sycamore locality:** The hardware used local gates on a 2D grid; “long-range-entangled” may describe correlations,
  not long-range interactions, and needs a metric.
- **Magic-heavy:** Non-Clifford gate density is relevant but simulation hardness also depends on geometry, depth, cuts,
  fidelity, and requested samples.
- **High bond dimension:** Demonstrate rather than assert it, and distinguish exact from approximate/noisy effective
  bond dimension.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The three-lock metaphor is intuitive but formally unreliable.

#### §17.13 — account of classical RCS pushbacks

- **Strong causal suspicion:** Classical tensor-network improvements exploited contraction paths, slicing, sparse
  amplitudes, fidelity tradeoffs, massive parallelism, and circuit geometry. Claiming noise specifically placed the
  state on a lower-bond-dimension manifold needs a cited technical result and may be false for the referenced methods.
- **State-versus-sampling:** Approximate low-fidelity sampling can be assembled without representing a single global
  low-bond-dimension noisy state. Avoid forcing all algorithms into the MPS narrative.
- **“Did not optimise against”:** Google deliberately selected hard circuit layouts and benchmarked classical methods;
  later algorithmic progress does not imply the original team ignored the exploited feature.
- **Next-generation intent:** Experiments respond to classical simulation, but also to hardware fidelity, scale, and
  application goals. The claimed alternating optimization cycle is a narrative hypothesis.
- **Source consistency:** Align this account with §17.8’s specific Sunway/GPU claims after primary-source verification.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The arms-race story is engrossing, but its causal mechanism is oversimplified.

#### §17.13 — moving-frontier conclusion

- **Empirical truth:** Advantage claims are relative to current best-known classical implementations, so the benchmark
  moves.
- **Belief versus theorem:** “Frontier itself is not collapsing” and “genuine advantage is widely believed” are
  community judgments. Define whether advantage means asymptotic, experimental, or useful.
- **Formal statement:** BQP≠BPP is a clean decision-class conjecture, while sampling advantage needs separate classes/
  conjectures and error models. Say which stubbornly open statement is intended.
- **Marketing balance:** The closing aspiration is strong and fair; the chapter itself must meet it by removing
  unsupported “strongest/unrebutted/almost always” rhetoric.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The final cadence captures the iterative nature of scientific benchmarking very well.

#### §17.14 — bridge to noise and error correction

- **Ideal-versus-real transition:** This is the natural point to move from complexity classes to fault tolerance.
- **“What can be done”:** Complexity upper bounds identify asymptotic algorithms under models; they do not fully delimit
  physical possibility, and absence from BQP is rarely proved.
- **Chapter-scope inconsistency:** Earlier Chapter 16 points magic-state distillation to Chapter 19, while this bridge
  says Chapter 18 explains where T gates/magic states/distillation and the surface code come from. Verify actual chapter
  contents and cross-reference the precise sections.
- **T-gate origin:** T gates are logical operations synthesized/injected from magic states in a chosen FT architecture;
  magic states are not literally where the mathematical gate “comes from” in every code.
- **Surface-code claim:** It is a leading candidate, not established singularly; cite dated rationale and acknowledge
  other code families if making architectural recommendations.
- **Resource estimate:** The 2019 tens-of-millions/eight-hour baseline and 2025 refinements need consistent primary
  citations, assumptions, and separation of logical algorithm cost from error-correction/factory overhead.
- **Metaphor:** “Physical lower bound on affordable” is not a formal lower bound; it is an engineering cost estimate.
  Avoid borrowing complexity terminology for budget constraints.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The possible-versus-affordable intersection is an excellent narrative hinge.

#### §17.14 — sanity check 1

- **Chain:** BPP⊆BQP follows by reversible simulation of randomized computation; BQP⊆PSPACE follows by polynomial-space
  path-sum/amplitude computation under a fixed algebraic gate set.
- **Question ambiguity:** Adleman–DeMarrais–Huang proves the tighter BQP⊆PP step, but PP is absent from the requested
  BPP⊆BQP⊆PSPACE chain. Ask learners to insert PP explicitly:
  `$\mathrm{BPP}\subseteq\mathrm{BQP}\subseteq\mathrm{PP}\subseteq\mathrm{PSPACE}$`.
- **“Unconditional step”:** Both displayed steps are unconditional and can be sketched; asking for one is fine.
- **Proof-on-the-spot:** This phrase is conversational but may intimidate; “sketch the simulation” is clearer.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** A compact containment reconstruction is a good retrieval check once PP is restored.

#### §17.14 — sanity check 2

- **Repeated strong defect:** Factoring is a function/search problem, not literally a language in NP∩coNP. Use a
  decision version such as whether an integer has a factor below a threshold, with carefully described yes/no
  certificates, or discuss the factoring relation and associated languages.
- **Witness issue:** A factor certifies compositeness; a “no” answer requires primality/lower-bound certificates or a
  threshold-specific argument, not “the factorisation itself either way.”
- **Non-completeness:** Membership in NP∩coNP plus NP-completeness would imply NP=coNP under standard many-one
  reductions, supporting the belief that the decision problem is not NP-complete.
- **Shor/SAT inference:** Shor solves factoring/order finding, not arbitrary NP-complete problems. Only an NP-completeness
  reduction in the correct direction would yield SAT; non-belief alone is not a theorem that no separate BQP SAT
  algorithm exists.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** It targets a vital misconception but currently repeats the chapter’s category error.

#### §17.14 — sanity check 3

- **Problem statement:** Require succinct bounded local terms and the promise `$E_0\leq a$` versus `$E_0\geq b$` with
  inverse-polynomial gap.
- **VQE implication:** If a uniform polynomial-resource VQE-style quantum/classical procedure solved all promised local-
  Hamiltonian instances at that precision, QMA⊆BQP; since BQP⊆QMA, equality follows.
- **Output nuance:** A heuristic energy estimate without a certified promise decision, or restricted ansatz/system
  family, does not imply the collapse.
- **Optimizer accounting:** Include shots, classical optimization iterations, precision, and success amplification in
  “polynomial-time.”
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** This cleanly connects a formal complete problem to an algorithm practitioners know.

#### §17.14 — sanity check 4

- **Good open response:** The recommendation-system/Tang case directly tests the access-model lesson.
- **Required specificity:** Ask for original and dequantized theorem citations, data structure/oracles, rank/condition/
  precision dependence, preprocessing, and output guarantee—not only a narrative model name.
- **“Same access”:** Encourage learners to identify where classical SQ access is derived from or comparable to quantum
  state preparation rather than asserting identity.
- **Alternative examples:** Provide a shortlist so readers do not repeat unsupported blanket claims about HHL/QML.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** It rewards critical comparison rather than formula recall.

#### §17.14 — sanity check 5

- **Premise overstatement:** T-count can dominate in a specified surface-code non-Clifford-heavy design; it is not
  universally more important than total gates, depth, qubits, or spacetime volume.
- **T/Toffoli:** Require a decomposition/factory convention before converting Toffoli count to T count.
- **Strong “free gates” defect:** No logical gates are physically free. Clifford operations may avoid magic-state
  distillation and be substantially cheaper, but surface-code lattice surgery, measurements, routing, and time remain.
- **Magic state:** Non-Clifford T/CCZ/Toffoli resources often require distillation/injection; name the architecture and
  alternatives.
- **Better question:** Ask learners to compare T-count, T-depth, factory throughput/footprint, data-block qubits, code
  distance, and total spacetime volume for one cited RSA estimate.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Resource tradeoffs are an excellent final check, but “free” bakes in the chapter’s misleading
  simplification.

#### §17.14 — separator and footer

- **Formatting:** The horizontal rule and repeated navigation are clean.
- **Links:** Previous, contents, and next paths match the opening.
- **Missing chapter references:** The density of named theorems, contemporary experiments, and resource figures demands
  a traceable bibliography/reference block; ensure all inline names resolve.
- **Entertainment: 3/5.** Functional and unobtrusive.

#### Chapter 17 overall verdict

- **Overall:** This is an unusually lively practitioner’s tour through a forbidding subject. Its containment chains,
  oracle caveats, speedup taxonomy, input-provenance maxim, and repeated connection to VQE/resource estimates are
  genuinely useful. Its weakness is epistemic overreach: formal theorem, oracle evidence, best-known algorithmic gap,
  experimental benchmark, and community belief repeatedly slide into one another.
- **Strong-defect shortlist:** Factoring is twice miscast as a decision language in NP∩coNP with a factorization witness
  “either way”; BQP-complete probability/dynamics problems omit promises and outputs; generic query separations are
  falsely said to lift cleanly to time; sampling-hardness assumptions and PH-collapse logic are misstated; T gates and
  Toffolis are conflated and Clifford gates called free; planar-Ising/FPRAS regimes are conflated; a QMA_EXP result is
  called exact without its succinct-size promise; dequantisation failure is treated as validation; only three
  simulable families are claimed to exist; PEPS operations and area laws are wrongly declared generically efficient;
  and noise is unsupportedly identified as the reason Sycamore circuits admitted tensor-network attack.
- **Recurring slight concerns:** Promise problems are described as ordinary decisions; oracle type/access is omitted;
  output and approximation metrics vanish; current best exponents and 2019–2025 experimental/resource rankings lack
  primary-source audits; classical and quantum resource units are compared directly; worst-case hardness is generalized
  to physical instances; British/American technical spelling varies; historical priority claims compress multiple
  papers; and “canonical/strongest/only/right” language converts perspective into fact.
- **Improvement priority:** Add a containment diagram distinguishing unconditional, conjectured, and oracle-relative
  relations. For every speedup/advantage example, tabulate model, input oracle, output, error metric, quantum upper
  bound, classical lower bound or best-known baseline, and epistemic status. Replace the three-family simulability claim
  with a nonexhaustive map and sharply separate MPS from PEPS. Finally, re-source all contemporary experiment and RSA
  resource claims against matched fidelity/hardware assumptions.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Complexity theory rarely reads this quickly. The oracle worlds, MIP*=RE, dequantisation drama,
  simulation arms race, and possible-versus-affordable bridge sustain strong intellectual entertainment throughout.
- **Second-pass status:** Complete for this manuscript file; the opening, all fourteen numbered sections, each class/
  containment statement, oracle and speedup catalogue entry, lower-bound/application subsection, five sanity checks,
  and footer were read and reviewed separately.

### `book/part-08-noise-and-qec/18-noise-decoherence-and-errors.md` — second pass

#### Chapter title, status, navigation, and opening role

- **Title:** Clear and appropriately broad, though “noise” and “errors” overlap; the chapter’s distinction between
  physical mechanism, channel model, and operational error metric should be stated explicitly.
- **Workflow metadata:** “prereviewed,” “Phase: 4,” and “19 / 19” again need a publication policy or build-time removal.
- **Navigation:** Previous, contents, and next links are coherent.
- **Prerequisite claims:** Verify Chapters 10–11 actually introduce every channel, Kraus/POVM object, Lindblad
  dissipator, and calibration-matrix concept promised here with matching notation.
- **“Any open-system evolution”:** A completely positive trace-preserving map represents physical state evolution under
  standard initial-system/environment assumptions, but non-Markovian initial correlations can complicate the “any”
  wording. A practitioner chapter can simply say “the standard apparatus.”
- **Named-failure-mode objective:** This is an excellent bridge from formalism to diagnosis and hardware decisions.
- **Benchmark interpretation:** A returned `$T_1$`/`$T_2$` is fit- and protocol-dependent, time-varying, and not a device
  constant. The chapter later acknowledges this; preview it now.
- **Forced-to-correct rhetoric:** Error mitigation and QEC are not sequentially exclusive stages, and spending qubits
  on correction may be designed from the outset. “Before full fault tolerance” is less melodramatic.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Turning abstract channels into a device autopsy is a strong and motivating premise.

#### How-to-read guide

- **Road map:** The phenomenology/catalogue/characterization/modeling/mitigation layers are well organized.
- **Section mismatch:** The guide places “fidelity” in §§18.10–18.14, while the heading list puts fidelities/error budgets
  in §18.16. Align the map.
- **Repeated crosstalk:** Crosstalk appears as §18.7 and again in §18.13; distinguish mechanism from simultaneous/cycle
  benchmark explicitly in the guide.
- **“Current” parameters:** Hardware ranges are temporally unstable and platform/metric selective. Mark the source date
  and cite a maintained benchmark dataset or vendor/primary papers.
- **SPAM wording:** Gate characterization seldom cleanly “disentangles” SPAM without assumptions; RB is robust to
  certain SPAM offsets and GST estimates a self-consistent gate set with gauge freedom. Use cautious phrasing.
- **Mitigation list:** The promised frank limitations are welcome and should include sampling overhead, model bias,
  noise drift, circuit scaling, and inability to restore lost information generically.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The layered map makes nineteen sections feel manageable.

#### §18.1 — taxonomy premise

- **Useful axes:** Bath location and coupling operator are sensible physical axes, but the five subsequent families mix
  mechanisms (TLS), effects (leakage), control imperfections, and spatial correlations (crosstalk). It is not a clean
  partition.
- **Strong universality overclaim:** These five do not dominate published budgets of “every contemporary platform.”
  Photon loss, atom loss, spontaneous emission, motional heating, laser phase/intensity noise, magnetic noise, charge/
  flux noise, measurement backaction, collisions, and fabrication disorder can dominate.
- **Alternative taxonomy:** Tabulate source, coupling operator, temporal/spatial correlation, computational-subspace
  effect, platform, diagnostic, and mitigation. Allow one mechanism to occupy multiple rows.
- **“Bath lives”:** Control electronics and coherent calibration error are not naturally a bath; acknowledge that the
  opening metaphor is loose.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** A physical-mechanism catalogue is inviting, but the claim of universality is immediately
  fragile.

#### §18.1 — thermal noise

- **Formula:** For an isolated two-level system at thermal equilibrium,
  `$p_1=(1+e^{\hbar\omega/k_BT})^{-1}$` is correct. Real transmons have higher levels, and their partition function
  slightly changes populations.
- **Numerical check:** A 5 GHz transition has `$\hbar\omega/k_B=h f/k_B\approx240$` mK; at 15 mK the two-level
  population is about `$1.1\times10^{-7}$`, so the stated order is sound.
- **Effective-temperature range:** “Typically 50–100 mK” is platform, shielding, filtering, frequency, cooldown, and
  measurement dependent; cite dated distributions rather than a universal range.
- **Mechanism mixture:** Nonequilibrium quasiparticles and stray photons need not produce a true Gibbs population
  describable by one effective temperature. Label `$T_{\rm eff}$` an inferred shorthand.
- **Bidirectional transitions:** Correct and important. The finite-temperature generalized-amplitude-damping channel is
  the natural cross-reference, not merely a criticism of zero-temperature amplitude damping.
- **Over-attribution phrasing:** A zero-temperature model omits upward excitation; it does not “over-attribute” a fixed
  observed error budget to relaxation unless a particular fit is described.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The fridge-temperature versus inferred-temperature discrepancy is a compelling concrete hook.

#### §18.1 — control noise

- **Good examples:** Amplitude drift, oscillator phase noise, AWG timing, and finite-anharmonicity leakage cover useful
  control-stack layers.
- **Strong coherent/stochastic conflation:** Phase noise and timing jitter can vary shot to shot; amplitude drift may be
  quasistatic; pulse distortion can be systematic. A “control fidelity ceiling” is not categorically the same error on
  every shot.
- **Drift timescale:** Calibration drift can turn a fixed coherent error into a time-dependent ensemble error, but it
  does not necessarily randomize independently across shots.
- **Finite anharmonicity:** Off-resonant excitation/leakage is also shaped by pulse duration/DRAG and can be coherent
  within the larger Hilbert space.
- **Terminology:** Separate miscalibration, stochastic control noise, distortion/transfer function, and leakage.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The examples make the invisible classical control stack feel consequential.

#### §18.1 — crosstalk

- **Definition:** Operationally useful, but crosstalk also includes static ZZ coupling, frequency collisions,
  measurement crosstalk, spectator-dependent phases, and correlated errors not well described as “driving qubit `$j$`.”
- **Pathway labels:** Direct/mediated/classical is a helpful start; “dispersive isolation” is not a generic property of
  every control line/platform.
- **Platform scope:** Capacitive buses describe superconducting devices, while shared motional modes and laser spillover
  matter for ions/atoms.
- **Strong superlative:** Crosstalk is not demonstrably the single biggest reason isolation and simultaneous fidelities
  differ on every platform; calibration context, heating, drift, scheduling, and frequency crowding contribute.
- **Metric:** Simultaneous RB changes a decay parameter under assumptions; it does not uniquely diagnose crosstalk.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Spectator corruption is immediately intuitive and motivates the later benchmark.

#### §18.1 — leakage

- **Physical-level examples:** Transmon and ion manifolds effectively show why the qubit approximation can fail, though
  ion leakage may go to states not naturally ordered as “higher.”
- **Definition:** Leakage means leaving the chosen computational subspace; it need not be upward in energy.
- **Strong QEC overclaim:** Standard Pauli QEC is not designed for leakage, but syndrome circuits may detect indirect
  symptoms and fault-tolerant protocols can tolerate bounded leakage with reset/swap/LRU strategies. “Do nothing useful”
  is false.
- **Persistence:** A transmon `$|2\rangle$` can relax to `$|1\rangle$` and then `$|0\rangle$`; rates differ from the
  qubit `$T_1$`. Leakage may also return coherently or be actively reset.
- **Error propagation:** The most dangerous feature is that a leaked data/ancilla qubit can create correlated faults
  through subsequent gates and violate the qubit-level noise model; say this directly.
- **LRU cost:** Pumping/reset/swap units add time, error, measurement, and routing; they do not simply restore arbitrary
  leaked states without consequences.
- **Cross-reference:** Verify Chapter 19 actually covers LRUs rather than only Pauli correction.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Leakage is a genuinely unsettling failure mode and the circuit-level contamination creates
  strong stakes.

#### §18.1 — two-level systems

- **Physical picture:** Defects coupling resonantly to superconducting qubits are a well-established source of avoided
  crossings and fluctuating relaxation.
- **Scope:** Substrate/interface/junction TLS prevalence and dominance depend on fabrication and device design. A single
  nearby TLS can dominate locally but is not the universal explanation of T1 variation.
- **Strong dephasing claim:** Ensembles of fluctuators can generate `$1/f$` charge/flux/frequency noise, but declaring
  “the TLS bath” the canonical/main source across devices and platforms is too singular. Multiple microscopic mechanisms
  remain debated.
- **Stationarity:** Drifting TLS parameters are one source of nonstationarity; cosmic rays, quasiparticle bursts,
  calibration drift, temperature, and adsorbates also matter.
- **Timescale:** “Minutes to days” needs measured examples and can vary much more broadly.
- **Hybridization spelling:** The book uses British spelling here; maintain chosen style.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Microscopic defects wandering into resonance provide a vivid physical antagonist.

#### §18.1 — platform-specific remainder and “universal vocabulary”

- **Own examples undermine taxonomy:** Laser-intensity and vibration noise overlap the control-noise family, while
  cosmic-ray quasiparticles overlap thermal/nonequilibrium excitation. The five families are not distinct.
- **Cosmic-ray duration:** Millisecond correlated bursts are device- and event-dependent; cite measurements and include
  spatially correlated QEC implications.
- **Chapter 21:** Verify it covers all named platform-specific mechanisms.
- **Strong conclusion defect:** The vocabulary is useful mainly for superconducting hardware and is not universal
  across photonic loss, neutral-atom loss/rearrangement, ion motional heating, semiconductor spin nuclear noise, etc.
- **Improvement:** Rename the section “Representative mechanisms, with superconducting emphasis,” then add a
  platform-by-mechanism matrix.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The exotic cosmic-ray example widens the lens, even as it exposes the taxonomy’s limits.

#### §18.2 — section framing

- **Dominance claim:** `$T_1$` and `$T_2$` are core single-qubit coherence metrics but do not dominate effective budgets
  on all platforms/circuits; gate control, leakage, readout, crosstalk, loss, and correlated errors can dominate.
- **Protocol dependence:** There is no unique `$T_2$` without specifying Ramsey, echo, or dynamical-decoupling sequence;
  the chapter’s naming convention should be declared.
- **Cross-reference:** Verify §10.9’s definitions use `$T_2$`, `$T_2^*$`, and `$T_\varphi$` consistently.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Returning familiar symbols in an operational setting is reassuring.

#### §18.2 — T1 protocol

- **Finite-temperature fit:** The more complete model is
  `$P_1(t)=p_1^{\rm eq}+[P_1(0)-p_1^{\rm eq}]e^{-t/T_1}$`, not the displayed product “up to” an asymptote.
- **SPAM:** Preparation and readout error change offset/amplitude; fit parameters must include them or use calibration.
- **Single exponential:** Correctly warns about nonexponential structure; multi-exponential decay may also arise from
  leakage, quasiparticles, or state-dependent readout.
- **Drift/statistics:** Interleaved repetitions and confidence intervals matter because `$T_1$` can drift during the
  sweep.
- **Beating:** Coherent exchange with a TLS can produce oscillations rather than a simple “decay” envelope.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The experiment is easy to visualize and the imperfect-fit warning adds realism.

#### §18.2 — T2 Hahn-echo protocol

- **Naming convention:** Many sources write `$T_{2,\rm echo}$` or `$T_{2E}$`; reserve `$T_2$` for homogeneous transverse
  relaxation only under a Markov model. State this choice.
- **Variable inconsistency:** The sequence uses total time `$\tau$`, then the envelope is written `$e^{-t/T_2}$`. Use
  one variable.
- **Measurement detail:** A final `$\pi/2$` analysis pulse with scanned phase is normally required to measure coherence/
  fringes in the X basis; “measure in X” abstracts this but should connect to hardware.
- **Echo action:** A Hahn echo refocuses quasistatic/slow detuning, not only a perfectly static offset. It also filters
  control noise according to its filter function and the π pulse has errors.
- **Envelope:** Exponential versus stretched/Gaussian form depends on spectral density and pulse errors; extracting one
  `$T_2$` may be convention-dependent.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The spin-echo reversal is elegant and experimentally concrete.

#### §18.2 — T2-star Ramsey protocol

- **Protocol distinction:** Ramsey free-induction decay uses two `$\pi/2$` pulses and often a deliberate detuning to
  fit oscillating fringes, not literally just the echo sequence with the π pulse deleted.
- **Expected ordering:** `$T_2^*\leq T_{2,\rm echo}$` is common under dominant low-frequency noise but not a universal
  theorem once pulse error, unusual spectra, or protocol definitions enter.
- **“All dephasing”:** Ramsey sees relaxation plus phase noise filtered by its sequence; it does not observe every
  frequency equally.
- **Order of magnitude:** Needs platform-specific sourced examples.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The no-echo contrast is intuitive.

#### §18.2 — rate decomposition

- **Domain of validity:** `$1/T_2=1/(2T_1)+1/T_\varphi$` holds for simple Markovian exponential relaxation plus
  independent pure dephasing. It need not describe echo-derived/stretched-exponential coherence under colored noise.
- **Bound:** `$T_2\leq2T_1$` follows in that model. Experimental fitted values can apparently violate it through drift,
  fit error, different measurement windows, or non-Markovian/protocol effects without violating physics.
- **“Same chemistry”:** Devices are hardware, not chemistry unless comparing materials/processes; the phrase is odd.
- **Causal inference overclaim:** `$T_2$` improving with unchanged `$T_1$` suggests reduced dephasing under controlled
  conditions, but filtering, pulse errors, drift, and protocol changes can also shift the fitted value.
- **Lockstep:** Simultaneous improvement does not prove relaxation limitation; correlated fabrication/control changes
  can affect both.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The additive-rate equation is useful, provided its Markovian boundary is prominent.

#### §18.2 — quasistatic dephasing and echo interpretation

- **Good intuition:** Ensemble averaging can erase observed coherence even when each shot undergoes unitary phase
  evolution; this is a crucial distinction between inhomogeneous broadening and irreversible decoherence.
- **“Fully coherent”:** Within a shot other relaxation/dephasing can still occur. Say the quasistatic contribution alone
  is coherent.
- **Echo limitation:** It refocuses offsets approximately constant over the sequence, while finite pulse width/error and
  noise near the echo filter passband remain.
- **“Truly dynamical”:** What remains includes faster noise plus pulse/control error and relaxation; it is not a pure
  measure of dynamical dephasing.
- **Strong ratio defect:** `$T_2/T_2^*$` is not a direct quantitative measure of quasistatic fraction for arbitrary
  nonexponential spectra/fit conventions. It is a useful qualitative indicator.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The difference between per-shot coherence and ensemble decay is conceptually rewarding.

#### §18.2 — mid-2025 platform numbers

- **Temporal audit:** Every range is time-sensitive and the review date is later than the stated snapshot. Verify with
  primary benchmark papers and state device/sample, median versus record, sequence, gate operating point, and date.
- **Best-in-class selection:** Comparing record transmons with generic ion/atom reports risks cherry-picking. Use a table
  with representative and record values plus gate times/fidelities.
- **Ion T1:** Hyperfine “T1” can be limited by state lifetime, magnetic/laser pumping, collisions, or experimental hold
  time; `>10^4` seconds and “essentially infinite” require a source and operational definition.
- **T2 under DD:** Dynamical-decoupling coherence cannot be compared directly with transmon echo `$T_2$` unless sequences
  and pulse counts are matched.
- **Neutral atoms:** Species, clock states, trap on/off, motion, magic trapping, and DD determine second-scale values.
- **Tradeoff:** Longer coherence/slower gates is broadly useful but compare quality-factor-like ratios and two-qubit
  fidelities; gate speed is not the only cost.
- **“Every quarter”:** Rhetorical and unsourced; replace with “changes rapidly.”
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Cross-platform scale differences are fascinating and practically valuable if normalized.

#### §18.3 — dephasing overview

- **“Three timescales”:** `$T_1,T_2,T_2^*$` are not three independent pure-dephasing timescales; `$T_2$` combines
  relaxation and phase decoherence, while `$T_2^*$` is protocol/inhomogeneous broadening.
- **Frequency-band insight:** Correctly emphasizes that pulse sequences sample different parts of the spectrum.
- **Lindblad coefficient:** With `$L_\varphi=\sqrt{\gamma_\varphi/2}Z$`, the dissipator decays off-diagonal entries at
  rate `$\gamma_\varphi$`; verify this convention against Chapter 10.
- **White-noise limit:** A constant-rate Lindblad dephasing semigroup corresponds to delta-correlated/white idealization,
  but Markovian approximations can arise from finite short-correlation baths.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The spectrum-first framing is exactly the right next layer.

#### §18.3 — stochastic phase and filter-function equation

- **Gaussian assumption omitted:** The exponential of a quadratic spectral integral follows for zero-mean Gaussian
  classical noise (or second-cumulant approximation). A generic stochastic process requires higher cumulants.
- **PSD convention:** Factors of `$2\pi$`, one-sided versus two-sided PSD, and whether the switching-function Fourier
  transform includes `$\omega^{-2}$` must be defined. As written the units of `$S F\,d\omega$` are unverifiable.
- **Mean:** A nonzero mean frequency offset produces a deterministic phase factor and can be moved into the rotating
  frame; zero-mean is a chosen separation.
- **Quantum noise:** A classical stochastic splitting does not capture all quantum bath effects or asymmetric spectra;
  say this subsection treats semiclassical longitudinal noise.
- **Sign:** Depending on definition, coherence is `$\langle e^{-i\phi}\rangle$`; for symmetric zero-mean Gaussian noise
  the sign is immaterial, but conventions should be consistent.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The filter-function integral powerfully connects pulse design to noise spectroscopy.

#### §18.3 — free-evolution and Hahn filters

- **Free filter:** `$t^2\sinc^2(\omega t/2)$` is consistent with the squared transform of a constant switching function,
  given one common PSD/filter convention.
- **“Sharply peaked”:** Its main lobe narrows around zero as `$1/t$`, but side lobes remain; DC dominance also depends
  on the noise spectrum.
- **Echo formula:** `$\sin^4(\omega t/4)/\omega^2$` is correct up to convention-dependent factors, but the preceding `$F$`
  definition must make the dimensions consistent.
- **Not a high-pass corner:** Dynamical-decoupling filter functions have passbands/peaks and stopbands, not simply an
  ever-rising high-pass cutoff. Periodic sequences shift dominant sensitivity to harmonics near the pulse rate.
- **“Anything slower”:** Suppression is continuous, finite, and pulse-error-limited; echo does not perfectly remove all
  frequencies below `$1/t$`.
- **Named sequences:** CPMG, XY-4/8, and Uhrig differ in pulse axes, timing, robustness, and target spectra; grouping them
  as one bandwidth knob hides design tradeoffs.
- **Figure pending:** The referenced SVG must receive its own one-file visual/content/accessibility pass; its alt text is
  informative but “moves the passband” should match the corrected filter language.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The spectral-control picture is highly visual and naturally engaging.

#### §18.3 — 1/f noise

- **Spectrum notation:** Use `$S\propto1/|\omega|^\alpha$` for a two-sided PSD; `$1/\omega^\alpha$` is problematic at
  negative frequency for noninteger `$\alpha$`.
- **Microscopic origin:** Broad distributions of fluctuator switching rates can produce 1/f spectra, but TLS are not a
  uniquely established universal origin for all charge/flux/frequency noise.
- **Cutoffs essential:** True 1/f behavior cannot extend to zero/infinite frequency; infrared and ultraviolet cutoffs
  remove the formal divergence and depend on acquisition time/device physics.
- **“No timescale”:** It has no single characteristic timescale over its scaling band, not literally none.
- **Gaussian-decay overclaim:** Ramsey decay under Gaussian quasistatic 1/f noise can be approximately Gaussian with
  logarithmic corrections. Non-Gaussian fluctuators/cutoffs/coupling sweet spots yield other forms.
- **DD gain:** Additional pulses reshape the filter but do not buy a universal factor of bandwidth suppression; pulse
  error and high-frequency noise create an optimum.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** A scale-free bath is conceptually intriguing and explains why measured coherence depends on
  observation protocol.

#### §18.3 — Markovian versus non-Markovian paragraph

- **Classical definition too strong:** Markov processes need conditional future independence given the present, not
  unconditional statistical independence at all separated times. Finite bath correlation time supports an
  approximation, not the literal statement given.
- **Quantum definitions:** CP-divisibility, semigroup dynamics, information backflow, and process-tensor memory are
  inequivalent notions of quantum Markovianity. Choose one operational definition.
- **Lindblad nuance:** Time-homogeneous GKSL generators yield semigroups; time-local Lindblad-form equations with
  time-dependent nonnegative rates can be CP-divisible and nonexponential.
- **Strong characterization defect:** Markovian decoherence is not always purely single-exponential or completely
  described by one constant rate; multilevel/multiple Lindblad operators yield several modes.
- **Simulation:** Fresh stochastic samples represent some classical Markov noise, not arbitrary quantum Markov channels;
  Kraus trajectories may be sampled with state-dependent jumps.
- **1/f:** Long correlations make 1/f noise a standard non-Markovian/quasistatic example, but the formal label depends on
  system timescale and definition.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Memory across gates is intuitive, though the formal simplification is too aggressive.

#### §18.3 — information backflow

- **One witness, not universal signature:** Revival of trace distance between an optimized pair of states is the
  Breuer–Laine–Piilo information-backflow criterion. Some non-Markovian dynamics under other definitions show no such
  revival, so “the practical signature” and “regularly” overclaim.
- **Tomographic burden:** Detecting backflow requires state pairs, time-resolved tomography, SPAM control, and
  statistical significance; it is not a simple routine diagnostic.
- **Physical revivals:** Oscillations can arise from coherent system modes, calibration drift, or finite environments;
  model selection remains necessary.
- **Markov statement:** CP-divisible dynamics contract trace distance, but positive-divisible/noninvertible subtleties
  deserve care if making a theorem-level assertion.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Lost distinguishability returning is a vivid way to make environmental memory tangible.

#### §18.3 — circuit accumulation example

- **Arithmetic:** `$1-0.999^{1000}\approx0.6323$`, so 63% follows if “fidelity” composes as independent survival
  probabilities.
- **Model defect:** Average gate fidelity does not generally multiply into end-to-end success this way; depolarizing
  parameters, observable, coherent error, dimension, and circuit structure determine composition.
- **Correlated-noise direction:** Slow coherent drift can accumulate quadratically/worst-case faster, but can also
  cancel, refocus, or produce circuit-dependent bias. It is false that correlated 1/f noise necessarily accumulates
  faster at the same reported fidelity.
- **Benchmark mismatch:** A fidelity averaged over random sequences/time may hide drift, but long-circuit prediction
  requires process tensors, cycle errors, context dependence, or repeated calibration—not a single 0.999.
- **Useful lesson:** The benchmarking-to-application gap is important; replace the numeric survival heuristic with a
  simulated coherent-overrotation versus stochastic-Pauli example.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The 63% number is memorable, but its simplistic multiplication risks becoming the wrong rule
  readers retain.

#### §18.4 — decoherence-limited error opening

- **Scaling:** Infidelity is linear in gate duration over coherence time at short times, but coefficients depend on
  amplitude damping/dephasing and average-versus-worst-case metric. `$\epsilon\sim t_{\rm gate}/T$` is an order-of-
  magnitude heuristic only.
- **Transmon arithmetic:** 20–50 ns over 100 µs is roughly `$2\times10^{-4}$` to `$5\times10^{-4}$`, so “around
  `$10^{-4}$`” is optimistic unless metric coefficients or longer T are included.
- **T1/T2 combination:** Use an average-gate-infidelity formula incorporating both relaxation and pure dephasing rather
  than a single unspecified `$T$`.
- **Ion range:** 1–100 µs spans one- and two-qubit gates and technologies; comparing “the same gate” is inaccurate.
- **Strong unit error:** “achieve `$10^{-4}$` to `$10^{-5}$` per-gate fidelity” must mean error/infidelity. Fidelity
  would be 0.9999–0.99999.
- **Causal conclusion:** If measured error exceeds the coherence-limit estimate, control, motion/heating, crosstalk,
  SPAM contamination, or model mismatch may dominate; it does not identify control noise uniquely.
- **Current numbers:** All platform values need dated sources and matched gate types/protocols.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Converting coherence times into a gate-error floor is exactly the calculation practitioners
  need, once units and coefficients are repaired.

#### §18.4 — control-limited errors

- **Rotation example:** A multiplicative 1% amplitude error gives the stated `$R_X(1.01\pi)$` only for a linear
  pulse-area/rotation calibration and negligible distortion/detuning.
- **Metric opportunity:** The coherent angle error is `$\delta=\pi/100$`, while average infidelity is quadratic in
  `$\delta$` (about `$\delta^2/6$` for a single-qubit unitary error under one convention) and worst-case coherent error
  scales linearly. Computing both would perfectly motivate §18.10.
- **Systematic qualifier:** Amplitude/phase/shape errors may drift or fluctuate; only stable miscalibration is the same
  coherent overrotation every application.
- **Axis errors:** Phase error changes rotation axis, detuning adds a Z component, and waveform distortion is not always
  representable as a simple angle rescaling.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The extra-rotation picture is crisp and naturally sets up coherent accumulation.

#### §18.4 — leakage/crosstalk pointer

- **Too thin:** A one-line pointer is acceptable after §18.1, but “quantifiable as a separate component” promises an
  additive error-budget decomposition that context-dependent leakage/crosstalk may not permit.
- **Section roles:** §18.13 appears to benchmark simultaneous/cycle behavior rather than uniquely separate causal
  components; make that limitation explicit.
- **Entertainment: 2/5.** Pure navigation.

#### §18.4 — 2023–2025 single-qubit fidelity ranges

- **Primary-source audit:** “Best-in-class,” platform ranges, years, qubit count, gate duration, simultaneous versus
  isolated operation, and uncertainty must be sourced to comparable primary experiments.
- **Metric mismatch:** “Single-qubit Clifford gates” may mean Clifford RB error per Clifford, error per primitive gate,
  or interleaved RB for a target gate. These are not interchangeable.
- **Selection bias:** Record fidelities on selected qubits do not describe device-wide medians/yields needed for
  algorithms or QEC.
- **Neutral-atom spread:** Atom loss, rearrangement, local/global addressing, and which logical clock/Raman gate strongly
  affect the number.
- **Temporal language:** A fixed 2023–2025 snapshot should not say “where the field stands today.”
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Cross-platform numbers are compelling, but only if the comparison table is disciplined.

#### §18.4 — two-qubit fidelity ranges

- **Gate heterogeneity:** CZ, CNOT, Mølmer–Sørensen, and Rydberg CZ have different durations, connectivity, calibration,
  leakage/loss conventions, and reported metrics. A single ranked list can mislead.
- **“Latest/most current”:** These are temporally unstable, undefined populations; use dated record and representative
  medians.
- **Evered citation:** Verify whether 99.5% is raw, SPAM-corrected, randomized-benchmarking-derived, postselected against
  atom loss, and on how many atom pairs.
- **“Successors exceeded”:** Name the papers and exact comparable number; otherwise this is unverifiable hearsay.
- **QEC relevance:** Simultaneous full-cycle error, leakage, correlated faults, measurement/reset, and yield matter more
  than isolated two-qubit average fidelity.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The platform race is intrinsically interesting and exceptionally easy to misreport.

#### §18.4 — measurement and idle ranges

- **Measurement:** Assignment fidelity needs definition (average of conditional correct probabilities), integration
  time, QND/repeatability, leakage/loss handling, and simultaneous-readout crosstalk.
- **Idle-unit error:** “`$10^{-4}$ per microsecond” is inconsistent with `$T\sim100\,\mu$s`; the raw short-time ratio is
  roughly 1% per microsecond, with average-infidelity coefficients still around several `$10^{-3}$`, not `$10^{-4}$`.
  This is a strong numerical defect unless the statement refers to much longer coherence or a special decoupled idle.
- **Platform contradiction:** On long-coherence ions/atoms idle errors may be tiny per microsecond, while on transmons
  the stated universal range is untenable.
- **Idle context:** DD pulses, spectator couplings, frequency parking, thermal excitation, and scheduling affect idle
  error beyond `$T_1/T_2$`.
- **Entertainment: 3/5.** Useful categories, but the idle figure materially distorts the budget.

#### §18.4 — claimed two-qubit bottleneck and code scaling

- **Universal bottleneck defect:** Measurement/reset, leakage, idle/dephasing, crosstalk, feed-forward, loss, and factory
  operations can bottleneck fault tolerance depending on platform and code.
- **“Decade of fidelity”:** Fidelity is near one; the intended phrase is likely a tenfold reduction in two-qubit
  infidelity/error rate.
- **No direct translation:** Surface-code distance depends nonlinearly/logarithmically on target logical error and on
  physical error relative to threshold; a tenfold physical-error reduction does not universally map directly to a
  fixed patch-size reduction.
- **Noise model:** Biased/correlated/leakage errors with the same average infidelity can yield very different thresholds
  and patches.
- **Cross-reference:** §19.12 should supply the actual logical-error fit and assumptions before this claim.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The QEC consequence raises the stakes, but “directly” conceals the most important modeling.

#### §18.4 — infidelity versus error-per-gate

- **Good warning:** Average benchmarking numbers are not worst-case channel guarantees.
- **RB conversion:** Standard Clifford RB estimates a decay parameter under assumptions; average error per Clifford is
  related by a dimension-dependent factor and extracting primitive-gate error requires further modeling. It does not
  simply “return average gate error approximating the depolarising parameter.”
- **Noise dependence:** Gate-dependent, non-Markovian, leakage, and time-dependent noise complicate the fitted decay and
  interpretation.
- **Worst-case example:** A 0.1% average infidelity can permit diamond distance on the order of its square root for
  coherent errors, so a percent-scale worst case is plausible; derive/bound it rather than asserting one chosen number.
- **Diamond metric:** Diamond distance is operationally natural for worst-case channel distinguishability including an
  ancilla, but “proper” is value-laden; unitarity, leakage, Pauli rates, and application-specific metrics also matter.
- **Measurement difficulty:** Direct diamond estimation is generally tomography-hard and GST estimates retain gauge/
  model issues; the lack of standardized reporting is real but needs a standards source.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Revealing what a string of nines can hide is excellent practitioner education.

#### §18.5 — readout framing

- **Strong oversimplification:** Readout error is not merely noise on a returned classical bit. The quantum measurement
  instrument includes assignment, backaction, state transition, leakage/loss, spectator disturbance, and post-
  measurement state.
- **End-of-circuit scope:** For destructive terminal measurement, a classical confusion matrix may suffice for some
  tasks; mid-circuit/QEC requires the full instrument/QND properties.
- **Cross-reference:** Verify §11.7 distinguishes assignment correction from physical measurement mitigation and
  correlated multi-qubit readout.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The classical-bit framing is accessible but too narrow for the mid-circuit discussion.

#### §18.5 — four conditional probabilities

- **Matrix language:** These are the four entries of a binary assignment matrix, not “corners” in a standard technical
  term; the metaphor is harmless.
- **Complementarity:** `$P(1|0)=1-P(0|0)$` and `$P(0|1)=1-P(1|1)$` only if every shot receives exactly one binary
  assignment and loss/leakage/erasure outcomes are folded into it.
- **Preparation contamination:** Calibration estimates combine preparation and measurement error unless heralding or a
  joint model separates them.
- **Current percentages:** “Usually 99%+” and 1–3% asymmetry require dated device distributions and simultaneous versus
  isolated measurement conditions.
- **Physical mechanisms:** T1 during integration is important for superconducting excited-state assignment, but
  overlapping IQ clouds, thermal excitation, measurement-induced transitions, leakage, and classifier bias also
  contribute.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The 2×2 table is intuitive and should be displayed visually.

#### §18.5 — asymmetry and integration tradeoff

- **Platform qualifier:** The text appropriately scopes the physical statement to superconducting hardware, though it
  still is not universal for every device/operating point.
- **Signal integration:** Longer integration can improve separation only until resonator dynamics, nonstationarity,
  state transitions, amplifier noise, and matched-filter choice set an optimum.
- **T1 probability:** For a qubit initially excited throughout a 1 µs interval with 100 µs T1,
  `$1-e^{-0.01}\approx0.995\%$`; the arithmetic is sound as a raw event probability.
- **Assignment mapping:** Not every decay event causes misclassification; timing within the integration and classifier
  weighting matter. Conversely other errors occur without decay.
- **Q-factor claim:** Reaching 0.1% is not simply a hardware Q-factor problem. Faster resonator depletion, Purcell
  filtering, parametric amplification, optimal filters/classifiers, latching/cycling schemes, and better T1 all matter;
  increasing resonator Q can slow response.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** The competing benefits of waiting longer make a memorable design tradeoff.

#### §18.5 — mid-circuit measurement

- **Protocol dependence:** A mid-circuit measurement need not reset the measured qubit; QND measurement ideally leaves
  it in the measured eigenstate, and explicit reset is added only when required.
- **Spectators:** Measurement-induced dephasing, resonator photons, frequency collisions, optical scattering, and
  classical crosstalk need platform-specific treatment.
- **Latency budget:** Feed-forward must beat the relevant logical schedule/coherence/error-correction cycle, not always
  the bare physical `$T_2$`.
- **Quality metrics:** Include assignment fidelity, QNDness/repeatability, leakage, reset fidelity/time, spectator error,
  erasure flagging, and conditional-operation latency.
- **“Leading frontier”:** Time-sensitive and broad; cite platform roadmaps/experiments or state it as a challenge.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The measurement becoming an active control operation effectively raises the engineering stakes.

#### §18.6 — state-preparation framing

- **Subdominance overclaim:** State preparation can dominate algorithms needing pure ancillas, repeated mid-circuit
  reset, postselection, photonic sources, atom loading, or error-detection. Scope the claim to terminal-circuit
  transmon initialization.
- **Mechanisms incomplete:** Pulse mispreparation, leakage, optical pumping error, atom/ion loss, imperfect cooling, and
  correlated initialization also matter.
- **Definition:** Preparation infidelity should be stated relative to the intended density operator and include
  correlations across qubits.
- **Entertainment: 3/5.** Useful but platform-narrow.

#### §18.6 — residual population

- **Current range:** `$10^{-3}$–$10^{-2}$` residual excitation is plausible for some transmons but needs dates, qubit
  frequencies, filtering, measurement technique, and whether active/heralded initialization was used.
- **Notation defect:** Calling the observed nonequilibrium population `$p_1^{\rm eq}$` contradicts §18.1, which explains
  that it exceeds the fridge-equilibrium prediction. Use `$p_1^{\rm ss}$` or effective-thermal population.
- **Two-level approximation:** Real initialization includes `$|2\rangle$` and higher-state population; the displayed
  mixture may be incomplete.
- **Correlations:** Quasiparticle/cosmic events can correlate initial excitations across qubits; a product of single-
  qubit thermal mixtures is not assured.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The many-orders mismatch from naive fridge equilibrium remains a strong hardware lesson.

#### §18.6 — active reset

- **Protocol:** Measure-and-conditional-X is clear, but latency, classifier confidence, QNDness, and post-measurement
  transitions determine performance.
- **T1 direction error:** A T1 event after a correct X-to-zero helps rather than harms; decay between measurement and a
  conditional X can cause the pulse to re-excite the qubit. Thermal upward events and readout misassignment also matter.
- **Error addition:** Residual reset error is not simply measurement error “plus” T1; conditional branches and repeated
  rounds require a transition model.
- **Numbers:** `$10^{-3}$–`$10^{-4}$` after 2–3 rounds and percent after one require primary data and matched duration;
  repeated rounds can saturate or add heating/leakage.
- **“Heralded”:** A conditional reset is not automatically heralded unless an additional verification measurement
  accepts/rejects the result.
- **Unconditional reset:** Resonator-assisted/dissipative reset protocols vary and can induce spectator error; comparable
  numbers need sources.
- **Cross-reference:** Verify §21.9 contains reset rather than a platform-specific section mismatch.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Feedback reset is an intuitive miniature control loop, and the branching errors merit a
  diagram.

#### §18.6 — SPAM inseparability, RB, and GST

- **Identifiability:** A single prepare–gate–measure probability indeed cannot uniquely assign error among preparation,
  transformation, and measurement without assumptions.
- **RB robustness:** Constant SPAM changes RB fit amplitude/offset rather than decay under the standard model; sequence-
  dependent SPAM, drift, leakage, and gate dependence can still bias fits. “SPAM-robust” needs this scope.
- **GST:** Gate-set tomography jointly estimates preparation, measurement, and gates self-consistently, but gauge freedom
  prevents a unique separation without a gauge choice and Markov/model assumptions.
- **Strong subtraction defect:** In many analyses one cannot simply subtract a combined SPAM contribution from gate
  metrics. RB fits nuisance parameters; GST models all components; neither licenses generic subtraction.
- **Acronym:** Expanding SPAM is useful, but avoid implying state preparation and measurement are physically one error
  class merely because they are statistically confounded.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The identifiability problem is subtle and important, and the competing benchmark strategies
  make it concrete.

#### §18.7 — crosstalk definition

- **Definition breadth:** Cross-qubit effects from control/measurement are crosstalk; effects on the same qubit’s
  leakage levels are control leakage/spectral leakage, not naturally “cross” talk.
- **Static coupling:** An always-on interaction can produce context dependence even without an active operation; include
  it explicitly rather than tying definition only to operations.
- **Classical/quantum split:** The labels can blur because classical drive leakage produces coherent quantum evolution
  and quantum coupling is controlled by classical pulses. A source-versus-mediated-interaction taxonomy is clearer.
- **Entertainment: 3/5.** Functional but repeats §18.1.

#### §18.7 — classical crosstalk

- **“Same wire”:** Separate control lines can couple through package modes, electronics, microwave launchers, or optical
  spillover; same-wire fanout is only one architecture.
- **Coupling language:** Capacitive/inductive coupling describes hardware paths, while a control field’s leak may be
  electromagnetic transfer rather than direct circuit coupling.
- **Mitigation overclaim:** Linear precompensation works for stable, measurable transfer matrices but not arbitrary
  nonlinear, frequency-dependent, state-dependent, drifting, or saturating crosstalk.
- **Calibration cost:** Measuring a full crosstalk matrix scales poorly and simultaneous pulses create pair/higher-order
  context not captured independently.
- **“Never exactly zero”:** Physically plausible but unnecessary absolute; below measurable/relevant tolerance is the
  engineering objective.
- **Entertainment: 3/5.** Clear, though overly schematic.

#### §18.7 — quantum/ZZ crosstalk

- **Mechanism:** Static residual ZZ coupling gives spectator-state-dependent conditional phases and is an important
  superconducting error.
- **Normalization:** Depending on Hamiltonian convention the term may be `$\zeta Z_iZ_j/2$` or `/4`; define `$\zeta$`
  before translating to phase.
- **“Entire time”:** Correct for a constant interaction in the lab/rotating frame, but echo sequences and commuting
  phases can cancel/track it.
- **Tunable couplers:** Residual off-state coupling, flux noise, calibration, parasitic direct coupling, and coupler
  leakage prevent generic “nearly zero” guarantees.
- **ZZ-free points:** Frequency parking may introduce collisions, dephasing, or reduced gateability; note tradeoffs.
- **Platform scope:** Shared motional modes/optical fields generate different quantum crosstalk on ions/atoms.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The invisible spectator-dependent phase is a strong concrete example.

#### §18.7 — crosstalk and QEC

- **Strong assumption defect:** Surface-code fault tolerance does not require all operations/errors to be independent;
  threshold theorems tolerate sufficiently local, bounded correlated noise under specified models. Many simple decoders
  assume independent Pauli priors, which is a different claim.
- **Decoder claim:** A decoder can incorporate pairwise/correlated edge models or learned/contextual noise; “cannot
  interpret” is false for standard extensions and too broad even for matching decoders.
- **Numeric transformation:** A 0.5% physical rate becoming “effectively 1.5%” under nearest-neighbor ZZ has no defined
  metric, coupling strength, schedule, decoder, or source. Remove or provide a full cited simulation.
- **Threshold:** There is no single surface-code threshold; it depends on circuit/noise/decoder/measurement/leakage and
  may be far below/above 1% under different assumptions.
- **Diagnostic:** Simultaneous RB detects context-dependent average decay but does not uniquely quantify QEC-cycle
  correlated faults; cycle benchmarking/correlation spectroscopy may be more relevant.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** Crosstalk threatening exponential logical suppression is compelling, but unsupported numbers
  manufacture drama.

#### §18.8 — leakage mechanisms

- **Definition repetition:** Repeats §18.1 nearly verbatim; consolidate the overview there and reserve this section for
  quantitative models/diagnostics.
- **Higher-level wording:** Leakage can go to lower/nonordered states, loss, or noncomputational manifolds; retain
  platform-general subspace language.
- **Transmon CZ:** Avoided-crossing CZ implementations can intentionally approach/hybridize `$|11\rangle$` and
  `$|20\rangle$`, but modern CZ families differ (tunable couplers, parametric, microwave-activated). Do not imply all
  transmon CZ gates use this path.
- **Ion analogy:** Off-resonant hyperfine leakage, motional excitation, and spontaneous emission are distinct; specify
  which counts as internal-state leakage.
- **Neutral atoms:** Residual Rydberg population may decay, be lost, or remain temporarily outside the computational
  manifold; atom loss often deserves an erasure category.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Platform comparisons are helpful despite repetition.

#### §18.8 — leakage danger

- **Syndrome behavior:** A leaked qubit may yield anomalous/random/repeated syndrome patterns, not simply “look like an
  ordinary error.” Its interaction-dependent propagation is the key distinction.
- **Lifetime claim:** `$|2\rangle$` lifetime can be shorter or related by oscillator matrix elements to computational
  T1; “often longer” needs evidence and may be wrong for transmons.
- **Persistence:** What matters is lifetime relative to QEC cycle and reset cadence, not bare `$T_1$` alone.
- **Empirical superlative:** “Single largest contributor” to time-correlated error across surface-code experiments is
  current, platform-specific, and likely unsupported universally. Cite named experiments.
- **Other correlations:** Cosmic rays, drift, residual couplings, measurement resonators, and decoder/model effects can
  dominate.
- **Entertainment: 5/5.** A fault persisting across rounds is an effective explanation of why leakage is worse than a
  one-cycle Pauli error.

#### §18.8 — gate-level mitigation

- **DRAG:** Derivative-removal-by-adiabatic-gate pulses suppress leakage/phase errors for weakly anharmonic systems, but
  improvement is device/calibration dependent; “order of magnitude over square pulses” needs a cited setup.
- **Baseline:** Modern gates rarely use raw square pulses, making that comparison pedagogically historical rather than a
  current performance claim.
- **Ion STIRAP:** Verify that STIRAP-like protocols are a standard leakage-mitigation analogue for the ion gates in
  scope; pulse shaping, polarization, detuning, and repumping may be more representative.
- **Tradeoff:** Leakage suppression can increase duration or phase/control error; optimize total error, not leakage alone.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** Pulse shape as subspace containment is intuitive.

#### §18.8 — circuit-level LRUs

- **“Pump back” danger:** Returning population from leakage does not generally restore the unknown logical state that
  existed before leakage; an LRU converts leakage into a located/unlocated computational error that QEC must correct.
- **Swap-and-reset:** A SWAP involving a leaked multilevel qubit is not an ideal qubit SWAP and can propagate leakage.
  Surface-code role-swapping/teleportation schemes need explicit leakage-aware gates.
- **Fresh ancilla cost:** Requires qubits, routing, reset, and fault-tolerant scheduling; ancilla may itself leak.
- **Period selection:** Too frequent LRUs add errors, too rare allow correlated propagation; this is an error-budget
  optimization.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The architectural response is useful, but “simplest” conceals a nontrivial leakage-aware
  operation.

#### §18.8 — decoder-level mitigation

- **Detection:** Syndrome streaks can indicate leakage but are not unique; persistent data errors, measurement faults,
  or crosstalk can look similar.
- **Decoder action:** A classical decoder cannot physically reset a qubit by itself; detection must trigger a controller/
  circuit operation and update the Pauli/frame/erasure information.
- **Information:** Direct leakage-sensitive readout or erasure flags can greatly improve decoding; include these
  alternatives.
- **Model:** Hidden-Markov, correlated matching, belief propagation, or learned decoders can model persistence; name one
  concrete approach.
- **False positives:** Resetting a nonleaked data qubit destroys state unless done through a fault-tolerant replacement/
  teleportation protocol.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** A temporal syndrome signature makes decoding feel like diagnosis rather than bookkeeping.

#### §18.8 — fault-tolerance theorem claim

- **Strong theorem overgeneralization:** Threshold theorems assume particular local noise models; some explicitly extend
  to leakage with leakage-reduction constructions. They do not share one premise “leakage suppressed below the
  threshold.”
- **Threshold dimension:** Leakage rate alone is insufficient; propagation strength, duration, locality, LRU error, and
  conversion to regular faults determine an effective threshold.
- **Precondition/consequence:** Fault-tolerant gadgets can themselves contain leakage, so mitigation is part of the
  construction rather than wholly prior to the theorem.
- **Chapter 19:** State the exact theorem/noise model to be taught there and align the wording.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** It correctly signals leakage cannot be ignored, but presents folklore as a universal theorem.

#### §18.9 — GAD Kraus operators

- **Algebra:** The four displayed operators form a trace-preserving generalized amplitude-damping channel under the
  stated convention, with downward branch weight `$p$` and upward branch weight `$1-p$`.
- **Parameter convention:** Textbooks sometimes define `$p$` as the ground-state equilibrium population and sometimes
  swap branch labels. Explicitly verify `$\sum K_i^\dagger K_i=I$` and the fixed point to lock the convention.
- **Finite-temperature T1:** `$\gamma=1-e^{-t/T_1}$` is valid when measured `$T_1^{-1}=\Gamma_\downarrow+\Gamma_\uparrow$`
  is the population-relaxation rate. It is not literally the zero-temperature downward rate reused unchanged.
- **Semigroup condition:** For a fixed thermal bath, upward/downward rates satisfy detailed balance; arbitrary
  time-dependent `$\gamma,p$` may not form a semigroup.
- **Higher levels:** The qubit GAD channel ignores transmon `$|2\rangle$` population and leakage, important at the
  precision this chapter discusses.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** The upward Kraus operator makes finite temperature operationally clear.

#### §18.9 — zero-temperature limit and fixed point

- **Limit:** At `$p=1$`, `$K_2,K_3$` vanish and ordinary downward amplitude damping remains; correct.
- **Fixed point:** The stated diagonal state with excited population `$1-p$` is correct for nonzero damping under this
  convention.
- **Equilibrium notation:** Use `$p_1^{\rm eq}$` only for a genuine/model-effective thermal fixed point; nonequilibrium
  quasiparticle populations may not obey detailed balance.
- **Readout asymmetry causal claim:** Downward transitions during integration contribute to worse excited-state
  assignment, but finite bath temperature is not the sole origin; even a zero-temperature bath gives downward T1
  asymmetry. Saying it has “the same origin” incorrectly makes nonzero T essential.
- **Idle statement:** Correct within the stationary GAD model; active drives, quasiparticle bursts, leakage, and reset
  make real idle fixed points more complex.
- **Entertainment: 4/5.** A non-ground fixed point is a useful correction to the default damping picture.

#### §18.9 — budget contribution and characterization

- **Unsupported percentage:** “1–10% of total error” has no defined device, operation, metric, or decomposition and
  cannot be universal across current cryogenic platforms.
- **“Dominates only when”:** Thermal excitation can dominate initialization/readout even when control/dephasing remain
  substantial; contribution depends on task and idling.
- **Temperature inference:** A single `$p_1$` and transition frequency yields an effective two-level temperature only if
  detailed-balance/thermal assumptions hold. Measuring upward/downward rates is a stronger diagnostic.
- **Standard-suite claim:** Plausible but should cite measurement protocols and distinguish direct population,
  heralding bias, and readout correction.
- **Spelling:** “Characterisation” remains British; ensure consistency.
- **Entertainment: 3/5.** Practical, but the unsupported percentage adds false precision.
